Preface BRAHAM LINCOLN learned to speak and write the language an intense repeated of Scott's Lessons. Can a similar miracle of achievement be effected through the use of any modern text? Given a determination, a devotion, and a persistence equal to Lincoln's, a present-day school boy or girl may do what the nineteenth-century rail-splitter did, and with similar materials. The facts of English form are in this book; the principles of effective writing are herein set forth-negatively through the faulty sentences of college students, positively through examples taken from the classic masters. 

Yet nobody was ever cured by the medicine he left in the bottle, or fed by the flour left in the bin; nor will anybody be improved by the page he leaves unread. The young student who wants to learn to write well must do his part. 

And the authors of this College Rhetoric believe that many a student will read, and ponder, and practice and the miracle will come to pass!

To meet the general requirements of English study, the scope of the text has been made wide. Page form, spelling, even handwriting, excluded though they were from the older books on writing, are given space. Rhetoric is the art of beautiful, or effective, language. Why, then, exclude any of the elements of beauty and effectiveness from the discussion? On the other hand, a perusal of these pages will show that many or most of the traditional terms of school "rhetorics" have been retained. The point of view is, on the whole, conservative. One purposes animates the

vi 

Preface entire volume and, it is hoped, fuses all parts into an organic whole: the purpose to fire our reader not only with. a sense of the glory of all language and of English speech but with a desire, in his own writing, to set down the best and keenest of his thoughts in the clearest expression. 

Most of the sentences offered for correction in this book have been taken verbatim from college themes. Some may be found to contain faulty construction of types other than the ones that are specifically under discussion. In any exercise, the student may correct every mistake he finds, of whatever sort.

Our thanks are due to members of the faculty of English in the University of Maryland who have rendered us helpful service in many ways.

H. C. H. S. E. H.

INTRODUCTION Counsel for the Writer 

Accept the burden. In written communication two parties are concerned: writer and reader. In the majority of instances the former is required to make the greater effort. He is attempting to teach, to stimulate, to convince. 

If he is earnest he will not spare himself, but will employ all the energy he has and every art he knows to make his meaning clear, his images suggestive, his conclusions inescapable. It is harder to write an essay, a story, or a book than to read one; and so it ought to be. The writer is making a bid for the reader's interest, his enthusiasm, or his vote. He will do everything in his power to make the reading of his work an experience of profit and delight. 

Conserve the reader's force. The best possible advice to a young writer is, put yourself in the place of your reader. 

Make plain his path to the center of your thought. He has a certain amount of energy to place at your disposal: do not waste it. With such force as your reader has at his command he must do the following things: 

1. Make out the characters of your handwriting, and conceive these as forming letters, syllables, and words. 

2. Grasp and hold the meaning and the emotional color or association of each word. 

3. Perceive the relations of the words and their organization into sentences and paragraphs. 

4. React to your story, your exposition, or your argument as a whole: weighing, valuing, opposing, consenting, condemning, approving. 1

College Rhetoric 

A certain amount of coal is fed into a furnace. The heat units released from the coal enable an engine to convert bars of metal into (let us say) carpet tacks. Now a certain number of these heat units will, because of friction, escape as radiant heat, and so be wasted; and the remaining units must do the work, produce the desired result. Hence the ideal engine is one which wastes least power in friction, and manufactures a maximum quantity of tacks. Now there is an analogy between the working engine and the activity of a reader's mind. All of the energy that he must expend in deciphering blurred handwriting, in searching for the probable identity of misspelled words and the probable intention of badly selected words, and in discovering relations concealed by bad structure and faulty punctuation and confused metaphors,—all this wasted energy must be subtracted from the total nervous force which he may devote to the appreciation and enjoyment of the writer's thought and fancy. Have mercy on your reader; help him; do not get in his way.

Respect the conventions. Every language is a system of symbols agreed upon, and to speak or write it correctly is to play the game according to the rules. These rules may be modified by common consent, but not arbitrarily discarded by a single player. One must not say down for up, except for accept, disinterested for indifferent. When a 

University of Maryland freshman wrote Know one new for No one knew, he defied the conventions and, for the moment, stopped the game. There is usually just one correct spelling of a given word, though there may be five possible incorrect ones. One reads rapidly and easily when words are correctly spelled, haltingly and with effort when spelling is inaccurate. A comma in the wrong place is as bad as a cinder in the eye; one must stop and remove the offender before one can get on with the task. Wrong case and tense forms, unattached modifiers, and obscure grammatical

Counsel for the Writer 

3 reference are as confusing as wrong signals in football. In writing, as in society, "no one liveth to himself"; for writing is communication, and communication can be achieved only through the operation of a medium of expression mastered and adhered to by all parties concerned. 

Be self-respecting. One can be dignified without solemnity or prosiness; one can be witty without turning cartwheels or getting down on all fours; one need not prance or cavort in order to gain attention. Energy of style is not smartness, strain, or posing. Insight is vastly more important than display; real beauty lies far beneath "fine writing" and the "flowers of rhetoric." The first and last principle of all great art is sincerity. To know or feel something deeply, with an intense desire to communicate the knowledge or feeling without loss, is the one thing that can almost promise success before the actual task of writing is begun. 

Be honorable. Young Student: Do your own writing.

Ask help of nobody. Plagiarism is the crime of a literary sneak. If you are past fifteen, life has already taught you as a separate person things that many others do not know. 

Your face, your eyes, your figure, your character are individual. Have faith in your own thought. Express your own ideas, without copying, without help, without consultation with persons, magazines, or books. If the result should prove to be crude, the crudeness will at least be yours, and you will have begun the task of learning how not to be crude. If, on the other hand, your instructor has asked you to look up library material before writing, read widely and assimilate what you find, but take nothing of phrase or even thought without giving ample credit, citing title, author, volume, and page. Your own honor and your own growth in intellectual and artistic power are of far greater importance than marks, prestige, or even a bachelor's degree.

CHAPTER I The Manuscript 

MANUSCRIPT is a copy of an author's own work in handwriting or typewriting: it is a record on paper of his thoughts, which he invites others to consider. Since the effect of the work depends largely upon the reading audience, it is essential that one make one's text readable by observing certain conventions of page form. 

While not absolute in all respects, the following rules are generally observed by careful writers. Their observance will simplify the reader's task and add to the attractiveness of the theme. 

Handwriting. It ought not to be necessary to say that a paper which is prepared for another must be legible; yet many students are careless and indifferent about their handwriting. A writer who desires a sympathetic consideration of his discussion should not annoy his reader by illegibility. 

One ought not to cultivate a freakish hand or make the letters radically different from the conventional forms. 

Individuality or originality in handwriting may well be sacrificed for the sake of legibility. Any writing that is difficult to read is poor. And any one can learn to write well if he has the will to do so. It is important to remember that when a reader's attention is focused on the handwriting, he is neglecting the argument of the text. 

Some of the more common faults in handwriting are analyzed here, with some suggestions as to how they may be corrected; try comparing each excerpt with a paragraph in your own hand:

The Manuscript 

5 

1. Do not cultivate a large hand. If you do so, you will waste paper and have a bulky manuscript. The letters in one line should never overlap those in the lines above and below. Make a special effort to reduce the size of l's, h's, k's, g's, b's, d's, and y's, and other letters will become smaller. 

2. Students who write in longhand should take especial pains to make the spacing of their letters, words, and sentences correspond to that used by the typist. Words should not be run together: the space conventionally allowed between words is about one-eighth of an inch. Nor should sentences be crowded together. Between the punctuation mark that ends one sentence and the capital letter that begins the next, double spacing (here a quarter of an inch) should signal to your reader that you have made a full stop. 

3. Write the lines parallel with one another. Do not allow your writing to become noticeably smaller at the right margin. Avoid running the line upward as you move. If you will see that the letters at the end of a word or of a line are brought down to the base line (imaginary or real), you will be able to correct your tendency to write diagonally toward the upper right-hand corner. 

4. Fill out the lines, and make your margin at the right as even as possible. 

5. Make your letter-slant uniform. Do not alternate between backhand and the Spencerian (forward) slant. 

6. Certain letters are easily confused. Take pains to make your vowels legible. Let your o's be closed, your i's dotted, and your t's crossed. Make m's, w's, n's, u's, and v's rounded at the loop, so that there may be no mistaking one for another. Your h's, d's, b's, p's, q's, y's, f's, k's, j's, and g's, should be carefully made.

Some types of handwriting are shown on pages 6-10.

11 black or blue-black ink. The size of the sheet of paper may vary with departments and with instructors, but as a general rule the regulation business-letter size or the conventional notebook paper is acceptable. Typewritten papers should not be submitted unless the instructor approves this form. When using the typewriter, the student should employ a black ribbon, double-space the lines (except in quotations), and make an especial effort to have his pageform evenly typed. The type should be carefully cleaned. 

Typographical errors must be regarded by the instructor as mistakes. 

The title. The title of the theme should appear at the top of the page about an inch and a half from the top margin and about one inch from the first line of the theme. 

It should be centered above the first paragraph of the introduction. The first word of the title, and every other word of the title except articles and prepositions,¹ should begin with a capital letter.

The margin. Whether the theme be typed or written longhand, the page should look as much like the printed page of a book as it is possible for the writer to make it. 

There should be even margins, an inch or an inch and a half at the top, bottom, and left edges of the paper. About a one-inch margin should be left at the right, though there will be some irregularity here, because words must be divided according to the principle of syllabication. The writer should take pains so to space his words and letters that the right margin will be as little jagged as possible. 

The margin at the left must be even. 

Indention. The first line of the theme and the first line of every paragraph should be indented one inch from the margin set by the other lines of the paragraph. Inserted quotations of considerable length or of set form should be 1 Some editors prefer to capitalize prepositions of five or more letters which appear in titles.

College Rhetoric indented an additional half inch (and single-spaced, with double spacing between stanzas or paragraphs of the quotation).

Contraction. One should not use contractions in formal 

English. They are therefore not permitted in expository writing unless the writer is quoting, either directly or indirectly. Little time is saved by contracting words, and confusion is often avoided by spelling out forms. It takes as much effort and time and ink to write He doesn't as to write He does not.

Consistency. Every writer should take pains to be consistent in his use and in his spelling of words. He should not write theatre in one place and theater in another unless he is making a point of the variation in the spelling of the word. One should not write About one hundred men and 

200 women came for the examination. Professor Jones should not be referred to loosely as Doctor Jones and as Mr. 

Jones in the same paper.

Figures. In dates, street addresses, long lists of numbers. and large sums of money, figures are often preferred to the written-out forms, which may become bulky and difficult to visualize. October 20, 1933, should be preferred to October twentieth, nineteen hundred and thirty-three. Even in formal invitations figures for house and street numbers are correct: 134 Guilford Street, not One hundred thirty-four,

Guilford Street. Large sums of money are better in figures than in expanded wording: The Merchants Association lost $1256.60, not The Merchants Association lost one thousand two hundred fifty-six dollars and sixty cents.

Underscoring. Italics are used in print to emphasize a word or phrase and to differentiate words referred to as words, foreign words, irregular forms and titles of books, plays, essays, and poems.2 The typist or the longhand 

2 Some authors prefer to use quotation marks for the names of plays, essays, poems, and short stories, and thus distinguish minor works from

The Manuscript 

13 writer should underscore to designate italicized forms. 

Certain symbols and abbreviations of foreign words, also, are sometimes put in italics: i.e., e.g., viz., vs.

Examples 

1. He was told that he must do it without delay. 

2. The word that may be a conjunction. 

3. The French phrase faux pas is often heard. 

4. accept, not except. 

5. I have read David Copperfield, Hamlet, and Evangeline since I saw you. 

6. A football game was being advertised-Chicago vs. Michigan. 

Abbreviations. Use few abbreviations, and only those which are generally accepted as preferable to forms written out: Mr., Mrs., Messrs. are perfectly correct even in the most formal writing, provided they are used with proper nouns, the omission of which makes them vulgarisms. 

VULGAR: The Mr. and the Mrs. are both invited. 

Abbreviations that can be regarded as syllables and pronounced as such should not be used in formal writing, especially if they are used alone; that is, without accompanying nouns.

BAD: My Prof. would not accept the Doc's. excuse. 

ALSO BAD: I had English Lit. the first period, Chem. Lab. 

the rest of the morning, and Gym. in the afternoon.

Names of states, towns, counties, rivers, and kingdoms should not be abbreviated in formal writing. 

BAD: We drove from Philly, Penn., to Los Ang. in five days. 

BAD: We crossed the Miss. River at St. Lo., Mo. major ones; i. e., names of books, which they italicize. Titles of chapters and other divisions of books also are usually put in quotation marks.

Abbreviations of many honorary titles are in good usage, though the modern tendency is to spell out personal titles, especially when these precede the names they identify. 

Thus, such words as Captain, Colonel, General, Professor, President, Governor, Doctor, and Reverend are preferably spelled out when they precede the nouns they describe. 

The following are correct: 

1. The Reverend Mr. Jones will speak to us. 

2. The Honorable Thomas Majors was elected by one vote. 

3. Captain John Smith was born in 1580. 

4. Governor Rolfe approved the lynching. 

3 The above list is by no means complete. It is often necessary for the author of a research paper to list his symbols and abbreviations, since hardly any text could include all the abbreviations that one could use in citing notes and references to authors and their works.

College Rhetoric 

Corrections. The final draft of a student's theme prepared out of class should not be marred with cancellation marks of any sort. All corrections should be made on the rough draft. Themes written in class may have insertions and cancellations. To insert a letter, a word, a phrase, or a clause, use the caret (^) and write the inserted elements above the line and centered over the caret. 

Quotations. Quotations that do not check the train of thought should be included as a part of the body of the theme. Several lines of a quoted text, and especially a paragraph or a page of such material, should be separated from the discussion by being paragraphed and single-spaced. 

The left margin of the quotation should be about one-half inch from the margin of the theme proper. 

If the quotation is indented and centered and singlespaced, quotation marks may be omitted altogetherespecially if the note or reference gives the text and the author of the quotation. It is, however, correct to use the quotation marks at the beginning of each paragraph of the quoted text and at the close of the last sentence of the entire quotation, although the mark is not entirely necessary in quoting poetry.

Footnotes. Footnotes are used to cite references and material which would seem to interrupt the body of the discussion if inserted in the text. Footnotes are essential in research papers; and the notations and references should be full and accurate, so that any one desiring to check the source of authority may do so with little effort. While there is no absolutely fixed form which writers observe, the following may be regarded as generally acceptable: 

1. The mark used to signify a note may be an asterisk (*); but since more than one note may occur on a page, it is wise to number notes, and use numbers in the text to refer to footnotes with corresponding numbers.

The Manuscript 

17 2. The arrangement of the parts of the references varies with different authors, though the following order is commonly accepted: (a) Name of author (surname first). 

(b) Title of book (underscored for italics.) Titles of poems and names of short stories, essays, and short reports may be enclosed in quotation marks. 

(c) Name of publisher. 

(d) Date of publication of book or magazine (month, day, year, and edition, if all are given). (e) Volume, chapter, and page. Refer, however, to the body of a poem or play not by page but by line, scene, act, or canto. For texts such as the 

Bible cite chapter and verse. 

Examples 

1. Bradley, A. C., Shakespearean Tragedy, Macmillan and 

Co., London, 1926, pp. 5-40. 

2. Ibid., p. 89. 

3. Histoire générale, vol. V., ch. I 

4. Bradley, op. cit., p. 79. 

5. Richard II is perhaps an exception, and I must confess that to me he is scarcely a tragic figure. 

6. Fisher, H. A. L., in Cambridge Modern History, IX, 

151, 159 ff. 

7. Cf. Hamlet's closing speech in Act I. 

8. Erasmus, Desiderius, The Praise of Folly, Hamilton, Adams and Co., London, 1887, p. 17. 

9. Scott, Sir Walter, "The Black Dwarf" in Waverly 

Novels, vol. III., Colliers, New York, n.d. 

10. Duncan Aikman, "American Fascism," Harper's Magazine, April 1925, pp. 513-519.

It is important to remember that footnotes must give all information as to source that the text of the theme does

College Rhetoric not mention.

When a title and a book are mentioned in the discussion, the note may be made to a particular book or text; as, 1. "George Eliot," Cambridge History of English Literature. 

2. "Gertrude Stein" in "Books," Time, September 11, 1933, p. 57. 

3. "Ben Jonson," A Dictionary of European Literature. 

4. “The Pre-Raphaelites," The Outline of Art, by Sir Wil liam Orpen, op. cit.

Bibliography. The bibliography is an important part of a research paper. It must include all the works studied in the preparation of the paper, classified and alphabetized. 

The following order is generally used in the grouping of the references listed in the bibliography: 

1. Books, anthologies. 

2. Magazines. 

3. Newspapers. 

The cover. The long and bulky theme should be encased in a folder, on the cover of which should appear the following notations: 

1. The title of the paper (underscored for italics). 

2. The author's name. 

3. The name of the course. 

4. The date of the writing or the date on which the paper is due or is submitted. 

5. The instructor's name. 

Shorter themes and all class exercises should be folded longitudinally, so as to open for the reader as a book. The

The Manuscript 

19 above notations should appear on the front cover of the folded pages. A little care in holding the two edges of the pages when one is folding them will help to give the assembled parts a neat appearance. 

Syllabication. The hyphen is used at the end of a line to separate the syllables of a word a part of which must be carried over to the next line. The hyphen must always come at the end of the line, not at the beginning, where one occasionally finds it in a student's theme. 

In dividing a word at the end of a line, one should always consider the reader, and make the break come at a logical place, so that the pronunciation and the meaning of the word may be easily visualized. The division is poor if one must glance a second time at the first part of the word before one can put the parts together to get a word that will fit in the context of the sentence. 

The good printer so spaces letters and words in a line that a word or full syllable will come at the end of each line. The margin at the right should be fairly even, although there is necessarily some irregularity when one writes on the typewriter or in longhand and divides the words only at syllable divisions.

The following suggestions may help in making divisions when a part of a word must terminate a line: 

1. The hyphen should come between the parts of a compound word: short-cake, smoke-stack, topsy-turvy, sleepingroom. 

2. The hyphen may separate a prefix from the word to which it is prefixed: pro-German, anti-slavery, countermove, ful-filled, re-paint, bi-plane, dis-appear, re-liable, prenatal, pro-nouns, con-junctions, re-strictive, ad-verbs, interjections, ex-pletives, tele-phone. 

3. The hyphen may separate a suffix from the word it. terminates: change-able, help-ful, win-some, child-like,

College Rhetoric introduc-tion, truthful-ness, responsi-bility, courage-ous, sing-ing, thorni-ness, picnick-ing. 

4. The hyphen should not separate a one-letter syllable from the rest of a word. By properly spacing his letters, the writer may carry over or add a single letter without making a very irregular margin. At the end of the line the hyphen takes up the space of one letter, and hence the following divisions seem absurd: brewer-y, dew-y, show-y, don'-t, fier-y, bacteri-a, pian-o. One should always carry over the entire word if there is just space for one letter at the end of a line, even though this one letter may be a syllable. The following divisions, though common in student writing, are not regarded as correct by careful printers and writers: a-gain, I-talian, e-mit, o-mega, U-ther, u-surp. 

5. One should not hyphenate abbreviations, even though each part of the abbreviation represents a part of a compound word. Wrong: D.A.-R., Ph.-D., in-st., Ok-la., Cal-if. 

6. The separation of proper names should be avoided whenever possible. To be avoided: Roose-velt, Mississ-ippi,

Miss-issippi, Kan-sas, Nebras-ka, John-son, Fran-cis, Henry, Ru-dolph, Miss-es, Faus-tus, Ham-let. 

7. When the addition of a suffix causes a doubling of the final consonant, the additional letter should be placed with the suffix at the beginning of the new line: 

RIGHT: sit-ting, refer-red, win-ning, ship-ping, preferring, begin-ner, excel-lence, quit-ter, stop-ping. 

WRONG: sitt-ing, referr-ed. winn-ing, shipp-ing, preferring, beginn-er, excell-ence, quitt-er, stopp-ing. 

8. One should never separate two consonants or two vowels which represent the sound of a single letter. 

Wrong: arraig-ned, re-ad, digrap-hic, decip-her, rhy-thm, psyc-hology, psyc-hic.

The Manuscript 

21 

9. One-syllable words should never be divided. If there is not room to put the whole word at the end of the line, the entire word should be carried over to the next line. Wrong: thro-ugh, tho-ugh, pre-ach, bo-il, mo-on, sta-unch, qu-ack, qu-ail, bre-ath, re-ach. 

10. In general, one ought not to divide short words even though they may be made up of syllables having more than two letters. Such words as the following are better not separated: po-et, ri-ot, to-paz, rel-ic, pu-pil, co-zy, ab-bot, civ-il, scis-sors, mea-sles, fea-tures, tri-als, wor-thy, du-els, mus-cle, im-age, bu-gle, en-sign, ra-vine, cri-sis. 

The student would do well to consult the dictionary for syllabication of words. The standard dictionaries mark with a light hyphen the places for proper syllabication.

The heavy and long hyphen is used between compound terms. Any place where one finds a hyphen, whether light or dark or long or short, is a legitimate place at which to make a word division. Consult the dictionary whenever in doubt.

The hyphen. The hyphen has two distinct and important uses: (1) to join the parts of a compound word, and (2) to join syllables of a word when they must be separated in writing.

COMPOUND WORDS 

Since there are no fixed rules governing the hyphenation of compounds, one must learn by observation and from the dictionary which forms to write solid, which to separate with hyphens, and which to write as phrase terms with no connecting mark. The average student is likely to use too many hyphens rather than too few. Unless there is good reason for the hyphen, it is better to omit it. The tendency today among reputable printers is to omit the hyphen from compounds which have long been recognized as words

2.

College Rhetoric having single, definite, and fixed significance, though there are some exceptions to this rule; e.g., mother-in-law, socalled, twenty-one, forget-me-nots, attorney-general.

The following rules may prove helpful in combining words to be used as compounds: 

1. The hyphen is used to join new combinations of words: parent-teachers association, daylight-saving time, hitchhiker, day-dodger, time-server. 

2. When the cardinal or ordinal numerals are used in compounds, they are usually separated by the hyphen: twenty-third, twenty-two, ninety-nine, ninety-ninth. 

3. Fractions written out are usually written with the hyphen joining the numerator and the denominator: twofifths, three-eighths, nine-fifteenths. When either the numerator or the denominator has a hyphen, the hyphen is then omitted between the numerator and the denominator: two thirty-fifths, eleven twenty-thirds, twenty-five ninety-eighths. 

4. Pronouns and nouns combined with nouns to denote gender are usually separated from the nouns by hyphens: he-bear, she-bear, turkey-cock, hen-sparrow. 

5. Proper nouns and proper adjectives combined are usually joined by hyphens: German-American, Franco 

Belgian, Anglo-Saxon, Spanish-American War, Indo-Euro pean, Balto-Slavic. 

6. Proper nouns or proper adjectives are usually separated from prefixes or suffixes by hyphens: pre-Shakespearean, ex-President, Chaucer-like, pro-German, anti-Semitic. 

7. Any prefix which has not become assimilated into the word it goes with must be separated by a hyphen. The following prefixes are usually hyphenated with nouns which follow them: ultra-, ex-, self-, pre-, and sometimes re-, non-, co-, half-. Examples: ultra-conservative, ex-convict, selfeducated, self-esteem, pre-prohibition, non-essential, co-

The Manuscript 

23 author, re-enforce (reënforce), "co-ed" (but coeducational), half-mast, half-tone. 

8. Family names coupled in surnames: Galli-Curci, Haldeman-Julius. 

9. Verb forms are usually separated from other parts of speech in compounds with the hyphen: clean-cut, so-called, red-tiled, well-bred, sight-seeing bus, fault-finding people, snow-covered Alps, fine-toothed, pleasure-loving, hightoned, weak-minded, machine-made, two-faced, goodhearted, eagle-eyed, pigeon-toed. 

10. Verb phrases used as adjectives or nouns have hyphens to separate the words in the phrases: blow-out, cut-out, jump-off, cut-off, coming-out party. 

11. The hyphen separates the words making up a phrase or a clause which is used as a single part of speech: a don'tcare-if-I-do person, a matter-of-fact person, hard-and-fast rules, stick-to-it-iveness, hand-to-mouth, a house-to-house campaign, a take-me-as-I-am person, a rake-hell lad, a hit-and-run driver, an easy-going man. 

The following compounds are correctly written with hyphens: absent-minded fore-tooth aides-de-camp free-hearted high-pressure knee-deep attorney-general free-spoken knee-high barrel-shaped good-fellowship knight-errant bas-relief good-hearted bric-a-brac goose-quills bridle-wise green-eyed bull's-eye half-and-half knock-kneed left-handed lieutenant-general light-fingered by-laws half-baked light-footed by-products half-blooded light-haired court-martial half-breed major-general court-plaster half-mast march-stepper cross-reference half-witted odd-acting cross-stitch hand-washed double-dealer heavy-hearted ox-eyed ping-pong

1. All true work is sacred. 

2. What is good is worth repeating. 

3. Labor to keep alive in your breast that little spark of celestial fire called conscience. 

4. Are you in earnest? 

5. Hence, you idle creatures. 

6. Nobody home? 

7. Done! 

Note. In conversation and dialogue, the first word of each speech begins with a capital: 

HAMLET: But, in the beaten way of friendship, what make you at Elsinore? 

ROSENCRANTZ: To visit you, my lord; no other occasion.

The first word in every line of poetry should be capitalized: 1. 2. 

A violet by a mossy stone 

Half hidden from the eye! -Fair as a star, when only one 

Is shining in the sky.

Nature and Nature's laws lay hid in night: 

God said, "Let Newton be!" and all was light.

3. 4. 5. 6. "I'll be seein' y'u," said Polly, Dimpled, laughing, blonde, and jolly, Eyes that dance and lips that pucker, Fingers gripping all-day-sucker. 

Maud Muller, on a summer's day, Raked the meadow sweet with hay. 

There was a young lady of Niger 

Who smiled as she rode on a Tiger; 

They came back from the ride 

With the lady inside, And a smile on the face of the Tiger. 

These be 

Three silent things: 

The falling snow . . . the hour 

Before dawn . . . the mouth of one 

Just dead.

Note. Certain free-verse writers ignore the rule of beginning the first word of each line of verse with a capital letter. The following will illustrate this scheme:

The dim gray of a chilly dawn follows a night of clinging fog and anxious watching in a starless gloom; gloom in which men wrestle with dull fears and hear no note of courage, but only the grim satanic laughter of evil shadows, darker than the mist.

No special effectiveness can be claimed for this modern mode, which would seem to mark only a desire to impress by doing "something different." Since it has not been fundamentally useful, it has not gained wide popularity, and the likelihood is that only a few poets will continue to observe it.

The pronoun I and the interjection O should be capitalized: 1. 

O Lord, Thou knowest how busy I must be this day: if I forget Thee do not Thou forget me. 2. 

I do not ask, O Lord, that life may be 

A pleasant road; 

I do not ask that Thou wouldst take from me 

Aught of its load. 

The first word of a direct quotation, when this expresses a thought as a sentence, should begin with a capital letter:

The Manuscript 

27 

1. May I ask you, Who took the responsibility of this task? 

2. The question is, How are we to get home? 

3. The proverb I learned is, "The rotten apple spoils his companion."

Every proper noun and every word standing for a proper noun should be capitalized. Proper adjectives derived from proper nouns and still associated with proper nouns should be capitalized: 

1. Jacob Jacobean. 

2. Elizabeth-Elizabethan. 

3. Italy Italian. 

4. America-American. 

5. England-English. 

6. France French. 

The problem in capitalization is to know which nouns are proper and which are general or common. The following suggestions may help in determining which words to regard as proper nouns and proper adjectives: 

1. Nouns and pronouns referring to God, Jesus, or the 

Virgin Mary are capitalized: (a) Teach me, O Father how to ask Thee each moment, silently, for Thy help. (b) Father in Heaven! Thou didst send Thy Son to reveal Thyself to us, Thy Father-love. (c) He prayed to the Blessed Virgin. (d) Jesus, Christ, Holy Ghost, Son of God, the Messiah, the Redeemer are names conventionally applied to the Deity, the Supreme Being. 

2. Names of churches or church organizations, the names of religious organizations and monastic orders, and the titles of members of monastic orders should be capitalized:

College Rhetoric (a) The Methodist Episcopal Church. 

(b) The Young Women's Christian Association. 

(c) A Franciscan Friar is the main character in this story.

(d) Foreign Missionary Society. 

3. Names of days of the week, months of the year, holy days, holidays, and festival days are capitalized: (a) He came Tuesday. (b) He left home October 20, and returned Christmas Eve. (c) Our Easter vacation begins on the Thursday before 

Good Friday. 

(d) How shall we celebrate New Year's Day? 

(e) The banks will close on the Fourth of July and on 

Labor Day. 

(f) Every school child will be asked to plant a tree on 

Arbor Day. 

Note.-Names of the seasons, unless personified, are not capitalized:

Spring and summer, autumn and winter, rush by in quick succession. 

4. Names of political parties, of governmental officers, of organizations and institutions should be capitalized: (a) I shall vote a straight Republican Party ticket. (b) Governor Charles Bryan has been elected to serve a third term. (c) The oldest son of the King of England is the Prince of Wales. (d) We shall visit the House of Representatives and the 

Senate the week Congress convenes. (e) His case will be tried in the Supreme Court on Monday. 

5. Nouns personified are usually capitalized to indicate the personification: (a) All was dark outside his reading lamp. Love and

Nature and Art were shut out from him.

The Manuscript 

29 (b) The Woods are gone away; they have fallen and left us. 

6. Names of points of the compass used to designate sections of the country are capitalized: (a) The North fought against the South in the Civil War. (b) There is more wealth in the East than in the West. (c) The Orient is the Far East. (d) He comes from the mid-West. 

7. The first and principal words in titles, names of books, articles, lectures, and reports should be capitalized. The articles, conjunctions, and prepositions within the titles are not capitalized: (a) Edith Wharton wrote The Custom of the Country. 

(b) He reads The New York Sun. 

(c) Shakespeare wrote Romeo and Juliet. 

8. Geographic names of streets, states, counties, and buildings should be capitalized: (a) The Chesapeake Bay is twenty miles from my home. 9. Names of the constellations are capitalized: 

I can show you the North Star, the Big Dipper, Job's 

Coffin, the Milky Way, and the Seven Sisters.

B.A. (Bachelor of Arts) B.C. (Before Christ) A.D. (Anno Domini) Y.M.C.A. (Young Men's Christian Association) 

Note.-Abbreviations of words naming parts of speech, and symbols of foreign words of a general class, should not be capitalized within a sentence: e.g., i.e., viz., vs., conj., adv., adj., prep., vb.

The abbreviations for forenoon, afternoon, manuscript, and pages may or may not be capitalized. The small letters are now preferred by literary writers: a.m., p.m., ms., mss., pp.

CHAPTER II The Theme Planning the Theme 

O NOT balk at any assignment the teacher makes. If at any the teacher mark he permits you to choose from a number of topics, take the one that for you has the greatest vitality. If one title only is appointed, reflect that any young person of moderate brains, a high-school diploma, and even a slight experience with life can find something to say on any subject. If you doubt this statement, hazard some innocent remark before a group of undergraduates on anything in the world "from a mermaid to a rhinoceros," and see how soon you will have an argument.

Defining one's position. Look at your subject in the large. Let us suppose the theme topic to be The College 

Paper. A dozen, twenty, fifty things can be said about it; but no number of "points" will, without organization, make a readable essay. Let us see. The college paper, like the church, the traffic court, horse racing, the beauty parlor, or tobacco, is a social institution; and the most interesting thing about an institution is its reason for being. Now examine yourself. You believe that people are justified (or are not justified) in going to church, paying fines, betting on races, curling hair, sniffing snuff, or publishing and reading a school paper. Take your stand for or against the institution. 

Thesis. The statement of your position is your thesis. 

Let it be supposed that you find the existence of the college paper justified by its value. 33

College Rhetoric 

Lines of division. Value is a general term. Limit it by asking questions: Who receives the value? What group?

Here are natural lines of division. Do the officers of the school favor the continued publication of the paper? On what grounds? What of the faculty? the staff? the mass of students? The identification of the distributed values may well constitute the body of your theme. 

The introduction must introduce. Now give thought to your introduction. This must be interesting-not perfunctory. And it must really introduce-must stir the sense, and direct the mind of the reader to the consideration of your argument. Look for your introduction in the college scene itself. What are the various attitudes of Tom, Dick, Harry, Susan, and Mehitabel toward the daily or weekly college journal as you have observed them? But a word of warning: if any considerable number of minds are likely to have a thesis contrary to your own, recognize the fact here. By concession or a rapid answering of objections, deal with the opposition now-in your introduction. To drag divisive views into the body of your treatment will tend to weaken your exposition by destroying its unity. 

Conclusion by application. A mere summary of "points," arguments, ideas is likely to constitute a mechanical and ineffective conclusion. If possible, get action, motor appeal, into your final paragraph. Is the college paper so good that it cannot be made better? If not, what can be done?

Do not trail into something inconsequential here. End on your keynote.

The Outline 

Look at the following outline, which we have just been formulating together, and imagine yourself writing a theme from it. Could you not, if asked to do so, clothe these outline bones with the living flesh of real emotion, thought, language?

The Theme 

35 

Introduction: 

THE COLLEGE PAPER 1. Attitudes differ: 

a. Enthusiastic support. 

b. Meek acceptance. c. Indifference. d. Hostility. 

2. Current criticism of college journalism: 

a. Partial to groups and cliques. b. Characterized by personal references which cause pain. c. Immature, extravagant in style. 

3. Concession, or brief refutation of above criticisms. 

4. Thesis: The college paper is an institution of positive value. 

5. Transition: Promise to analyze value as shared by four parties.

I. Administration: 

1. Convenient for announcement. 

2. Medium for promotion of University ideals. 

II. Faculty: 

Opportunity to learn student opinion and sentiment.

III. Staff: 

1. Training for possible future vocation. 

2. Experience in news-gathering. 

3. Writing under pressure: incentive. 

4. Formation of ésprit de corps.

IV. Students: 

1. Information. 

2. Entertainment. 

3. Awakening of interest in college events. 

4. Promotion of solidarity, sense of institutional life. 

Conclusion: 

1. Improvements may be brought about not chiefly through a. censorship. b. faculty advisers. c. courses in journalism. 

2. Student pride, student sentiment, alone can materially contribute to elevation of standards.

College Rhetoric 

Let the students demand and produce a paper characterized by a. intelligence. b. candor. c. decent restraint. d. life.

The student will observe that the above outline is developed for the discussion of matters on which persons are likely to have opinions. With a subject of different type one might find it absurd to attempt to find a so-called "thesis." Thus, if one is to write on First Impressions of 

Chicago, he need voice no opinion whatsoever: he need not "take a stand" and defend it. On the other hand, the theme need not, and probably should not, be made a mere narrative. "I arrived in Chicago at six a.m., and the first thing on the program was breakfast at the station-cakes with maple syrup and a cup of coffee" is a bad start. It promises a dreary string of non-significant and improperly correlated personal details. Before writing on "Impressions," one would do well to group these impressions in accordance with some logical or psychological principle; invent an introduction which will really introduce; plan a conclusion which will effectively conclude; and then write the outline.

ADVICE 1. Submit an outline with every theme. The teacher may not insist upon your doing so, but he will most certainly not object. 

2. Write the outline before you write the theme-not after. 

3. Make the outline a record of thinking already donenot a promise to think when the necessity arises. 

4. Include a clearly stated thesis, or at least a purpose, in your introduction.

The Theme 

37 

CAUTIONS 

1. Do not number either your introduction or your conclusion. Neither of these is a member of a series. 

2. Do not use the word Body to represent a division of your theme. It will be understood that the large section intervening between introduction and conclusion is the "body." 

3. Do not include the same point (expressed in the same or different words) in more than one division of your outline. 

4. Use the same type of symbols for divisions of the same rank; as Roman numerals (I, II, III) or capital letters (A, B, C) for principal phases; Arabic numerals (1, 2, 3) for subdivisions; small letters (a, b, c) for subdivisions under numerals. 

5. If you have only one subdivision, do not give it a number. 

6. Do not use different parts of speech (as adjective and noun) to indicate points in the same series.

Exercises

2. Disadvantages.

III. Conclusion.

2. Thesis: Immigration should be further restricted.

Writing the Theme 

Approach the task with confidence. Anything you do will be vastly better than nothing. The poorest composition ever written by a freshman was in some sort a miracle.

The Theme 

43 Many persons have never written a paragraph or a line of any sort. It is a big, fine thing to have put even a few commonplace ideas together in such fashion that they say something—convey some sort of reflection, observation, or conviction to another mind. 

Do not hesitate or apologize. A Shakespeare clown says of his absurd bride, "A poor thing but mine own." A Yankee saying is when there is only a little molasses in the cup- “It will last till it's gone." Old Ulysses and his worn-out, battered crew, starting on a final cruise of adventure, admitted that they were not the men they once had been, but added cheerfully, "That which we are we are!" Do not say, "I never could write a theme; therefore I cannot write one today.". Say rather, "What I can do I can do. Here goes for the best work I can turn out now. Some day I will do better." When the paper is done, you may think it pretty good-it will probably be better than you think it is. 

Pick a subject related to yourself. If you are asked to choose your own subject, try to find one that touches your own life: your experience, knowledge, interests. But do not make the mistake of thinking that these are more limited than they are, or that what you know would not make good composition material. Anybody who ever saw a carload of coal dumped, or heard men talk about horses, or voted in a school election, or heard good or bad singing, or saw two negro teams play baseball, has plenty to write about. If you have waited your turn in a doctor's office, or seen bugs roll a dung ball, or studied manners in a street car, or washed shiny earthenware, or chopped cotton, or raised cauliflower, or fished for minnows with a bent pin, you have thrilling material. A young woman from a rural district, upon being asked to write on The Farmer's Problems, declared that the only problem she could think of was where the farmer should put his watermelon patch. She was only mistrusting her own experience. She really knew

College Rhetoric a good deal about farm mortgages, crop failures, soils, rotation of crops, markets, transportation, rural schools and churches, and rural politics. When once convinced that what she knew was important, she produced a readable paper. 

Think things over. ting ready to write.

Take all the time you need for get- 

Pace the floor. Sharpen your pencil as often as you like. Bend your mind to your subject until it seems stirring, vital to you. Chesterton says, "There is no such thing as an uninteresting object in the universe. 

There are only uninterested people." When your subject matter begins to live, pick out the most vivid features of it. 

These are salient, significant. Resolve to write about these. 

See the thing through. But you must relate your ideas to one another. You must see your theme as a whole. 

Have not thoughts but a line of thought. Try to have vision, foresight, to glimpse the whole subject at once. Let a simple purpose, logical or emotional, run through your entire piece. It takes some strength of mind to plan a whole argument or exposition, and some patience to execute one completely. Do not "string out" or "pad" your material to make it cover a given amount of space; on the other hand, do not omit necessary parts-leave no gaps. 

Keep your reader in mind: do all that you can to make him see, think, feel what you desire. To accomplish this you will need an outline. Perhaps it would be well at this point to read again the section of this chapter devoted to planning the theme. 

Write courageously. With a good outline actually made and on paper, your work is half done. Charge into the task of composition with pleasure, assured at a decent, possibly a brilliant, success. Write while you have steam up; keep going. Some fine authors write slowly, others rapidly; but all surrender to the mood of the hour and the theme.

The Theme 

45 Revise with care. When you have finished, let the work lie for a few hours or even days; then rewrite before you submit your paper to another's criticism. You will discover flaws and correct them; have new inspirations and work them out; condense here and expand there; catch elisions, mistakes in grammar, or other crudities; substitute bright, original phrases for hackneyed formulas; test figures of speech and see that all are organic and effective; scrutinize every statement, to make sure that it is true. 

Profit by criticism. Upon receiving the theme from your instructor with his comments, do not glance at the grade and, should it be low, surrender hope; or take one horrified look at the red pencil marks and crush the sheets together in resentment. You now have a chance to learn something: it is as if you were face to face with a friend. The reader may have marked you too low (he has his own not infallible standard of values). He may have made mistakes, may even have corrected what is already correct (he is human, and hence liable to slip of eye and judgment). In the exacting task of correcting a hundred papers your reader may have become nervous and have written a too sharp or caustic comment: he should not have done So, but try to forgive him, even as you hope to be forgiven. If you can get an interview with him, perhaps you two can come to a mutual clearing up of obscurities and a cordial rapport. 

Make a real advance in the second writing. A faulty theme should be revised in the light of the reader's corrections. Go to the limit of your powers in working over your paper. Do not be content with merely changing spelling and marks of punctuation in accordance with the instructor's suggestions. Such slavish work should net you an advance of about one per cent only on your theme grade. 

Try to learn the principle underlying each correction.

College Rhetoric 

Look up and study carefully every marginal reference. Recast sentences and whole paragraphs. Clarify all doubtful statements. Where the connection between ideas was faint, A FRESHMAN'S FIRST THEME, WITH HIS INSTRUCTOR'S CORRECTIONS

College Rhetoric Judging the Theme 

In the following pages certain student themes are offered for study, correction, and evaluation. These, and the accompanying specimen of a corrected manuscript, should be examined closely. By seeing what others have done, one may learn to do oneself: excellencies may be imitated, errors avoided. It may be well at this point for the student to turn to Chapter IX of this book and read what is there set down concerning the principles of rhetorical style. 

Let us propose the following as tests which one might profitably use in estimating the quality or value of a paper: 

1. Is the theme on the subject? Does it really and sincerely attempt to fulfill the promise made in the title? 

2. Does the author have something of value to say? 

3. Outline the theme. Is the material as a whole well organized? Is there unity of purpose? progress in thought? 

When you have finished reading, can you glimpse the demonstration as a whole and place the individual parts? Is there a logical grouping of sentences in the paragraphs? 

4. Is the form good in detail? Is the style dignified, grammatical? Are sentences well knit, phrases fresh and graceful? Is the spelling accurate? the punctuation intelligent? 

5. Would the paper look well in print, just as it stands?

The following are original themes, good and bad, written by undergraduate students and reproduced here without alteration. 

1. Make any comments, corrections, or instructions you care to offer between lines or in margins, as in the specimen manuscript on pages 46 and 47. 

2. Grade the paper according to whatever system of grading is employed in your college. 

3. At the close of each paper, write a general estimate or evaluation of the theme as a whole. Explain what you approve and what you disapprove. Advise the author as to what he might profitably do in rewriting the paper or in developing his powers in composition.

MY VACATION READING 

Books are very pleasant to read with leisure but they sometimes prove difficult to study. I am very fond of books, nothing is more pleasant to me than to have a nice book to read after the work is done and you don't have any one to talk with. I especially like to read on a rainy day when there is nothing else to do but stay in the house. 

My collection is very small. I only have about fifteen, some are classified as good books, some are the lives of great men and the rest are just cheap novels. The books

48B 

Introductory Practice Leaf that I like are a few novels. They are a few novels by 

Zane Grey, they are exciting and they keep you interested in the book. The best book I read was Ben Hur. I also started Pilgrims Progress but it did not appeal to me so I didn't finish it. Being interested in most outdoor sports, I get pleasure out of reading Hunting and Fishing magazines. 

My home is located near the water, it is nice and quiet and proves to be an ideal place for reading. There are a few summer homes built close and are owned mostly by people from Washington. Most of them prove to be very good friends. Even though my home is in the country, it is hard for me to get to read because there are other things which prove more interesting to me. 

For about three years in high school I was required to make book reports. In that way I found out something about books and how interesting they could be. They also prove to be a helpful way in finding information. 

My family does not take very many newspapers, only about three weekly papers and a few monthly magazines. 

I like to read newspapers because I want to find out what is going on around me. Last summer I read nearly all

Introductory Practice Leaf 

48C the papers that we got and also a few that were given to us. The ones I enjoyed most were the Hunting and fishing magazines and some continued stories in the Grit paper. 

The stories were written by some well known authors. 

I did not have any steady work last summer but what I did was mostly working on the water. After my main work was done I usually found amusement in some outdoor sport and did not read much except on Sundays. In the evenings I helped my mother with the work around the house. My favorite sports are swimming, fishing and baseball. In the hot afternoons I could get more pleasure out of taking a swim or going fishing than I could reading. In that way I nearly always neglected my reading, but when I did get interested in a book, I would generally finish it and let the work go undone.

The value of reading is very essential, it acquaints you with what is going on, and broadens the mind to some extent. A great many people read just for the sensual pleasure that they get out of it. If people did not read, they would have to depend on some one else for the news and it would be exaggerated sometimes. The moral value of reading is also very great. During the summer I read

48D 

Introductory Practice Leaf some religious papers and they taught me what was the right thing to do. I think if more people would read religious papers there would be some better people and the morality of the country would be greatly improved.

ORIENTING THE FRESHMAN 

It has only been the last eighteen months, or two years that there was such a public condemnation place upon the very old, collegiate tradition and manners; in which the newly arrived students were oriented. 

I have a personal conviction that this thesis, will be severely criticised by the reader. But I am in that respect, like one of our United States Senator, who some time ago made this very broad assertion; "I have made up my mind (if it is possible for man to make up his mind) and God 

Almighty can not change it."

This is exactly where I stand on this subject; knowing what I do about the minor and minute details of orienting freshmen on our campus. The method used at present, and in the past with the hope of obtaining results (such as, loyalty, stand pat, school spirit, willingness to do, and an active participation leading toward mutual success) has been for centuries fought out between great armies; and

48F 

millions of lives lost, all in vain. For today those very same results are still hoped and believed to be possible by an "Iron hand" rule. "Man's life and nature is, as it was, and as it always will be." 

That very quotation is my test, or reason for treating this grave subject; which may cause expulsion from school, if the proper department would consider it very dangerous. 

The orienting of freshmen in this school and the methods employed, here as else where, are contrary to that law or that quotation which is based upon sound facts; even has stood the very test of battle, "Man's life and nature is, as it was, and as it always will be." 

There fore it is not evident and scientific evidence based upon solid, cold, facts that Iron Rule does not produce the desired results; love, faithfulness, willingness, obedience, working for mutual success. One of the hardest things in life to do is to buck up against human nature. So why try to do it? It does not give results, cannot, will not and never will be able to give them.

It is amazing that our great scientist do not endeavor to settle this question or like many other questions, give it up

48G and leave the philosopher make his study of it, for truly it could be proven a psychological fallacy to expect forced labor to be willing and obedient servants of his fellowman. 

Even a study of the historic thesis of forced labor will reveal, that the Great Roman Empire came to an untimely end because of serfdom, forced labor. The very same spart of human nature which caused them to rebell caused the American Civil strife.

But yet we think it still can be done, it is still practiced every year; knowing as we do what past experiences have taught us.

Would it not be better to employ some other more scientific method. Since the world is "agogg" on scientific proceedure, which would be more kindlier. Kindness always survives over its enemy, hate. Why not use a modern day method; which is in preparation. 

Convince the public that our schools are no longer shelter for "child like behavior," but the abode for matured thinking people. Thus dealing with the orientation of freshmen in a "gentleman like manner" and not the barbarian method of centuries old. 

The idea I wish to leave is that, the nature of man is

48H 

to do; but not to be forced to do. Every one of us are human beings, so why try to go contrary to our own convictions. Can not we at least profit by past experiences and use a modern scientific method to try to obtain human willingness, love, school spirit and for ever abolish the fallacy of "Iron Hand" methods.

ATHLETICS 

Whether athletics were practiced extensively among ancient Jews is uncertain, but there is a phrase in one of the psalms "rejoicing as a strong man to win a race"-which suggests that they were. We are certain, however, that the ancient Greeks made much of athletics. We have many records to show that part of the education of every wellborn youth of Athens was a training in the manly sports of his country, and that the Greek looked upon the development of his body as the first requisite of his education. In fact so far did this ideal of a perfect body carry the Greeks that they developed the art of sculpture to a perfection in the representation of the human form never since equaled. 

In our colleges athletics take a prominent part. We believe in training not only the mind but also the body, and provide teachers and expensive equipment for such training. No student who is physically capable of taking daily exercise is allowed to go through his earlier college years

48J 

without entering courses in this important branch of college work. Military drill is the form in which most men students are forced to undergo this training. Some students, however, object to drill on religious grounds, for it is well known that some religious sects believe strongly in non-resistance. Since authorities do not wish any man to violate his religious principles, such men are allowed to substitute gymnasium work instead. All women in the college are required to take gymnasium during the first two years of their course. Much attention is paid to the development of teams to contest with other colleges in football, baseball, and basketball, and in this direction the greatest imaginable enthusiasm of the students is called out. They make excitement of contest, the stimulus of unrestrained enthusiasm, the exercise of loyalty, and the sense of collegiate soldarity which the Saturday afternoon game gives them almost their only chance to feel. Few college occasions are more brilliant than a football game on a keen October afternoon. The low sun strikes across a grandstand fluttering with gay banners and pennants of many colors, and alive with young men and women whose holiday faces are joyous with zest of life, and eager with

hope of victory. This development of contesting teams has gone too far in view of the general purpose of the college. It has absorbed the greater part of the energy of the athletic department so they pay comparatively little attention to the rest of the students. Their need to train winning teams makes them reject the services of unpromising material, and throw great stress on the training of the best they can find. And on the whole these are overdeveloped. In other words they devote their attention to the training of those who need it least, and neglect those who need it most. Of course those who need it most are weaklings who sit in the grandstand and take their exercise. by proxy. The purpose of athletics in college is to provide healthful exercise and recreation for its students in view of their normally sedentary and arduous work in school. 

This purpose could be better served if the college devoted all the energy of its athletic department to bringing every student out upon the athletic ground to play football, baseball, basketball, tennis, and other sports. 48K

THE DIFFICULTIES IN WRITING A THEME 

In our course in English this year, we are required to write many themes. These themes are taken different ways by students. By this I mean that they are dreaded by some while they are liked by others. Writing themes, to some students, is a hard job while to others it is an easy one. When giving the pupils the opportunity to write on their own topics some are satisfied while others don't know what to write about. You are always given your assignment about a week ahead of when it is due. All of that week you should gather information for your theme. 

If you have heard an amusing or thrilling experience, jot it down in brief, for it may be of some use in writing your theme. Artitles in newspapers often afford a good subject on which to write. Students often make up their mind that they can't think of anything to write a theme on and they very seldom make a good job of it. Often however, the pupils are asked to write themes on specific

48M 

topics. This also is usually met by storms of disapproval. 

They say that they don't know enough about the subject to write a theme on it. Maybe they don't but they will never gain any information about the subject by fretting over it. 

The thing to do is to get out and look for information about the subject. Sometimes the students put off writing themes until the last night and then they hurriedly write one. These are not themes but are merely bunches of poor organized words. Some things cannot be done in a hurry and theme writing is one of them. An instructor when assigning a theme, should never state how many words long it should be. This often makes a student spoil his theme. 

If he hasn't enough words he will add useless and poor sentences to make enough and if he has too many he will cut down his theme and sometimes take out important parts. Altogether theme writing is a very ticklish job both for students and instructors.

48N

1934, PRENTICE-HALL, INC.]

THE SORT OF HOME I SHOULD LIKE TO OWN 

My intense longing was formerly for a home of riches and spendor. I wished to have the kind of place where it is natural to say, "My hat, James," and motor to a pink tea; the sort of place where the betterment of the ignorant poor is the topic of conversation, while the high iron railing keeps the sidewalk pedestrians from touching the walls of the magnificent edifice. Money is not a mtter to be considered there. The great are called by their first names. There one speaks of "the millionaire I met at 

Newport," and "the young count with whom I traveled in Provence." In those circumstances it is easy to refer to "the winning of the international cup-race by my yacht," "the record my bay horse made in the derby at Epsom 

Downs," and "the way I played faro and roulette at Monte

Carlo." 

My ideals have changed, however, and conditions such as lack of ready cash may further alter my plans; but my

tastes do not yet require a hovel on Poverty Boulevard. 

My artistic sense has not quite evolved to the stage where I am satisfied by a hut built of flattened tin cans and adobe. My demand is not quite met by a bent rusty stove pipe for a chimney.

A small but neat and comfortable house is my desire at -present, and will be my desire until my oil wells yield ten thousand barrels a day, and until I get my patent on "The 

New Improved Fly Swatter." The country, where one can get pure air and sunshine, suits me. Yet the advantages of being in touch with the city, and not living in narrow isolation will be sought. The privilege of an outdoor life and humanizing contact with the soil is beneficial. 

This country place will be my home always,-during the summer's long days, when the autumn rain beats steadily on the roof, and when its cheery lights shine out across the winter's drifted snow.

Pardon me for being indefinite in regard to details, since they are variable and can be changed indefinitely. One cannot say that materials of certain dimensions, quality, and quantity will make a home. The chief thing is, that the resulting interiors do not look like hotel lobbies. There are certain things felt rather than seen that make a home.

48P It is questionable if there can be abiding happiness in a residence where the deed is held by some New York realestate company, or the mortgage is held by Skylock in some humbler city.

Fire proof material is preferred, because it is a good business proposition. As for all real economies, the first cost may be more, but an air of permanence is given that no frame building can have. The danger of Indian raids is over; consequently better structures are being erected in this country. Therefore let my house be built like London 

Bridge: 

"Build it up with stone so strong 

Then 'twill last for ages long."

Americans developed the grand American type of architecture, the colonial. At a lower cost more beautiful buildings can be built in this type, than in the Gothic, Italian, or French Renaissance. Also, because it is artistic, I, as a patriotic American, favor the colonial style with its balance and proportion. It is said that other styles of buildings may come and go, but the colonial is always correct. 

The rooms will be light and airy, with high ceilings. 

A good modern system of lighting and heating will be installed. As a rule bright colors will be in the north

48Q 

rooms and darker colors in the south rooms. The furniture will be sturdy and substantial. Satisfying plainness is less tiring than elaborate attempts at beauty. Prints of really fine work will be preferred to paintings by inferior artists. One motto on the wall will be good, but trinkets that only catch dust will be absolutely eliminated. A fireplace and shelves of books are needed for a home. 

The lawn will be adjoined with tree bordered paths, and will afford a long unbroken view toward the highway. 

Tops of other houses will show against their groves. 

Green fields will surround the house, and woodlands, and streams that wander among the grassy banks, streams whose waters, splashing over the smooth round-pebbles, are ever clear.

The towers, domes, and minarets of my fairy place allure me no longer. Their bright mosaics, glistening fountains, graceful ebony columns, and silver lattice work, for me, have lost their charm. For they are soulless things. I never want to sit in their chilly, silent, tenantless halls. I never want to tread their cold hard stones. I do not want a mansion, however stately, built of lifeless crystal and heartless bronze.

48R Give me a house that has association with life, where every nook and corner is dear. Let the dwelling be far from hurry, strife, and battle's fierce contention. To the wanderer within the gate of abode let sorrow and disappointment be forgotten. To the passing stranger may happiness and childhood's pleasures be once more recalled, so that even he will say,-"The man who lives here cannot be other than wise and good, for this is his home."

48S 

[COLLEGE RHETORIC. 

1934, PRENTICE-HALL, INC.] 

.Rating.... Due

THE NEWSPAPER AS A COMMUNITY FORCE 

The newspaper a community force? Decidedly, from the time it is rushed from the press, smelly with printer's ink, half the items left out because they were "received too late for publication-full particulars next week," till it goes to its last resting place on the pantry shelves, the newspaper is a force. Regardless of literary excellence or rhetorical power; as a record of community interests; by virtue of the extensive field it needs must cover; it is bound to be a force. To touch every line of activity, to lead in every move for civic improvement, to be foremost in every advance for social uplift, to guide and encourage the intellectual life of the community, in every way possible to promote the public welfare-this is the province. of the community newspaper. 

The newspaper is a force then, from the standpoint of business, to be supported; politically, to be feared; morally, to be upheld; socially, to be courted; intellec-

48T 

tually, to inspire; historically, to be preserved. 

How eagerly do we scan the advertisements upon opening our newspaper! Not only do we wish to learn of the latest bargains, but as reading material we note the phrasing, the ingenuity of the composition. The gossip of business rivals invite interest in the local page. We sight the amount of space appropriated by the new hardware store, thereby gauging its standing in the business world. 

The amount of business enterprise in the community is reflected in the newspaper, through the offices of which many an advertising scheme is originated. If business is dull, we seek out the editor and put it to him to devise some means of starting things. Advertising pays—it is up to the editor to make it pay. We leave details to the editor. Business is dull, not because of the war in Europe, or failure of crops, or stringency in the money market, but because we lack a good, live editor, such as they have in our neighboring county, who concocts wonderful schemes of voting contests for the most popular young lady, a grand bargain dollar day, a grab sale, or the giving away of a piano.

In the world of politics, we feel keenly the force of the newspaper. Here, assuredly, we do not know our own

48U minds until we know what is the newspaper's. Do we wish to run for political office? Better sound the editor first, there's simply no use entering the race without the support of the paper. It takes a man of principle to run a newspaper during a campaign, to stand by his convictions, fair, impervious to temptation of personal advantage.

If we can enlist the services of the editor, we have a powerful aid in our campaign for civic improvement. Do we need better roads? Get the newspaper interested. 

Do we need a new town hall? Whisper it to the editor. 

To be sure, he never ceases afterwards to remind us of it, alluding on all possible occasions, to the "edifice of which we feel justly proud, having been largely instrumental in its erection." But the fact remains that the newspaper is largely instrumental in every public enterprise, discovering the need of it, encouraging the possibility of securing it, devising ways and means towards attaining it.

The newspaper plays a large part in shaping the morals of a community. For instance, what a powerful ally against the liquor evil. The paper that comes out boldly on the right side of this great issue is wielding a mighty

48V 

` 

power for good. For there is no question of its influence here; every stroke is bound to tell. It reaches the mass of the people, gets talked about, thought about; it is a foe at which its evil antagonist has no means of striking back. 

A newspaper that takes a high moral standard, will be supported. It may be weak, inefficient, but the community demands that it must possess a fair standard of morals. Ready to lend aid to charity and religious enterprises, eager to promote educational advancement, anything that points toward higher living and purer thinking-this must be the moral outlook of the paper. 

A spirit of neighborliness pervades the local pages. We are all a large family, feeling a keen interest in what seems trivial enough to the outsider. We want to know when 

John Jones went to a rival town, when young Brown took his first ride in his new car. We want to know when it is time to do our fall plowing, and when it is time to put on our winter flannels.

So-and-so is sick and we fly to visit him. A poor family moved into the neighborhood. We hasten to their relief. 

A lecturer is coming to town. We depend upon the newspaper for a statement as to his ability. It is the newspaper that stimulates the movement for the chau-

W tauqua and the lecture course, whatever of entertainment comes within our range; and it is the newspaper that is scathing in its criticism, if the expected treat does not fulfill its promises.

Then the Society Column is such a delight! Those satisfying write-ups that mark us as people of social prominence, we know how to entertain in the most approved fashion. If our names appear within this column, then truly we are within the pale. 

I recall a most humorous account, not the fault of the newspaper, I believe, but reflecting the attitude of the community towards a trying situation. A young man had been most faithful in doing janitor work for the Sunday school, ringing the bell and building the fires, quite gratuitously, in the schoolhouse where the services were held. 

The people whom he had thus benefited, sought to show their appreciation by holding a sort of reception for him. 

The plans were extensive; there was some decorating; a bounteous supper was arranged for; and as a crowning feature, they were going to present the young man with a chair. The write-up was duly appreciative. Each detail was dwelt upon, even to the presentation of the "small token of our gratitude, accompanied by the good wishes of

48X 

the entire community"; and then, quite at the end of the description, and seemingly not having affected the program in the slightest, came the sentence, "The only feature to mar the evening's pleasure was the guest of honor having failed to appear!" No comment seemed necessary. 

The newspaper simply pictured the situation exactly as it had occurred, and in fancy, I can hear the chuckling of the editor as he wrote it. 

Exceedingly critical are we of the construction, grammar, or spelling of our newspaper. We expect it to be a model of correctness, to possess a certain literary style, as indeed it should. Into how many homes does it enter as absolutely the only reading matter, excepting of course the Bible, and possibly a hymn book, a catechism, and almanac. Particularly is this true among foreigners. 

Some of us who live in neighboring localities, and who have the ability, contribute the news of our district, under suitable titles; as Balm of Gilead, Stoddard Lectures, and the like. When news is scarce, we fill in with bits of our own composition, sometimes in appropriate verse. 

Thus the paper offers a field for our literary aspirations. 

Through the editorial column there is a splendid oppor-

Y tunity for intellectual influence. Writing upon whatever topics are at the moment of peculiar interest to the community, or calling attention to the happenings of the world at large, there is a wonderful chance for directing thought along right channels.

Still one more division remains, one that endears itself to the hearts of the older subscribers who live largely in retrospect, and that is, "The Twenty-five Years Ago" column. There is an immense amount of satisfaction in rereading the events of a quarter centery ago, to those who have watched the passage of time from the same viewpoint; and the old people by the fireside sit in reverie after its perusal, and chat happily of by-gone days. 

So the newspaper a community force? It is a part of our lives. It announces our arrival into this world; watches our career through school, puts our names on the honor roll, follows us through college, eager to herald our first triumph; anxious to extol the worth of our "fellow townsman" if we do aught of merit; proud of our successes; sorrowing in our griefs; marries us-in conventional black though it be, and faithfully cataloging the list of impossible, well-meant contributions as "beautiful

48Z 

and useful presents"; buries us, writing an obituary in our memory that forgets our mistakes and remembers only our virtues.

Then let us consider the immensity of the newspaper's undertaking, and be chary of our criticism, lend it our support; give something of the encouragement and help to its author that he has given to us-the community.
