BALTIMORE: MY HOME TOWN 

Baltimore presents a very unattractive aspect to a stranger. Unlike Philadelphia or Washington, it had no original plan, and therefore seems to wander aimlessly over a vast area of rolling land. Unlike the big cities in the 

West, it is an old town, and every decade has left as its mark a type of architecture differing somewhat from any that preceded or followed. The motley result is an ugly conglomeration. A tourist has probably heard of Baltimore as the "Monumental City," "The City of Churches," "The Gateway to the South," and "The City of Beautiful 

women.' He has a mental picture of a sunny town, composed of wide streets, flanked by awe-inspiring statues and places of worship, along which walk petite southern belles, carrying parasols or wearing wide-brimmed hats. What he actually finds is an ordinary city differing from others of similar size only in that the streets are narrower and ill kept; the buildings perhaps are older and consequently

48BB 

dirtier; the monuments are unimpressive and usually obscured; the churches are just the same as millions of others all over the country though unquestionably more numerous than in any other city having the population of Baltimore; and most disappointing of all, the women are just like women found in Chicago and New York, and they do not even speak the southern "drawl.”

The people in Baltimore are divided into two classesthe "old families" and the others. The so-called "old families" in Baltimore represent the aristocracy of America. 

They have been in this country a long time and have known more generations of culture and wealth than the average citizen. Their seeming difference is so evident in 

Baltimore that they may be easily singled out on the street. The "others" differ from the middle class everywhere else only in that they move more slowly, think more slowly, and adopt new ideas long after they have been proved safe in other cities.

Recreation in Baltimore does not mean, as in other large cities, movies, theaters, concerts, clubs, and cabarets. It is more likely to mean hunts, polo, yachting, swimming, and social gatherings in large homes. The people are far more interested in the gossip rumored at a bridge game

48CC 

than in international politics discussed in a lecture. Conversation at dinner parties is far more likely to be on the good points of a hunter than on the good points of a new book or on the qualities of a good actress. 

Should one think of possible improvements in Baltimore, he would conceive first better streets, since in the present state they make such an unfavorable impression on visitors. 

It would be impossible to tear down all buildings and erect them again with some thought of harmony, but if more merchants and bankers would do away with their roccoco establishments and put up in their places modern and simple structures, the result would be a treat to every one's eyes and a benefit to the reputation of Baltimore as a metropolis. 

If our many statutes were each given a bath and a plot of well-kept ground, we should deserve the title, "Monumental City." If we only had more pride about historic relics, such as the Shot Tower and the tomb of Edgar Allan Poe, they would be cared for and publicized. If some restriction were made on the number of billboards and gasoline stations within certain limits, it would not be difficult for one to recognize a definite charm in the city.

48DD  

But to change the exterior appearance of a living thing, it is generally necessary to begin on the interior. If 

Baltimoreans would forget their sharp line of class distinction and join in a concentrated effort to improve the city in which they live, a great handicap would be overcome thereby. If they could find an interest in things cultural, the facilities for personal improvement would increase. The people complain that good plays never come to Baltimore. The producers claim that 

Broadway hits are not sent to Baltimore because the natives do not attend. We have few concerts, a brief opera season, no stock company, and practically no lectures or art exhibits of importance. It is not that Baltimore is of insignificant size but that the people in Baltimore are significantly indifferent to the things things that make life richer.

Any individual who has an ambition to improve a city as old as Baltimore would have a Herculean task, but an idealist might have just such dreams as these.

MY HOME TOWN 

Our town, which lies in the midst of the richest farming land of Nebraska, is an easy going, little community, fairly well content with itself, yet not without a certain sense of humor which prompts it occasionally to poke fun at its own shortcoming and general uninspiredness. It is a drab little town-drab both in paint and in morality. No particular distinguishment can it boast, being neither a Sodom nor a New Jerusalem. To those of us who have lived in it and who know the design of the wall-paper in every man's house, it is full of pleasant memories and sweet associations, but to the rank outsider it is exactly like 9,999 other villages which speckle the landscape of western prairies.

But it is this similarity, this very TYPICALITY, that makes a discussion of my home town worth while. As its difficulties and troubles are the difficulties and troubles of 9,999 other little towns, the solution of its problems means

48FF 

the possible answer to some great industrial questions, which have of late been interesting the general public. 

It is an agricultural necessity, the result of wheat and corn and cattle. Its function is to supply the people of the surrounding farms with a social and commercial center; to mediate between them and the city. Its business should not be confined to competing with its sister towns in matters of spider-legged water towers and electric illumination, but should include educational, social, and moral improvements so that it may be attractive and beneficial to the farmer whose crops must be marketed and whose sons and daughters must have training and culture. 

Our town, while thriving in a small way, is not doing one tenth of what it might accomplish in the matter of general uplift. It is not paying for value received. Could it but once get a vision of its possibilities; could some miracle inject new life into its sluggish veins, it would arise and meet the obligations which the twentieth century imposes. We await a prophet or an earthquake. Meantime, we may profitably speculate on ways and means by which improvement might proceed, should we ever exert ourselves.

48GG 

In the first place, there might be organized in Our Town a Citizens' Club, whose avowed purpose would be the advancement of the town in every way. Many towns have tried this scheme already. In fact, nearly every 

Nebraska village points with pride to its Commercial Club, or its Boosters' Club, or its Get-Together Association, whatever the name may be. But the organization I propose for Our Town would be a broader one than the majority of those with which we are familiar. It would not only include every man in town willing to pay a small membership fee, but would seek recruits among the farmers who have interests in the town. Its slogan should be co-operation. It would make its appeal to men primarily through their pocketbooks. If properly managed, interests might be aroused as never before, and the club could be made a permanent feature. Lectures by experts, and short courses in cooking and farm management, might be arranged for during the winter; prizes might be offered for "bests" raised or made during the summer months. 

Once the movement is started, there is no saying where it might stop.

It would not be very long before people could be shown that some kind of public library is almost a necessity, if

48HH  

they are to know more of modern methods in various trades and occupations. The library question might be agitated the second winter or some time when interest in other movements seem flagging. Technical and agricultural magazines and books, government bulletins and maps would probably be the best selection for the first installment of reading material. The beginning would have to be small, but if the library could be proved to be of use, it would grow steadily, and with it would grow in the community a zest for knowledge, a desire to be abreast of the times.

Awakened interest and quickened intelligence would sooner or later, seek out the school as a weak place in the civic armor. Here, of all places where efficiency should be at a premium, will be found waste, obsolete methods and antiquated curricula. Our Town people believe in education. Every one advocates it from the banker, who sends his children to eastern boarding schools, down to Daddy 

Norton, whose proudest boast is that his son, Ziny, "speaks algebry like a native." Nevertheless, we have not been willing in the past to concern ourselves with the school question deeply enough to demand that our schools shall keep step with the progress of civilization. We have been

48II content to pattern our system after an iron-clad model, and have not been keen enough to adapt it to the needs and requirements of life in our particular locality. 

A beautiful system we have in Nebraska-eight years in the grades, and the boy is shoved on to the high school. 

Four years of amo-amas-amating here, and he is supposed to go on to college. After due time he is graduated, and the world is his for the fencing. Singularly, this perfectly logical, nicely adjusted, machine education has not proved out. In Our Town a small percent of graders stay more than one year in high school, and a still smaller percent of high school boys and girls ever finish college. Tom Jones cannot understand why he must spend four years stuffing his brains with Nth power formulae and long lists of seemingly unrelated dates. His father builds the best kind of houses without using a single Latin conjugation; his uncle manages a half-section of land, and does not know that a Diet of Worms can have other than decapitalized interpretation. He sees high school graduates taking positions that an average eighth-grader could fill. Feeling his own lack of training, he soon leaves school to enter a tradeperhaps even in spite of parental advice and loses the inspiration that comes from contact with books.

48JJ 

As a remedy for early school leaving I would suggest a change of curriculum. Strong courses in soils, crop management, commercial geography and arithmetic, practical civil government, constructive English, usable manual training and domestic science might be offered. Anything may be added to the course of study that will benefit boys and girls and that will tend to keep them interested in school. Of course, a broader outline of study would necessitate better and more expert teachers, which improvement would in turn, make a greater drain upon our pocketbooks; but this would be borne cheerfully, if returns were certain.

Our Town must know that its future welfare is dependent upon the welfare of its children. To much regard cannot be shown for education of its young people. 

Neither can their social intermingling be neglected. Our 

Town has now little to offer any one, let alone our young boys, in the way of social recreation. Great-hearted philanthropists have at various times offered us pool halls and saloons, but these have not seemed to accomplish the good their owners no doubt intended. 

The problem of the boy or young man may be solved by offering him a chance to exercise both his mental and

48KK his physical powers in competition with his fellows, and by providing a place where he may meet others of his own sex and age for the purpose of clean conversation and companionship. Y.M.C.A.'s have accomplished this in some towns; but Our Town boys would probably be wary of the initial "C." A Junior Improvement Society might be organized, taking as its model the association previously suggested. Gymnasium equipment of a simple sort might easily be obtained, and room would be gladly provided. 

Winter evenings would then find restaurants empty and box-car card-rooms deserted. 

A great social handicap in Our Town is the lack of at social center. We need a better town hall, or an opera house, or some kind of building where people may center their interests. It is not feasible to use the school house, as some communities do, because of the inconvenience such use would cause the school and the school-teaching staff. 

This, however, is purely a physical difficulty, and will be overcome in time.

One side of life in my home town has not yet been mentioned, and that is the one which deals with our religious views. In Our Town there are three churches of about equal antiquity, architectural beauty, deadness. 3

48LL  

Each has its meager little feminine congregation, its fruitless round of morning and evening Sunday services, and its dreary mid-week prayer meeting attended by half a dozen old ladies, the minister, and the janitor, who fain would escape. Not one of these churches can afford to pay its minister a salary that is a fair living wage, or that would be any inducement to a man of talent and ambition. 

A combination of these churches would effect a great economy, not only in actual expenditures for heating, lighting, and general up-keep of buildings, but also in effort. 

One large sewing circle would be more efficient than three small ones.

One very good minister could be supported by the combined salaries which the churches now pay individually. 

Many objections to this plan of combination would present themselves, which are mainly outgrowths of prejudices and animosities of long standing, and it is these objections which will make church reform the slowest of all reforms in Our Town. People hesitate long before giving up traditions they have cherished from childhood; but it is time to awaken to the absolute necessity of religious reorganization and become broad enough to make concessions to each other's belief. If our ministers could forget their

48MM 

theology and study humanity with a sympathetic insight into its vices and its virtues, Our Town might see a light. 

We want to hear of man's obligations to man, rather than his relation to some dim Divinity, the conception of whom is not the same in any two religious sects. We want a workable religion.

In all these matters of business, school, and church we need to find a common ground and work with one accord to a common end. We lack a co-operative spirit, and it is that and that alone which will be the agent of our salvation.

I see in my imagination a unified town-a community of open-minded American men and women who have the same ideal of citizenship and strong sense of civic and social responsibility. Our Town will never be perfect; but by a continual urging of certain specific reforms, and by determined effort on the part of those who have been able to get a little broader vision than the usual one, perhaps Our 

Town may hope sometime to touch the hem of Perfection's garment.

Many conditions can be traced which evidence progression instead of retrogression in the future of the small town. Within two or three generations, the foreigner, who

48NN  

has been a source of anxiety and uneasiness in our land, will be one of us. Indeed, it may be he who shall supply the impetus which shall send us further ahead then we have been heretofore. For it is his sons now, who control our banks, our markets, our best farm lands. The substantial German and the dominating Bohemian will not be a detriment to us. They are mingling more and more with native-born Americans, and with each other; and the result will be a new brand of Americans, who, though of sadly mixed ancestry, will be, on the whole, a more vigorous variety.

Another straw which shows what way the wind blows, and which is indicative of a change in rural life, is the increasing agitation of the back-to-the-land idea. Men are beginning to awaken to some extent to the truth that a fortune lies in the soil for him who has the courage to seek it.

Men have asserted that a closer communication with the cities, by means of parcel post, extended mail service, telephone, and radio would finally result in the obliteration of the village. This assertion is not liable to be substantiated. More than ever does the country need the small town. It may be that the use the country will make of it

in time to come will not be quite the one we have been accustomed to attributing to it; but that it will find a place in our social system is beyond doubt. 

The mission of the little town of Nebraska is to minister to the physical and intellectual needs of the farming community to which it owes its being. It must attract the farmer. It must show him that it can give his boy and his girl something in the way of education that will be valuable to them; and it must provide the farmer himself with a means whereby he can grow morally and intellectually.

These things Our Town must do, if it would not suffer extinction.

The Theme 

49 

The Research Theme 

"Reading maketh a full man.”—Bacon. Accent onsearch, please! There are subjects the proper discussion of which requires citation of facts, statistics, opinions of such character and volume that no student would be expected to carry them "in his head." Library study here is both legitimate and necessary.

Never "crib." Warning must be given: research is work. 

Pleasant work it ought to be and often is, but work none the less. It should be observed, furthermore, that to use the library as a dodge for getting out of work is illegitimate and disgraceful. A purely original paper and a research paper are two distinct things. To "lift" a theme from a book or magazine and hand it in for credit as one's own is plagiarism-literary thievery. The plagiarist is cousin to the pickpocket and the burglar, and deserves to be expelled from a class as a thief is thrust out of a dormitory. 

Let us suppose, however, that, acting upon the advice of your instructor and with his full knowledge, you have resorted to the library with the intention of gathering facts or ideas or both. An intelligent method will help you in amassing material and arranging it. 

Read widely. If you have had a course in library science, you will know how to use the various "guides" and the card catalogue in locating the things you want. If not, you will save time by asking help of a librarian. Then, do not think you must find a book or magazine article of the exact title of the theme you intend to write; you must not, in fact, confine your reading to just one thing. Gather half a dozen or a dozen books or papers, and at least glance through them all. Be able to cite diverging evidences and contrasting opinions. Come as near as you can to finding out what a number of writers have learned and written in the field. 

Develop a scheme of your own for rapid reading, skimming,

College Rhetoric or "galloping" over parts which do not contribute to your purpose. If books are too discursive, read chapter headings carefully and study deliberately only chapters that bear most directly upon your subject. Spend a good many hours on the job. Have pride in getting to know more than anybody else in your class or your fraternity about this one matter. 

Make notes, but not too many. Do not try to copy all the good things you see you may find yourself copying a whole book. Enter salient points, just such as you will be able to use and therefore need to have where you can get at them. Be sure to jot down author, publisher, volume, and page of everything you intend to quote; to do so will obviate the necessity of looking back for these later. For ideas not to be quoted a few syllables will serve, provided they be such as you yourself can interpret. But outlining a chapter will fasten its substance in your memory; moreover, outlining is good intellectual exercise. Find a core of thought and gather all your material about it. Make an intelligent, suggestive outline, fairly complete in principal and subordinate parts. 

Write fully and intelligently. Now you are ready to make the theme itself. Write in your own language, enriched, perhaps, by the terminology of the subject about which you have been studying. Give credit for everything you quote (for the art of making footnotes, see page 16). 

Give credit also for every idea you appropriate from another ("It is Robinson's view that . . ." or "Sutcliffe differs from 

James here, offering the following observation . .").

Scorn to let your reader believe something original that is not your own.

The Book Review 

A certain amount of collateral general reading is usually required in a composition course. From the authorized list

The Theme 

51 of books select titles according to your taste or, better still, with regard to what you need to broaden your education. 

Allow plenty of time for deliberate and thoughtful reading of the entire book, to the last page. 

Put as much of yourself into your review as you possibly can-your own reactions, intellectual and emotional. Do nothing hastily. Do not answer questions in short words which are vague in tone and commit you to little ("yes," "very," "I do not think so"); a child could make such replies, and a deceitful child could make them without having read the text. Make your report a vehicle of selfexpression as well as of literary analysis. Contrive to tell what the book has done for you or to you; whether you like it or dislike it, and on what grounds. Be modest but courageous in the expression of your own thought and feeling. An intelligent teacher will respect you for candor as well as for industry. 

OUTLINE FOR BOOK REVIEW The Author: 

1. His time, his nationality, and some of his contemporaries. 

2. A list of his more important contributions to literature, including novels, poems, dramas, essays, and historical, biographical, and scientific works. 

3. A brief sketch of his life. 

Note.-State where you obtained the above information.

The Book: 

1. Number of pages; number of chapters. Is the book divided into parts? Do the parts or chapters have separate titles? If the book is so divided, quote some of the titles and comment upon their significance. 

2. General theme or purpose. Write a good paragraph on this point. 

3. The author's style: Is it entertaining? Would you describe the style as bookish, dull, direct, animated, brilliant, stiff, artificial, obscure? Are the sentences long or short? Define some of the

College Rhetoric new words. Discuss the quality and quantity of humor, pathos, dialogue, dramatic situations, description, epigrams. 

4. The author's learning: With what fields of knowledge does he appear conversant (history, astronomy, mythology, agriculture, botany, the fine arts). Illustrate by citations. Does the book reveal the author's likes and dislikes, convictions and prejudices? 

5. Author's philosophy: What does he believe? What ideas does he advance that are new to you? Does he convince you of the rightness of his views? Explain your position. 

6. How does the book compare as a piece of literature with others you have read? What sort of persons do you think would be interested in it? 

7a. (Answer only if the book being reviewed is fiction, prose or verse.) 

a. Type of story: Is it a romance or a realistic novel? 

b. Retell the story in one page of manuscript. c. Name and discuss the chief characters. Tell what becomes of each. Quote characteristic speeches and personal descriptions. d. Plot: Is it simple or complicated? Are there thrilling or dramatic incidents? Are there suspense, anticipation, surprise? 

Elaborate on these points. e. What is the historical time, place, social background? How much time is covered by the action? Are the scenes outdoors or indoors, or both? Are the customs, manners, and speech of the persons of the story old-fashioned or modern? Illustrate by citations. 

f. How satisfactory is the conclusion of the story? How does the author dispose of the major characters? 

7b. (Answer only if the book being reviewed is not fiction.) a. Write two or three pages setting forth the general content of the book. Quote liberally in illustration. b. What is the general scope or argument of the work? (Treat somewhat fully.) c. What is the author's method in developing his idea? Is it a narrative or an exposition? Illustrate by citations from the text. d. Compare with another book of the same sort that you have read. e. How important is it that others read the book?

CHAPTER III Words General Observations 

Importance of words. What is the most important invention of the human race? Not the plow, the airship, the radio, the automobile, the sewing needle, the compass; not the knife, the gun, the cart, the boat; not the telescope, not even civil government-but the word. Only the lucifer match (potential instant fire) contests with language (potential signal and understanding) for supremacy. Fire enables a man to live anywhere upon earth; speech enables him to live more fully, more richly, more safely wherever he chooses to be. 

Uniqueness of words. One man-with the coöperation of a relatively small group-invented the electric light; all men have invented the word. They have been at the task for tens of thousands of years, and are still striving to perfect the invention, though they will never succeed. 

Language attempts to communicate all feeling and all thought-every vast notion and every daintiest microscopic distinction of fancy and idea. It tries to picture all phenomena, not only discovered but discoverable. It is ever shifting with the panorama of things and human customs and desires. With the possible exception of mathematics, it offers the most fascinating study in the universe. 

Words as a by-product. Notwithstanding its incalculable value to humanity, language is little more than a byproduct of physiology. Speech is formed from the vitiated 53

College Rhetoric air, foul with waste substances, that is driven out of the lungs when it is no longer capable of oxidizing the blood. 

Except for the vocal chords (small resonant membranes in the larynx), all the so-called "organs of speech”—lungs, throat-wall, palate, tongue, nasal cavity, teeth, lips are used primarily for breathing and for the grasping, biting, chewing, turning, and swallowing of food. How finely the process of speech illustrates Emerson's statement, "Nothing is exhausted in its first use!"

The Student's Duty and Privilege 

Personal mastery and language. Urgent private and social needs, as well as the highest ideal considerations, dictate that the teaching and practice of speech and writing shall bulk large in the curriculums of schools, from kindergarten to college. Yet results will never be measured wholly, or even chiefly, by the excellence of textbooks or the devotion of teachers; they will accrue from the personal effort of girls and boys and men and women. Joseph Conrad was totally unacquainted with English until he was fully grown, yet became one of the finest masters of the tongue. Pride in one's own powers, pride in one's fellows-a nation or a civilization, pride in the human race, and a determination to realize the richness of life that comes with discovery: these urge the nobler sort of minds to continued probing into the secrets of language.

The materials. Objects of language study are everywhere: the interior of one's own mouth; one's vocal articulation; the words, tones, lispings, stammerings, and speechtricks of companions-their slang, imitations, metaphors, phrases; papers, of course, and magazines and books. 

The process. Most of us ought to spend more of our hoarded energy on speech, spoken and written, than we do. 

Even if we have to steal for this purpose some of the force that we have been putting into bridge-playing and money-

Words. 

55 getting, the investment will pay. We ought to stop halfstuttering and saying "unh" and "well-a." We ought to stop repeating, wasting words, chattering, and clacking. 

We ought to stop making vague, loose, ill-considered statements. We ought to quit the use of forms we know to be barbarous and ungrammatical. More than all, we ought not to be content with just any word that "comes." We ought to train ourselves to select the right word for each idea if such a word exists. 

The power in variety. Through learning words and accustoming one's tongue or pencil to say or write a different term for each color, image, or relation, one gains power; that is, one gains facility in communication, in projecting one's own particular thought, in achieving one's particular purpose. Variety is no mere ornamentation; it is the very life and efficiency of speech. If we are content with stating a fact in the loosest fashion, we remark, The man went down the street. But if the real truth requires a revelation of the manner of the man's going, we must substitute for went a less general verb. One of the following is likely to serve the purpose better: slouched, swaggered, slunk, tiptoed, strutted, crept. Any bright child, if set the task, could increase this list by a dozen lively verbs, each picturing a special and discriminated gait. 

Extension and intension. The extension of a word is measured by the number of objects or phenomena it covers: bird has more extension than wren. Its intension is measured by the number of attributes the word connotes: wren has greater intension than bird. That is, there are more birds than wrens, but wrens have all the characteristics of birds in general besides some special ones of their own, such as size, color, morphology, and habits. Each word has its proper use. One says birds (not wrens) when he is contrasting the former with mammals; but he would do well to say that wrens (rather than birds) flit about the eaves,

College Rhetoric because eagles (another kind of birds) would be hardly likely to do so.

Types of Wrong or Ineffective Word Selection 

Be merciful to your hearer or reader. Do not require him to do your thinking for you. Do not be content with the wrong word or the approximate word. Use the right word. 

Wrong word. Do not use the wrong specific term. Do not ask the salesman for pork loin when you mean to buy plate beef. Do not say that men should oil roads so as to avoid dust when you mean prevent or lay dust. Do not tell a boy that his winning a prize was a tribute to his ability when you mean that it was evidence of his ability. 

An amusing story (which probably has no foundation in fact) has been told of Noah Webster, who was punctilious in his use of words. The tale runs that the eminent scholar was seen by his wife in the act of kissing the maid. Upon the wife's exclaiming, "Why, Mr. Webster, I am surprised!" the culprit is said to have replied with dignity, "Mrs. 

Webster, can I never teach you a proper appreciation of the niceties of English speech? You are astonished. I am surprised." 

The following sentences are taken from actual writing of college students. Hints of possible improvement are in the parentheses: 

1. The town has not improved, because of neglect and disinterest (indifference?) on the part of its citizens. 

2. The city ordinance (council? government?) should buy and operate the water plant. 

3. Chain stores have now occupied (supplanted?) the small, inadequate stores of earlier times. 

4. Fast swimming is seldom used (practiced?), because the swimmer becomes exhausted. 

5. First-class students are criteria (examples? patterns?) of moral behavior.

Words Choice of prepositions. Idiom (the special character or habit of a language) determines the correct use of a given preposition. Care in the selection of prepositions is the mark of an active mind and a discriminating ear. Avoid confusion. One is in trouble, but gets into trouble; goes into a room, but stays in it. One divides candy between two children but among members of a group. One stays at home, not to home. The child was born at ten o'clock in the morning, on June the eighth, in the year 1934. 

The following sentences, taken from student papers, contain prepositions awkwardly or incorrectly used. More appropriate prepositions are given in the parentheses: 

1. I have been an observer to (of) countless quarrels on (over) this subject. 

2. He has a reputation on (for) being punctual. 

3. One's attitude of (toward, towards) his college subjects is important. 

4. There is considerable difference of (between) the two schools. 

5. The public shows great enthusiasm to (toward, towards, for) football. 

6. First in (among) the advantages of fraternity membership is the opportunity it affords of (for) social development.

59 Words of similar sound. Do not confuse words of similar sound but dissimilar meaning. A freshman, recording the impressions of his first week at college, wrote: 

The other students are just like you. Their willing to be kind to you and make you feel your one of the family. 

For their read they are; for your read you are. 

Similar blunders are seen in the following: 

1. Every student should precipitate (participate) in sport. 

2. The country was knew (new). 

3. This is how it effects (affects) industry. 

4. You use (used) to go to church with your boy friend. 

5. The village was forty miles distance (distant) from the city. 

6. The water supply was inefficient (insufficient). 

7. All these departments help toward given (giving) you a good education. 

8. Railways effect (affect) the town on the commercial side. 

9. We must be loyal to the particular group we belong too (to). 

10. We removed the petition (partition) between the two small bedrooms.

Note. A careless or partially literate person may write in place of the legitimate word a term of his own invention which resembles a true word. 

1. The buildings are kept in good condition by the use of funds approxiated (appropriated) by the government. 

2. A happy person does better work than an uncontented (a discontented) one. 

3. They came by theirselves (themselves).

65 General vs. specific words. "The wise man shows his wisdom in separation, in gradation, and his scale of creatures and merits is as wide as nature. The foolish have no range to their scale-what is not good they call the worst, and what is not hateful they call the best."¹

Do not be like the young woman who knew only two adjectives: whatever she liked she called "grand"; whatever she disliked she called "rotten." Let us make a scale of adjectives representing judgments in varying degrees of approval: superb. noble "GOOD" fine

Words Reference. The words this, which, it, they, their, he, his (to cite a few out of a general class) are relational. 

Relate means "carry back." The business of these terms is to connect the new thought with the old, to make a speech or an essay "all of one piece." Their proper use makes for clarity, prevents bewilderment. Be kind to your reader or hearer. Make his task of following your demonstration easy-as easy as you possibly can. Do not make him guess or let him guess at what you mean. tional words accurately; and use plenty of them. 

AGREEMENT OF NUMBER 

Use rela- Do not refer to a singular antecedent by a plural word, or a plural antecedent by a singular word. A student wrote,

The purpose of a university is so to familiarize the students with the meaning of life that he may be able to apply these to life's situations.

For he read they; for these read it or their knowledge. 

The following sentences, taken from student themes, show errors in number agreement: 

1. The student must attend punctually each class. However, they are (he is) allowed some cuts. 

2. Every one realized that it was their (his) own government and concerned them (him). 

3. Reading is good for anyone, no matter who they are (he is) or where they are (he is). 

4. Books make a person popular. His or her (“or her” is unnecessary) conversation becomes more interesting because of their (his) store of knowledge. 

5. If a person moves from one part of the country to another they (he) must adapt themselves (himself) to certain changes. 

6. Have everybody bring their (his) dime.

College Rhetoric 

7. Most of the new students desired to know something about his instructor (their instructors) and the price (prices) of books. 

8. I want to thank each and every one ("and every” is unnecessary) for their (his) contribution. 

9. Pass the contribution basket, so that if there is anyone here with a church envelope they (it) may be deposited. 

10. There is hope that with the closing of the speakeasies it will aid (that the closing of the speakeasies will aid) in restraining modern youths from criminal acts, who do so (who engage in such practices) only because he thinks he is (they think they are) doing something courageous in defying the law.

Each example cited above contains a pronoun which is not in number agreement with its antecedent (the word to which it refers). But confusion in number may appear where no pronoun and antecedent combination exists, as in the following: 

1. The old towns died as towns, becoming a mere group (mere groups) of buildings. 

2. One copy will be sent free to contributors (each contributor). 

3. She gave them all (each) a piece of bread-and-butter. 

4. Each freshman must report to the Dean of the College.

Here (in whose office) he will receive a directory card. 

5. Reading is divided (Readers may be divided) into two distinct classes: some people read for entertainment and others for knowledge. 

6. All the members of the class have (Each member of the class has) a vote. 

7. All persons in my class must hand in (Every person in my class must hand in) a notebook.

ANTECEDENTS 

A common fault in speech or writing is to use it, this, or which with no definite antecedent. Illustrations follow:

Words 

71 1. But if a woman wants to smoke, the street is no place for it. 

To what does it refer? For it substitute smoking or the practice. 

2. If the smoking habit has already been formed and can be broken, it should be done. 

What should be done? Try recasting the sentence:

If it is possible to break a smoking habit already formed, it (or the habit) should be broken. 

3. I should like to start my business subjects soon, because this is what I care for. 

IMPROVED: I should like to start my commercial subjects soon, since preparation for business is what I care for. 

4. Many streets are not paved, which (a fact which) causes unfavorable comment on the part of visitors. 

5. A city home usually has little or no lawn. This (the lack of space) gives a person used to rural surroundings a hemmed-in feeling.

Who refers to persons; which to inanimate objects or live things of non-human classification; that to either persons or things in a restrictive clause. 

1. The door was blocked by a large dog, who (which) eyed us angrily. 

2. My home town has about twelve hundred inhabitants, most of which (whom) work in a neighboring city. 

The examples just preceding contain nonrestrictive clauses. That is, The door was blocked by a large dog makes complete sense in itself without the aid of the clause following. The terminal clause does not identify a particular dog. But the dog that killed the sheep had to be shot contains a restrictive or identifying clause, that killed the sheep.

College Rhetoric Warning 

That is seldom or never used to introduce a nonrestrictive clause. 

WRONG: Washington, that had led the Revolutionary armies, became the first President. (Substitute who for that.)

WRONG: Gold, that is a rare metal, is a satisfactory medium of exchange. (Substitute which for that.)

Words 

73 Inflection Attitude. One cannot afford to be hostile or indifferent toward "grammar." The grammar of a language is its system; and an intelligent and intelligible speech system makes for certainty of utterance and ease of understanding. 

Definition. Inflection, which is the "bending," or change, of basic forms to give them a variety of meaning, is one of two most important characteristics of the languages of the western world. The Aryan races have hit upon this scheme of making one word do the work of two or three or a dozen; and some thinkers believe that such eminence as these races have attained in science, government, and invention has been due in large measure to the flexibility and relative simplicity of their speech. 

Clear thinking and inflection. Accurate use of the different inflectional forms is the mark of a keen mind, as well as of a sensitive ear. A good thinker will employ all the resources at his command whenever he desires to communicate his idea; and inflection is among the most important of these resources. It is not enough to have vocabulary merely (that is, words in unrelated form); one must be able to present ideas as they appear in the relations of subject, predicate, and object; of governing and governed, principal and subordinate, actual and desired, more and less, present, past, and future; of completion and incompletion. These relations (and others) are expressed with rapidity and certainty by those little standardized devices which we call inflection. The student ought not to think of grammatical differences as of a burden of facts to be learned or a burden of habit to be acquired. He would do well to regard them as achievements of mankind, which he too may achieve and delight in achieving; as ammunition which he may add to his own magazine of power.

College Rhetoric 

Scope. Words are inflected for case, gender, number, tense, person, mode, voice, and comparison. Inflection also may mark differences between finite and non-finite aspects of verb meanings; and within certain limits it may show differences between one part of speech and another. 

No paradigms (declensions or conjugations) of inflected forms are offered here: a student who needs to consult such tables may find them in any formal English grammar. 

Common faults in the use of inflectional forms are set forth in the following paragraphs, and in exercises based for the most part on the actual writing of college students. 

Case 

Confusion arises between uses of the nominative case and the objective case of pronouns (English no longer dis tinguishes by inflection between nominative and objective nouns). Some nominative forms are he, she, they, we, who, whoever; some objective forms are him, her, them, us, whom, whomever.

The nominative is used properly in the following constructions (positions, functions): 

1. The subject of a finite verb. 

(a) He and I (not him and me) are friends. (b) She and Jane (not her and Jane) left a moment ago. (c) They (not them) are the best one can buy. (d) None are so wise as they (not them). (e) Whoever (not whom or whomever) steals my purse steals trash. (f) I am willing to take whoever (not whomever) can do the work. 

2. Predicate nominative (subjective complement, a noun or pronoun that completes the predicate and refers to the

Words subject) following an intransitive verb (a verb which does not express action terminating on an object). (a) I am he (not him) that liveth. (b) It was she (not her) that helped me. (c) It is they (not them) that have conquered. (d) Who¹ (not whom) were you at home? 

3. Subject of a participle in an absolute phrase (an independent phrase consisting of a noun or pronoun modified by a participle, or verbal adjective). (a) I (not me) being his father, he must respect me. (b) They (not them) having failed, we must take up the task.

The objective case appears in the following constructions: 

1. Object complement (that upon which action terminates) of a finite verb (a verb which is used in making a statement). (a) Send him and me 2 (not he and I). (b) Leave Jane and her (not Jane and she) at home. (c) Call them (not they) and their friends. (d) I cannot decide whom (not who) I ought to send. (e) Send whomever (not whoever) you choose. 

2. Complement of an intransitive verb, referring to the object complement. 1 3 (a) We thought the intruder to be him (not he). (b) We believed our enemies to be them (not they). 

Analyze You were who? 

2 The student will do well to note that a mistake is more likely to occur where a pronoun is coupled with a noun or another pronoun in the same construction than where the pronoun stands alone. One's ear will warn against saying, "Send he" or "Send I," whereas one may be aware of no absurdity in "Send him and I"-or even in "Send he and I." 

8 Him refers to intruder, which is the object complement of thought.

College Rhetoric 3. Principal term of prepositional phrase. 

(a) Our father left his wealth to him and me (not he and I). (b) My heart goes out to them (not they) of little hope. (c) They seemed sorry for us (not we) children. (d) He seemed worried about John and me (not I, or myself).¹ (e) Award the prize to whomever (not whoever) you esteem worthy.

The subject of a gerund (verbal noun), when expressed as such, should be in the possessive case: 

I do not approve of his, John's (not him, John), remaining in the room while the matter is discussed. 

It will be seen that his or John's does not denote possession, exactly. The word is introduced to identify the subject of remaining-to show who is or is not to remain. The best usage prefers the possessive case rather than the objective in such construction. 

We wondered about their (not them) having accepted the invitation. 

4 Few speakers would say "worried about I," yet many are heard to say "worried about John and I." Again, shaken by doubt as to whether I or me would be correct, an imperfectly trained speaker may evade the issue by saying "myself," which may be either nominative or objective. But myself is properly intensive (I did it myself) or reflexive (I hurt myself), and is therefore not quite the best word for the place. The right word is me.

Number 

English distinguishes between the singular and the plural number of nouns (boy, boys; child, children; brother, brothers, brethren; man, men), verbs (is, are; was, were; comes, come; has, have), pronouns (he, they; his, theirs; him, them; I, we; me, us), and pronominal adjectives (this, these; that, those).

The law of number is one of agreement; that is, the number of the verb must be identical with that of its subject, the number of the pronoun with that of its antecedent. and the number of the pronominal adjective with that of the noun it modifies. Illustrations follow: 

1. A large variety of activities is (not are) afforded. 

The subject is variety, not activities. 

2. There have (not has) been only fifteen that have (not has) entered college.

The subject of the principal clause is fifteen, not there. The subject of the subordinate (adjective) clause is that, and that is plural because its antecedent (the word to which it refers), fifteen, is plural. 

3. There are (not is) only three of us. 

The subject is three, not there. 

4. Each one of us has (not have) a different aspiration. 

The subject is each, not us; each is singular. 

5. Every one of us owes it to himself (not owe it to ourselves).

It should be noted that the expressions anybody, everybody, somebody, nobody, one, any one, every one, some one, no one, each, any, either, neither are all singular. Each of them should be followed by a singular verb, and the subsequent pronoun should be singular: 

1. Everybody has his own (not have their own) opinion. 

2. Some one has left his (not have left their) coat. 

3. Neither of them has (not have) any right to complain.

College Rhetoric 

The importance of the agreement of pronoun with antecedent (the word to which the pronoun refers) has been emphasized on an earlier page, under reference. 

I do not like this (not these) kind of person (not a person).

Kind is singular, and the adjective modifying it should be singular. The word person is generic (almost, though not quite, the same as plural). Prefixing the article a (cognate with the adjective one) makes the idea individual, and hence destroys the generic sense. We cannot have a kind of one person. The distinction is a delicate one, which the best usage approves and careful speakers and writers generally observe.

Similarly, one should say best of all rather than best of any, because there can be no choice of only one object. 

This is the general feeling in regard (not regards) to racetrack gambling.

Pluralizing the abstract noun (regard is an abstract noun) should be done cautiously. "In regards to . . . "in respects to . • • 

." is like which one seldom or never hears.

Folk, a collective noun, primarily means tribe, nation. 

CORRECT: Germans as a class are (an) intelligent folk. 

To use the plural folks as the equivalent of people, relatives is colloquial. 

LOOSE: He was good to his folks.

Tense 

Tense is a modification of verbs which enables them to represent time in different phases. The tenses of English verbs are present, past, future, present perfect, past perfect, and future perfect. Perfect means completed; hence present perfect means completed-at-the-present-time, and so forth. For illustration:

PRESENT: I see 

PAST: 

I saw

FUTURE: I shall see PRESENT PERFECT: I have seen 

PAST PERFECT: I had seen

FUTURE PERFECT: I shall have seen PRINCIPAL PARTS 

The so-called "principal parts" of a verb are the basic forms, given which one is able to construct the entire system of verb forms. These parts are commonly the present indicative or infinitive, the past indicative, and the past participle.

A "regular" verb forms its past indicative and past participle by the addition of a dental suffix (-ed, -d, -t) to the present infinitive: deem deemed deemed breathe breathed breathed burn burned burned (burnt) (burnt) The "irregular" verb forms its past indicative and past participle by some other method of change, or by no change at all: rise rose risen hold held held set set set One should not invent a regular past indicative or participle for a verb whose past is properly irregular:

College Rhetoric 

1. I knew (not knowed) it would happen. 

2. The boiler burst (not bursted). 

3. The news was broadcast (not broadcasted). 

On the other hand, one should use the proper (regular) 

past of a regular verb: 

1. The attorney pleaded (not plead or pled) the case. 

2. The housewife heated (not het) the water. 

And one should refrain from using an unauthorized past form of an irregular verb, as et for ate, the past of eat. 

SIMPLE AND PERFECT TENSE FORMS 

Careful speakers and writers make a logical discrimination between the simple present, past, and future tenses and the perfect tenses corresponding respectively to them: 

1. I have just come (not just came) from the scene. 

2. When my time came to register, I found to my astonishment that my high-school credits had not been mailed (not were not mailed) to the registrar of the University as my high-school principal had promised (not promised). 

3. I shall not be happy till I shall have written (not write) Finis to this episode. 

May and Might 

Uses of may and might should be carefully distinguished.

The historical distinction of present and past is preserved in perfect tenses: 

CORRECT: He may have taken the wrong road. 

CORRECT: He might have saved himself if he had known.

When used without the auxiliary have, may suggests a fair degree of likelihood: It may rain or it may not rain, the chances being about equal. On the other hand, might sug-

Words 

81 gests a less degree of likelihood, being usually followed by a contingent (provisional) clause introduced by if: 

He might be induced to run, if the vote for his nomination were unanimous.

In present expressions of desire, may is preferable to might: 

1. We hope he may (not might) succeed. 

2. We pray that this may (not might) come to pass.

But in past expressions, might is correct: 

1. We hoped he might succeed. 

2. We prayed that this might come to pass.

SEQUENCE OF TENSES 

When a verb of mental action in the past tense takes a noun clause as its object, the predicate verb of the noun clause should be in the past or the past perfect tense: 

1. He thought (believed, decided, said) he would do the work. 

2. He thought (believed, said) he had done (not has done) the work.

When, however, the idea expressed in the noun clause is universal, and therefore timeless, the present tense may be used:

He declared (believed, taught) that the earth is round. 

HISTORICAL PRESENT 

In vivid narration, the present tense may be used consistently in the sense of the past.

Suddenly he sees his opportunity, rushes forward, and begins frantically to batter his way out at the point where his friends have made an opening.

College Rhetoric MIXED TENSES 

But when one has begun a narrative sentence in the present, one should not lapse into the past. The following is loose: 

The fullback seizes the ball, darts out to the side, and threw it just after he had been tackled.

TENSE INFLUENCED BY ATTRACTION 

The tense of an infinitive may be "attracted" into that of a finite verb upon which it depends. The resulting construction may be illogical and awkward:

QUESTIONABLE: I should have liked to have gone. 

IMPROVED: I should have liked to go. I should like to have gone.

Person 

A finite verb must agree with its subject in person:

SINGULAR FIRST PERSON: I am, was 

SECOND PERSON: you are, were 

THIRD PERSON: he, she, it is, was

PLURAL 

we are, were you are, were they are, were 

Such expressions as "he am," "you am," "I is,” and “you is" are heard, perhaps, only among illiterate folk. But "you was, ""was you?" and "wasn't you?" and even "aren't

I?" are common in the conversation of college students. 

Use the following expressions: 

1. Weren't you (or were you not) in my class? (Not 

Wasn't you in my class?) 

2. Where were (not was) you when I called? 

3. I am right, am I not (not aren't I)? 

Shall and Will: Should and Would

Distinction in the use of the above forms is observed by careful writers. Difficulty arises chiefly in the selection of the proper verb to go with I or we, the pronouns of the first person. The rule is: use will and would with the first personal pronoun only when the idea of determination or willingness is present (that is, only in cases in which the human will is active). The following are correct: 

1. I will (am determined to) do this, cost what it may. 

2. I will (am willing to) do my part if you will do yours. 

3. I would (have the will to) do it if I could. 

4. We will (are determined) to carry this measure through. 

5. We will (are willing to) do what you ask. 

6. We would (have the will to) accept the charge if we had the means.

College Rhetoric 

In cases in which the action of the will is not involved, prefer shall and should with I and we: 

1. I shall (not will) be in my room till eight o'clock. 

2. We shall (not will) meet at the station. 

3. I should (not would) like to see you soon. 

4. We should (not would) be happy to have you come. 

In a similar sense, shall may be used with the second person in a question, when an answer in the first person is anticipated: 

QUESTION: Shall you be at the party? 

ANSWER: I believe I shall. 

In most cases, shall used with the second and third persons properly denotes the speaker's determination: 

You (he, they) shall do as I say. 

Should with the second and third persons denotes obligation: 

You (he, they) should be more careful.

85 Mode While the forms of the subjunctive mode are not so frequently employed now as formerly, and while they are not seen so commonly in prose as in poetry, dignified speech requires them in the expression of certain ideas. 

The essential distinction between indicative and subjunctive is, that the former expresses factual ideas, and the latter, conditional or dependent mental conceptions. "I am the King" (indicative) states a fact. "If I were the 

King" (subjunctive) presents a condition contrary to fact but held in the mind. Similarly, in "I wish I were the 

King" wish is indicative; and were is subjunctive, expressing the thing desired.

INDICATIVE 

1. He is guilty. (Statement of fact.) 

2. I believe that he is guilty. (Judgment concerning fact.) 

3. He must have seen the accident if he was here. (Condition supposing a fact.)

SUBJUNCTIVE 

1. He would not be here if he were guilty. (Supposition contrary to fact.) 

2. It is the sentence of the court that he be fined. (Judgment involving determination.) 

3. I wish I were free. (Desire for something conceived as possible.)

An awkward substitution is that of would be for were in a clause of supposition contrary to fact: 

CRUDE: If I would be he, I should go home. 

IMPROVED: If I were he, I should go home.

Similar substitutions are those of will, can, would, could for may, might in clauses denoting purpose. May and might here represent old subjunctive forms:

College Rhetoric 

1. I am working hard in order that I may (not can, will) earn money for my education. 

2. He worked hard in order that he might (not could, would) get enough money to pay his tuition fees. 

Would have for had (an old subjunctive) is equally bad: 

If I had (not would have) seen him, I should have called out.

The so-called "modal" auxiliaries can and could are sometimes carelessly used for may and might in expressions of permission. 

CORRECT: May (not can) I use your pen? 

CORRECT: He asked whether he might (not could) go.

Words 

Voice is a modification of the transitive verb which denotes whether the subject is acting or being acted upon. 

ACTIVE: I see the star. 

PASSIVE: The star was seen by multitudes.

AWKWARD PASSIVE 

An awkward effect is produced by a forced, unnatural use of the passive.

AWKWARD: The task is as difficult if tried to be done by oneself. 

IMPROVED: The task is as difficult if one tries to do it oneself.

An equally awkward sentence is the following: 

Before selecting a book titles should be read carefully. 

Here the gerund (verbal noun) selecting, which is in the active voice, has no expressed subject-only an implied one. 

The writer would do well to have both the gerund and the finite (predicate) verb in the active voice, and let the same word serve as the subject for both:

Before selecting a book one should read titles carefully. 

A WORD OF GENERAL ADVICE ABOUT VOICE 

Where the meaning admits of choice, one should in the majority of cases prefer an active construction. The passive is feebler, often less vivid, less positive. It might be well for the student to look through some paragraphs of his own writing with a view to strengthening the effects by changing passive to active forms.

Words 

89 

Comparison 

Regular comparison of adjectives and adverbs is made by adding to the positive the suffixes -er and -est to form the comparative and superlative, respectively. 

POSITIVE 

long

COMPARATIVE 

longer 

SUPERLATIVE 

longest soonest soon sooner 

The comparison of an adjective or adverb of several syllables may be indicated by prefixing the adverbs more and most:

POSITIVE 

competent delicately

COMPARATIVE SUPERLATIVE 

more competent most competent more delicately most delicately

The student would do well to consult textbooks on grammar for adjectives and adverbs of irregular comparison, such as good, better, best; ill, worse, worst.

ERRORS IN COMPARISON 

One should avoid confusion of comparative and superlative: 

1. He is the older (not oldest) of the two. 

2. He is the oldest (not older) of the three.

One should be careful not to confuse the comparative or superlative of an adjective with the comparative or superlative of the corresponding adverb: 

1. He does it more easily (not easier) than I. 

2. Intelligent colts are most easily (not easiest) trained. 

The particle than is, by the majority of careful speakers and writers, made to follow only words in the comparative degree:

College Rhetoric 

1. He is older than his brother. 

2. They came sooner than we expected. 

BAD: He is the most vicious than all the rest. 

IMPROVED: He is the most vicious of them all. He is more vicious than any of the others. 

Rather, other, and else (which means other) are, however, properly followed by than: 

1. He would rather go than not. 

2. I have no argument other than the one I have stated. 

3. This is nothing else than sheer folly. 

Different is not in the comparative degree, though it suggests divergence, which is a sort of comparison. Most students of form are careful to avoid different than, preferring different from: 

1. His reasons are different from (not than) mine. 

2. The case is different from what (not than) I expected.

Words Mob English 

BARBARISMS 

The Greeks called foreigners hoi barbaroi in imitation of the latter's speech sounds. Outsiders were men whose tongues and lips said "bah-bah" instead of articulated and recognized syllables and words. To this day the word barbarism is used to denote rudeness of manners and ignorance of art, learning, and literature. More particularly, in speech, a barbarism is an offense against purity of diction; it is an expression that is not accepted as standard usage of a particular language. "Ain't" for am not, are not, is not, a form in common use among the illiterate, is seldom admitted, except by way of a joke or imitation, into the speech of cultured folk. "Orate" and "enthuse" are other barbarisms.

Solecisms 

A deviation from the idiom of a language or from the rules of syntax is a solecism: he don't (for doesn't); hadn't ought (for ought not).

Provincialisms 

The speech of the provinces (places remote from the mother city, the metropolis) is likely to develop forms indigenous to the remote section. He taken (for took) the money; he written (for wrote) the letter, are not infrequently heard in certain rustic American neighborhoods.

Slang The word slang (related to the verb sling) dates in English only from the eighteenth century, and is thought

College Rhetoric to be associated with two foreign phrases, meaning "sling the jaw" (to use abusive language, to "slang") and "slangword" (an insulting word, a new word that has no just allusion, a slung-word). The term came to be applied to the speech of traveling showmen, thieves, and beggars. It is used now also in reference to the speech of any special group, as of craftsmen or of a particular profession. Snitch (inform) and moll (a girl, or gangster's mistress) are underworld slang; drag (a woman one takes to a dance), blind date (a person of the opposite sex with whom one makes a social engagement without previous acquaintance), and go nerts (become insane) are college society slang; and to major in and to minor in (to make a major, minor study of) are student-professor-dean-registrar slang, perhaps seldom recognized as slang at all.

Words 

Colloquialisms 

Colloquial expressions are forms which, though unobjectionable in the intimacy of conversation, would be excluded by the more careful from formal writing. They include many abbreviations, and phrases bordering upon slang. "He's an awfully good sort," "He's a tough proposition," "I don't get you" are colloquial. More loosely, the term colloquial may be applied to all forms of speech (including barbarisms, solecisms, and slang phrases of all sorts) which have no place in formal composition. 

COLLOQUIALISMS, PROVINCIALISMS, AND UNAUTHORIZED FORMS 

Note. Where the word wrong is used in respect to a colloquialism, in the following list, the term should be interpreted as meaning inadvisable as an expression to be used in formal composition. ad. Colloquial for advertisement. ain't. Webster labels this as colloquial or illiterate. Its use for am not, is not, or are not is condemned. allow. This verb is frequently used by careless speakers and writers to mean grant or believe. Do not say, I allowed he ought to come, or 

I allowed (or 'lowed) she was with you. alright. There is no such form as alright. One must either use all right or find another term. anything. Anything is colloquially used to qualify a noun or pronoun. 

WRONG: I am not anything of a dancer. anywheres, somewheres, everywheres. There is no authority for the use of these forms. argufy. A colloquial or dialectic form. It should not be used for argue, debate with, or prove. attackted. A vulgarism for attacked. auto. Colloquially used for automobile. It should not be used in formal writing. awful. Awful should not be used for very, extremely, remarkably. 

Consult the dictionary. 

WRONG: The cake is awful good.

College Rhetoric badly. Colloquially used for severely. To say, The man was badly punished is to indicate that the punishment was not well administered. beat. Colloquial when used for win the victory and punish, torture, or cheat.

QUESTIONABLE: Yale beat Harvard this year. He will beat up on you for doing that. He beat me out of ten dollars. beat. Colloquial or slang as a noun: I never knew his beat. bike. Colloquial for bicycle. borned. The past participle of bear is born or borne. There is no form borned. boss, bossy, bossism. All three forms are colloquial or slang. boughten. A dialectic form in England, colloquial in the United States. brainy. Colloquial for intelligent. brash, brashy. Colloquial when used to mean saucy. broadcasted. An unauthorized form. The principal parts of broadcast are broadcast, broadcast, broadcast. bulldoze, bulldozer. Both forms are colloquial. bunch. Colloquial when used to designate a group or party. bust, busted, bursted. None of these forms exists in good English. 

The principal parts of the verb burst are burst, burst, burst. burgle, burglarize. Both forms are colloquially used to mean commit a burglary. chaw. Chaw and chawed are now regarded as vulgarisms when used for chew and chewed. chunky. Chunk and chunky are labeled colloquial forms in Webster's 

New International Dictionary. 

WRONG: Give me a big chunk of beef. 

WRONG: He is heavy set and chunky. claim. Colloquial when used for profess, assert, declare. 

WRONG: He claims I saw you. 

WRONG: He claims that he is innocent. clean. Colloquially used for completely, fully. 

WRONG: I think he is clean wrong. clumb. No such form exists. 

WRONG: He clumb over our fence.

Words come to, came to. Provincial for become or became conscious. 

WRONG: He came to after the doctor arrived. complected. This form is often incorrectly used for complexioned. 

The word complect means plait together, interweave, and is not related to the word complexion. The adjective complexioned is formed by adding the participial ending -ed to the noun complexion. contraption, contraptious. Both are colloquial forms. Contraption should not be used to mean contrivance or new-fangled device. cussed, cussedly, cussedness. These forms are to be considered vulgar. 

VULGAR: He cussed the proprietor. date. Colloquial for engagement or escort, or as a verb for make an engagement. 

WRONG: I have two dates for Sunday night. Let me date you for Monday. My date won't come before nine o'clock. deal. Colloquial for business transaction or "treatment" in general. 

WRONG: We promise you a new deal now. dig. Colloquially used for study laboriously or ploddingly. 

WRONG: He has to dig for what he gets. diner. Colloquial for dining car. 

directly. PROVINCIAL: I shall go directly.

WRONG: He was done out before we started. down. Archaic or colloquial in the verb use overthrow.

WRONG: Firstly, I shall discuss the main topic.

109 

Trite Phrases 

A stereotype is, literally, a cast of reading matter in type metal which can be inserted in a page or column and run as a mass, without the labor of individual typesetting. 

Figuratively, a stereotyped saying is one whose words are run together in lines or blocks. "After all, the children of today are the citizens and voters of tomorrow"; "tired but happy"; and "making the world a better place to live in," are stereotypes. That is, they are labor-savers. The words do not have to be sought out as words, but come to hand in blocks, or ready-made molds. The French cliché has the same meaning, literal and figurative, as stereotype. Other terms for the overworked, ready-to-order expressions are trite or hackneyed phrases, forms, or formulas.

Not infrequently the cliché represents a settled, popular, comfortable opinion or social attitude. One avoids the agony of thinking by quoting the phrase, perhaps even mouthing it out importantly, as if he had invented it: "A woman's place is in the home"; "You can't change human nature"; "The exception proves the rule." The last of these is peculiarly vicious, in that it is used to make the manifestly impossible statement that the exception demonstrates the rule; whereas the word prove is used here in its original sense of probe; and the aphorism properly means, the exception tests the rule-that is, if the rule can be shown to explain the apparent exception, it may be valid. 

The habitual use of trite word combinations is, then, the indication of a timid, conventional mind and a lazy tongue. 

In the course of a dull address of thirty minutes' duration, a speaker was heard to say "worthwhile" fourteen times and "worthwhileness" twice. Worth while, a perfectly vigorous and legitimate phrase, originally meant and ought still to mean worth the time it takes to do a thing: It will be worth your while (the while of an hour or a day) to visit Congress,

College Rhetoric the prize-fight, or Oberammergau. But in the speech of the sluggish of mind it comes to be the hazy equivalent of "good" or "OK." Worthwhile, or worth while, in its popular sense, may be differentiated into excellent, valuable, vital, interesting, choice, valid, convincing, fine, acceptable, alluring, irresistible, thrilling. Worthwhileness, one would suppose, must mean value, importance, necessity. 

If, then, one be determined to deliver one's hearers or readers from the dreary monotony of the deadening phrase, let one resolutely eschew "Did you ever stop to think?" "physically, morally, and spiritually," "extra-curricular activities," "this materialistic age," "contacts," "objectives,' "for the simple reason that," and "thus we find.”

The Student's Attitude Toward Colloquial Forms 

There is no discredit in the moderate use of expressive slang in the intimacy of familiar colloquy. Educated persons know that keen, telling metaphors of slang origin may be destined to become vital parts of the accepted and authentic language; and they employ these figures without apology. They may even go farther, and for homely or humorous effect lapse into illiterate solecisms, doing this with the full knowledge of the absurd character of their speech. But it is one thing for an occasional slang expression to be graded up to the level of the accepted phrase, and quite another for language as a whole to be allowed to sink to the level of all slang, silly as well as brilliant, and of the loosest and crudest syntax. As all men and women of culture ought to be in a conspiracy to improve human standards of justice, character, and achievement, so they ought to conspire to improve (not degrade) standards of breeding, art, and language.

But there are young writers, and perhaps a number of mature and experienced authors, who make a boast of indifference or hostility to form, as if form were identical with formality, stiffness, snobbishness, affectation. "I want my writing," one may exclaim, "to be like life itself, to imitate the accents of actual speech, to thrill with the heartthrob of deep human emotion!" If colloquialism, stale formulas, and bad grammar would actually simulate life at its keenest and reveal the tragic emotions, the suggested mode might justify one's adopting it. But what the reader is more likely to get from these is the stammering, the uncertainty, and the weak logic of clacking housewives and soap-box whittlers.

We would advise the student writer thus: The wit of slang is the wit of the mob; let your wit be your own.

The wisdom of "wise saws and modern instances" is the

College Rhetoric mob's wisdom (not always wise, sometimes foolish, likely to be partial, not seldom mischievous); do not echo it; let your brilliancy be your own-make your own discoveries.

By all means be democratic; but sympathy with "common humanity" never requires that one stoop to such vices of language as correspond to drinking quantities of bad gin and beating one's wife. No amount of equalization of privilege and putting education within the reach of the masses will make “him and me done it," "lay off ut,” and “hadn't ought to a-never a-went" dignified, witty, and forceful. 

Make a compact with yourself to speak and write clear, well-considered, original phrases and sentences, observing nice distinctions of syntax, structure, and word selectionand observe how difficult it is to keep the covenant for one day. 

Superfluous Words

Economy of words often results in economy of a reader's energy; multiplicity of words makes for weakness or haziness of idea.

The student came of a family whose finances were limited to a moderate income. 

This may be reduced profitably to The student came of a family of moderate income. Consider 

Be sure that the book is worthwhile reading. 

Delete while and note the improvement.

POOR: Canton was a very unusually pretty town. 

IMPROVED: Canton was an unusually pretty town.

Very is the most consistently overworked word in the language. The average literary style might be improved by the elimination of four-fifths of the very's.

Words He planned to be a doctor, lawyer, or mechanic, as the case might be. 

Cut off the last five words.

One had a limited choice from which to choose from. 

Cut off the last five words.

Try suppressing such expressions as and so forth, et cetera, etc., and many other things. These phrases put upon the reader the labor of supplying details. When you have gone as far as you think desirable in making a list of particulars, stop. Say, Busses and automobiles (not Busses, automobiles, etc.) supply transportation. Tautology

Tautology is a repetition of meaning in different words. 

In He became despondent and down-hearted, the adjectives have practically the same meaning. Again, IMPROVED: He became despondent.

It is not hard to visualize the resulting confusion and chaos.

Chaos includes confusion. Omit confusion and. 

But he was paying no attention, however, to the region itself.

But and however are not both needed. 

I appeal to each and every voter. 

Each and every is a hackneyed and tautological phrase.

Its retention in popular speech may be due to its alliterative quality. In this respect it is not unlike time and tide, in which tide originally meant time (a meaning still observable

College Rhetoric in Christmastide, noontide). Every voter is simpler and more effective than each and every voter.
