Quotation Marks 

1. Quotation marks are used to inclose a direct (but not an indirect) quotation: (a) The watchword then became, "Taxation without representation is tyranny." (b) "Good words butter no parsnips" is a proverb. (c) "I am sorry to trouble you," said Fred, "but you are the only friend I can consult." (d) He said that he was much annoyed by the order. 

2. A series of sentences constituting an unbroken quotation requires but one pair of quotation marks: "All women become like their mothers. That is their tragedy. No man does. That is his." 

-OSCAR WILDE. 

3. A long quotation of more than one paragraph should have quotation marks at the beginning of each paragraph and at the end of the last paragraph: 

4. Names of poems, short stories, magazine articles, and titles of essays or book divisions are usually inclosed in quotation marks. It is not uncommon, however, to see these set in italics when printed, especially when names may be recognized as independent titles. Names of important plays are usually italicized rather than quoted:

College Rhetoric 

(a) The death of a lovely woman is the theme of nearly all of Poe's best poems: "The Raven," "Annabel 

Lee," "Ulalume," "Lenore," and "The Sleeper." (b) "Hark, Hark, the Lark" is one of the finest songs in 

Shakespeare's Cymbeline. 

(c) Have you read Walt Whitman's "Song of Myself" in his Leaves of Grass? 

5. Single quotation marks (‘. . .') are used for a quotation occurring within a direct quotation: "I believe," said he, "Some falls are means the happier to arise.'"

Note. A formal question or a resolution for a debate or a title of an original theme should NOT be enclosed in quotation marks. (a) The question is, Where do the starlings die? (b) The question to be debated is, Should the government own and operate the railroads? 

(c) I shall write a theme on Learning How to Study. 

When other punctuation comes at the end of a quotation which is a complete sentence, it should be placed inside the quotation marks: 

QUESTIONABLE: "May I ask", she said, "what you plan to wear to our dance"? 

CORRECT: "May I ask," she said, "what you plan to wear to our dance?"

The Interrogation Mark 

1. The interrogation, or question, mark is used at the end of a direct (but not an indirect) question, which may or may not be a complete sentence: (a) Is this the right word? (b) His first question was, "When do we eat?" (c) Must I budge? Must I observe you? Must I stand and crouch under your testy humor? (d) He asked what I wanted.

Punctuation 179 A series of short questions may be regarded as one sentence. The interrogations may be elliptical clauses connected by a coördinating conjunction. The capitalization of the first word of the entire sentence is sufficient: 

(a) What is the population of London? of Paris? 

(b) Where are your towers? and your servants? and your horses?

Note. The interrogation mark should not be used to end an indirect question: (a) I wonder why you came. (b) Learn what he wants. (c) I know how old she is and how much she weighs. 

2. The interrogation mark is used to show doubt as to the correctness of a date, of a figure, or of an assertion: 

Boethius, the great philosopher, lived in Rome 475?-525?

But the interrogation mark should not be used loosely to punctuate ironical expressions or assertions: 

CRUDE: (a) He weighs at least a ton. (?) (b) He drinks gallons (?) of coffee when I make it. (c) I am dieting (?), and you may soon behold me sylphlike.

One should never use more than one question mark to close an interrogation: 

CRUDE: And do I like to swim???

Any punctuation mark indicating pause is omitted when it coincides with the question mark within or at the end of a sentence: 

WRONG: "Do you really love one another, children, ?", asked the good old man. 

CORRECT: "Do you really love one another, children?" asked the good old man.

College Rhetoric 

WRONG: His first question was, "Is there any place like home?". 

CORRECT: His first question was, "Is there any place like home?"

The Exclamation Mark 

The exclamation mark is used to end an ejaculation, a positive command, or any other expression which shows strong feeling: 

1. Begone! Look! Alas! 

2. Stop that radio this minute! 

3. Oh, I shall die of grief! *Note. An exclamation mark may follow Oh when the stress of feeling is put on the interjection: 

Oh! mercy, heaven, look not so fierce on me!

The exclamation mark replaces other marks of punctuation which normally would come at the end of the exclamation: 

A horse! a horse! my kingdom for a horse!

Since the reader must depend upon the punctuation to indicate strong feeling, the writer should use the exclamation mark with caution. One should not use the mark to label his humor or satire. It is bad taste to tell a reader when to laugh or to call attention to one's satirical remarks: 

CRUDE: 1. And did I learn what the teacher thinks of 

Ha! Ha! me! 

2. You should see me in a red dress! Wow!

Extravagance is generally bad form. One would do well to make it a rule never to use more than one exclamation mark to end his ejaculation: 

CRUDE: 1. And what a line he gave us!!! 

2. How stupid of me!!!!

Punctuation 

181 

The Dash 

1. The dash is used to set off a parenthetical expression which is felt to be tacked on or added as a sort of summary or afterthought: (a) James abdicated and went to France-a voluntary act. (b) Speak softly and carry a big stick-you will go far.2 (c) Three things are necessary for success-first, backbone; second, backbone; third, backbone. (d) 

Her voice was ever soft. 

Gentle, and low-an excellent thing in woman. (e) Kings are like stars-they rise and set. (f) To be or not to be that is the question. (g) I believe that this matter of specialization is already ³—and as the years pass will become more and more the keynote of success. (h) Energy, system, perseverance these are the great components of success in a young man's life. (i) God sees all souls naked-stripped of their material coverings, husks, and impurities. 

2. The dash is used before an author's name when it is put at the close of a quotation: 

Avoid excess in everything. 

-SOCRATES. 

3.

The dash is used to show that a sentence or clause is incomplete: "I bear nobody ill-will, thank God, and Mr. Wrench saved me in the pleurisy, but he'd better have let me die-if-if-” 

4. The dash may be used after the comma to denote longer pause and more emphasis than that usually indicated by either the comma or the dash alone: 

2 The colon may be used here instead of the dash. 

3 Parentheses may be used here.

College Rhetoric 

(a) (b) While stands the Coliseum, Rome shall stand; 

When falls the Coliseum, Rome shall fall; 

And when Rome falls,-the World. 

Men are four:

He who knows, and knows he knows,- 

He is wise follow him.

He who knows, and knows not he knows,- 

He is asleep-wake him.

He who knows not, and knows not he knows not,— 

He is a fool-shun him.

He who knows not, and knows he knows not,- 

He is a child-teach him." 

-ARABIAN PROVERB. 

The dash replaces the period at the end of a sentence when normally both would come together, but the dash does not replace the exclamation mark or the question mark.

The dash should not be used carelessly for the period, the semicolon, the colon, or brackets. Too many young writers use the dash as a last resort when they do not know what mark of punctuation to use and choose the dash as being the safest guess. The different uses of the dash are not many, and every student should master them. Brackets 

Brackets must not be confused with parentheses either in form or in use. Brackets are used in editing the writing of another person. 

1. Editors use brackets to inclose their own comments or conjectures as to parts missing or illegible in manuscripts which they are editing: 

(a) From Gummere's Handbook of Poetics: 

"Now what is such a song? It is The Epic [Epic, from Greek epos, a 'word,' then a 'narration': Cf. Saga, which equals 'something said.'] 

---

Punctuation 

183 (b) He [Friar Bacon] is regarded as the first English philosopher. (c) The affect [effect] will becom [e] more noticeable as the[y] grow older. (d) And after all, who is to determine what I should give to the community chest. [?] 

The insertion of brackets in a text does not in any way modify the punctuation, unless the inclosure suggests a correction. Brackets may come after the period at the end of a sentence or at any place within the sentence without affecting the original marks of punctuation. 

Parentheses 

1. Parentheses are used to inclose a word, a phrase, or a sentence that constitutes an interruption to the thought of the sentence or the text in general: (a) Now that I have succeeded (if succeeded I have) in persuading you to take a vacation, I should like to invite you to visit us. (b) Elizabeth enjoyed a wonderful calm (some short gusts of insurrection at the beginning being excepted) nearly forty years. (c) The merchant's object in all his dealings must be (the public believe) to get as much for himself and leave as little to his neighbor (or customer) as possible. (d) Unless you give at least forty-five minutes each night to careful, fatiguing reflection (it is an awful bore at first) upon what you are reading, your time is chiefly wasted. 

2. Parentheses are used to inclose a figure which verifies what has been written out in the word or phrase preceding it: (a) I shall inclose ten dollars ($10.00). (b) We sold three thousand sixty-two (3,062) Christmas stamps.

College Rhetoric 

3. Parentheses are used to inclose numbers, letters, or other symbols that are used in numbering items in a list of illustrations:

Parse in the following sentences (1) the verbs, (2) the adverbs, (3) pronouns, (4) nouns: (a) He swam clear across the stream. (b) He ought to study harder. (c) 

The teacher gave Mary the prize. 

4. Parentheses are used to inclose notes, asides, references, and acknowledgments that are better included in the text than placed as footnotes: (a) No classic themes have ever been revived with such power as in Marlowe's (and Chapman's) Hero and Leander. (b) We can readily understand the popularity of the Poema Morale (c. 1170), a good example of a reflective poem. (c) Shakespeare's famous passage about sleep (Macbeth II, ii) has a number of metaphors, combined in the figure of Variation. (d) The white rose of York (I Henry VI, II, ii) is "this pale and angry rose.' 

(e) The great art in prose or verse is to leave on the reader's mind the most distinct and sharp impression possible (cf. H. Spencer's The Philosophy of Style). 

5. Parentheses are used to inclose the translation of a foreign word or phrase:

No chadir (Hebrew school) shall be held in a room used for cooking and sleeping.

Note.-Parentheses should not be used to denote deletion or cancellation. Nor should they be used for brackets to inclose an editor's marks or comments.

The singular form, parenthesis, is used to denote (1) the parenthetical expression or (2) one of the curves or (3) both of the curves. The plural form, parentheses, is preferred, however, in referring to the curves as a pair. Both of the following expressions are correct:

Punctuation 

185 (a) Inclose in parenthesis. (b) Inclose in parentheses. 

Dots and Asterisks

Dots (a series of periods . . .) and asterisks (stars ***) 

are used to indicate an omission in a quoted text. An omission within a line of prose or verse is usually indicated by a series of three dots or asterisks, though a greater number may be used to fill out the line if necessary. To show an omission of one or more complete lines of verse, a full line of dots or asterisks is used. Both the dots and the asterisks should be regarded as parts of the quotations, and should be inclosed within the quotation marks. Usage seems to favor the dots to show ellipses of words or lines, and the asterisks to indicate the omission of stanzas or paragraphs, though this practice is by no means fixed. 

Some typists use the asterisks to show note references, the number of asterisks corresponding to the number of the reference. The use of numerals, 1, 2, 3, etc. for identifying note references is to be preferred, however, especially if one must put more than one note on a page. 

The following excerpts are quoted from standard texts to show the use of dots and asterisks: 

1. The law of worthy life is . . . fundamentally the law of strife. . . It is only through labor and painful effort, by grim and resolute courage, that we move on to better things. 

2. Instead of saying, "Sorry, I can't see you, old chap, but I have to run off to the tennis club," you must say, "... but I have to work." 3. 4. "They hadna been a week from her 

A week but barely ane They hadna been a week from her 

A week but barely three . . . 'Awa, awa, ye ill woman, Nae ill woman for me;

College Rhetoric 

What ye hae done to Earl Richard,

Sae wad ye do to mee.' * * * * * * * * 'O there's a bird intil your bowir 

That sings saw sad and sweet; 

O there's a bird intil your bour 

Kept me fra my nicht's sleep.' * * * * * * * And she sware by the grass so greene, Saw did she by the corn, That she had not seen Earl Richard 

Sen yesterday at morn. 5. 

-Young Hunting.¹ 

Thou knowest, though Thy universe is broad,

Two little tears suffice to cover all. 

Note. The apostrophe and the dash (see p. 181) are also used to denote omissions: 

1. Joe O'Conner will call for you. 2. I shall see you at Mrs. H-r's (H . . . r's or H***r's) on Monday at eight o'clock.

The Apostrophe 

1. The apostrophe and s are added to a singular noun to denote possession: (a) John's book was stolen. (b) The day's work is now over. (c) He lives on Jones's farm. (d) I enjoyed your reading of Burns's poems.

The s is sometimes omitted if the singular form ends in a sibilant and the repeated sibilant would be difficult to pronounce, especially if the word following begins with a sibilant: (a) For conscience' sake, do apologize. 

4 From English and Scottish Popular Ballads, Houghton Mifflin Co., New York, 1904, p. 142.

Punctuation 

187 (b) Listen to Charles' story of the flood. (c) We enjoy reading Dickens' short stories. (d) Moses' laws are the Ten Commandments. 

The apostrophe with s is added to a few pronouns to show possession, though pronouns as a rule change form without the addition of the apostrophe and s. One, one another, and the -body compounds add the apostrophe and the s: (a) One's duty is not always easy to perform. (b) The teacher asked us to correct one another's tests. (c) I am not interested in everybody's affairs. 

Note. The forms any one else's, any one's else, somebody else's, somebody's else, etc., are correct. 

2. In the formation of a plural noun not ending in s, the apostrophe and s are added to denote possession: (a) We sell men's handkerchiefs. (b) We sell women's shoes upstairs. (c) The children's playground should have more equipment. 

3. The apostrophe is added to plural noun forms ending in s to show possession: (a) The girls' clubs will meet in this building. (b) The teachers' meeting has been postponed. (c) The Boys' Glee Club will sing tonight. 

4. The apostrophe and the s are added to the last word of a phrase which constitutes a name to denote possession: (a) The Duke of Buckingham's palace is very beautiful. (b) The Senator from Idaho's vote will decide the question. (c) Woodward and Lothrop's store will be closed tomorrow.

This construction is often awkward and inelegant, and it is wise to avoid using the apostrophe and the s tacked onto

College Rhetoric the last word of a compound or phrase unless the combination is easily pronounced. It is perhaps better to say, The home of a Representative from California was destroyed by fire than A Representative from California's home 5. The apostrophe is used to denote an omission in a contracted word: (a) We won't go if you don't decide to go with us. (b) Can't you see that I've been busy? (c) It's time to sing. (d) He belongs to the class of '31. 

6. The apostrophe followed by an s is used to form the plural of figures and signs, of words and letters: 

1. Cross your t's and dot your i's. 

2. You have too many and's in this paragraph. 

3. I made three A's, two B's, and one C. 

4. Omit the 3's and 4's in this assignment. 

5. Avoid using &'s in formal writing. 

6. He has four *'s on one page.

Note. There is good authority for adding the s without the apostrophe to indicate the plural of letters, words, and signs. The following are logical enough and are accepted by some printers as plural forms: ds, ands, 3s, &s.

Warnings 

The apostrophe should not be used carelessly to denote plural forms of nouns: 

WRONG: Ladies' are not permitted to register for this course.

RIGHT: Ladies are not permitted to register for this course.

WRONG: He has lived in five different country's. 

RIGHT: He has lived in five different countries.

Punctuation 

189 WRONG: He found the bank's closed yesterday. 

RIGHT: He found the banks closed yesterday. 

WRONG: Household utility's are more expensive this year. 

RIGHT: Household utilities are more expensive this year. 

The possessive pronouns his, hers, its, yours, ours, and theirs used absolutely or predicatively should not have an apostrophe before the final s: 

WRONG: The books on the shelf are her's and your's. 

RIGHT: The books on the shelf are hers and yours.

CHAPTER VIII 

Spelling

EARNING to spell is not an easy task. On the other Le hand, it is not, to most intelligent persons, an impossible one. The following suggestions may help the student who has set for himself the task of learning to spell: 

1. Know the meanings of the words you wish to use; and know how each word you use should be pronounced. Some words are not pronounced as they are spelled, but most words are. Note anomalous spellings and memorize exceptions. 

2. Proper syllabication will help one to spell accurately.

If you do not know how to separate a word into syllables, consult the dictionary, and study each part of the word. 

Then look away from the printed page and visualize the word. After this write the word several times. Get your hand, your eye, and your ear accustomed to the word which causes you trouble. 

3. Never misspell the same word over and over. Once your attention has been called to a misspelling, study the word, try to discover why you misspelled it. Do not repeat the mistake. 

4. Make it a practice to study etymology. Often the key to correct spelling is found in an early form. 

5. When different spellings of a word are recognized as correct by the standard dictionaries, choose the simpler one. 

Also prefer the American form of a word to the English or other foreign forms. When in America do as the Americans 191

College Rhetoric do. Program is to be preferred to programme; humor, to humour; meter, to metre; kidnaped, to kidnapped; traveler, to traveller. 

6. In writing out foreign words, especially French, observe carefully all the markings, unless your dictionary gives you authority to Anglicize the form. Write tête-à-tête, not teteatete; débris, not debris; attaché, not attache; résumé, not resume; rôle, not role. 

7. Capitalize all words regarded as proper noun derivatives. Write English, not english; German, not german;

Indian, not indian. Other languages do not, however, have the same rules as English for the capitalization of proper nouns. A borrowed noun must be written as it would be written in its original language; as, Schiff, "ship." 

No one wants his name about to write out a 

8. Spell proper names with care. carelessly spelled. When you are proper name, note all the vowels in it; and make an effort to write legibly a's, e's, i's, o's, m's, n's, w's, and u's. Remember that a man's name is his personal property, and he may have a certain pride in it. A mistake in a single letter may cause confusion. When one writes Johnson was a great literary critic, one should mean Samuel Johnson, rather than Ben Jonson, whose name has no h in it. 

1. Both Samuel Johnson and Ben Jonson were literary critics. 

2. Spenser's (not Spencer's) Faërie Queene is an allegory. 

3. Herbert Spencer (not Spenser) wrote The Philosophy of Style. 

9. Complete each word. Do not carelessly omit the final letter. They is frequently written the in student papers; and there often appears as ther; and therefore, as therefor. 

10. Make use of all the rules you know for spelling the words you use. A simple rule will often save you time and effort when you are preparing a manuscript. When a rule

Spelling 193 fails to help you in determining the correct spelling, however, you must consult the dictionary. When in doubt do not trust your ear or your friends or your relatives. Certain words are not spelled phonetically; and your friends and relatives may know less about spelling than you do. 

11. Make use of any associations or trick schemes you possess or can invent to help you to remember how to differentiate words similar in spelling or sound. If you confuse proceed and precede, study the words until you have some way to tell how to differentiate them. Perhaps you will conclude that the one that has three e's has them appearing singly, and the one having two e's has them appearing as double ee. Corpse and corps are often confused in spelling, though seldom incorrectly used when orally employed, since they are not pronounced alike. Remember that the one with the final mute e (or "dead" e) is the one to use in representing a dead person. You may remember to spell villain and Britain correctly by associating the ai (a before i) as in certain, which word you may not misspell. Lose and loose (or losing and loosing) are often confused. The one representing a loss has "lost" one of the o's.

Find 

Keep a list of all the words you misspell month by month, and see that the list decreases rather than increases. a scheme for guiding you in choosing the correct word and the correct spelling. 

12. Above all, show the reader of your papers that you really care. If you make a serious effort to correct all the errors marked on your written work, your spelling will improve.

General Rules for Spelling 

Rule I.—“"ei" and "ie" pronounced as “ee” or “ā.” 

Use i before e except after c or when sounded as ā in neighbor: chief, relieve, belief, deceive, conceive, vein, heinous.

College Rhetoric 

Exceptions: either, neither, weird, seize, leisure, plebeian, inveigle. 

Note. The following words are not spelled according to this rule because the ei or ie is not pronounced as ee or ā: height foreign heifer sleight forfeit counterfeit 

Rule II.-Final silent "e." ancient conscientious deity

Silent e is usually dropped before a suffix beginning with a vowel, and is usually retained before a suffix beginning with a consonant: love use loving using lovable usable advise advisable adviser measuring measurer lovely useful advisement measurement measure 

Exceptions: 

1. Words ending in -ce and -ge retain the silent e when adding -able and -ous, so that c or g may not be pronounced with a "hard" sound (i.e., may retain their character as continuous sounds): peaceable traceable noticeable serviceable changeable outrageous courageous advantageous 2. Verbs which end in ee and oe retain the final e to prevent mispronunciation: seeing freeing agreeing shoeing hoeing toeing canoeing 3. Dyeing (from dye) retains the e to prevent confusion with dying. Also singeing retains the e to prevent one's mistaking the word for singing (participle of sing).

Rule III.-Word ending in a single consonant. 

Monosyllables and words accented on the last syllable, ending in a single consonant preceded by a single vowel,

Spelling 195 a vowel: stir stir (r) ed double the final consonant on adding a suffix beginning with stir (r) ing hop hop (p) er hop(p) ing hop (p) ed red red (d) er red (d) est infer infer (r) ed control control(1) ed control (1) er red (d) en infer (r) ing control(1) ing control (1) able picnic picnic (k) ing (ck=cc) Exceptions: 

1. X* is never doubled: * X=ks. tax tax ed tax ing box box ed box ing mix mix ed mix ing 2. A final consonant is not doubled when it is not preceded by a single vowel or when it is not accented on the last syllable: defend defending Final consonant bind binding burst bursting not preceded build building by a vowel. infer * inference benefit benefited benefiting 

Accent not on the last syllable. *Accent shifted when suffix is added. banquet shoot cheat banqueting shooting cheating cheated

Final consonant not preceded by a single vowel. 

Note 1.-Quit, quiz, equip are not exceptions, since u after q equals w. and i may therefore be regarded as a single vowel: quit quit (t) ing quit(t) ed quiz quiz(z) ing quiz(z) ed equip equip (p) ing equip (p) ed

College Rhetoric 

Note 2-A few words not accented on the last syllable but ending in a single consonant preceded by a single vowel, may or may not double the final consonant, though modern usage favors the single consonant: worship quarrel travel worshiped (or worshipped) quarreling (or quarrelling) traveled (or travelled) traveling (or travelling) kidnap kidnaping (or kidnapping) kidnaped (or kidnapped) 

Rule IV.-Words ending in "y." 

1. Words ending in y preceded by a consonant usually change y to i on adding a suffix beginning with any letter other than i: bury bur ies buri ed (but bury ing) busy busi ness busi ed (but busy ing) body bodi es bodi ed cry cri es cri ed (but cry ing) try tri es tri ed (but try ing) duty duti ful deny deni es deni ed (but deny ing) reply repli es repli ed (but reply ing) Exceptions: said, paid, laid, staid, (or stay ed); but when -ing is added, the forms are spelled by the rule: saying, paying, laying, staying. 

2. When y is preceded by a vowel, it is not changed to i before a suffix beginning with a vowel: delay monkey joy enjoy delayed monkeyed joyous enjoyed delaying monkeying enjoying, enjoyable Note.-Soliloquy and colloquy are not exceptions to this rule, since the u after q is regarded as a w, and not as a vowel. The -es suffix is added to these words after changing y to i as in Rule IV (1): soliloquies and colloquies. 

Before adding the suffix -ous to words ending in final y, the y is often changed to e: 3. beauty pity duty beaute ous pite ous dute ous

Spelling 197 4. The final y is usually retained in compounds: babyhood. anybody daylight copyright byword myself Rule V.-The final letter of a prefix, a suffix, and a word.

1. Words ending in l or n: 

A word ending in a single lor n retains the letter on adding a suffix beginning with an l or an n: real ly cordial ly wool ly equal ly final ly formal ly drunken ness stubborn ness thin ness usual ly commercial ly

A word or prefix ending in double l or double n usually drops one of the letters on adding a suffix: already skilful * fulsome wilful * almost altogether always fulfil (fulfill) *Skilful and wilful are also spelled skillful and willful, but the shorter forms are to be preferred. 

2. One is usually dropped from a suffix ending in double l: skilful wilful mindful doubtful tactful cupful careful deceitful 3. The final letter of a prefix is usually retained before the same letter in the root: dis solve mis spell dis sent mis shapen 

See Rule VIII (1).

Rule VI.-Words ending in "-ede" and "-eed" from 

Latin "cedere."

College Rhetoric 

Most words derived from Latin cedere are spelled with -ede: precede accede concede intercede recede retrocede antecede cede 

Exceptions: Three words so derived are, however, spelled with -eed: exceed, succeed, proceed.

Rule VII.-Latin derivatives with suffixes "-able," “-ible,” “-ant," "-ent," "-ence," "-ance," and English words in "-able." 

1. A word of Latin origin usually retains the stem vowel of the conjugation from which the word is derived. Words having -ance, -able, -ant endings are usually from verbs belonging to the first conjugation; and words having -ence, -ible, -ent suffixes come from the other three conjugations. 

Thus servant (from Latin servāre) keeps the infinitive ā in the ending. Dependent (from Latin dependere) keeps the infinitive e in its ending. Credible (from Latin credibilis, which is from the infinitive credere) has -ible. 

2. Words of Anglo-Saxon origin usually take the -able: readable, tameable, sellable. 

Exceptions: There are many exceptions to any rule governing the spelling of derivatives, and it is wise to consult a dictionary when in doubt as to the correct spelling of a word of Latin origin.

Observe that noun forms like defendant, confidant (masculine form), confidante (feminine form), dependant, repentant, do not follow the rule given above (Rule VII). 

They have come into English from the French rather than as direct borrowings from Latin.

Rule VIII.-English and Latin prefixes. 

1.

Words having prefixes ending in the same consonant

Spelling 199 as that with which the stem begins usually have double consonants: dissect interrogate missent misspell 

2. Do not confuse the following prefixes: (a) ac- (from Latin ad, to) with ex- (from Latin ex, from, out, away): accept except (b) ante- (before) with anti- (against): antediluvian anti-slavery (c) dis- (apart) with de (from, about): disarm disaster discern dismiss dissolve descend depress describe demolish despair (d) per- (through) with pre- (before): perform perspire precede preclude 

Rule IX.-Plurals of nouns. 

1. Most nouns form their plurals by adding an s to the singular: boy girl house boys girls houses 2. Most nouns ending in -o preceded by a consonant form their plurals by adding -es: potato volcano tomato mosquito potatoes volcanoes tomatoes mosquitoes

College Rhetoric 

But a few nouns ending in -o form their plurals by adding -s: piano banjo pianos banjos altos alto 3.

Nouns ending in y preceded by a consonant change y to i and add -es: family lady families ladies

If y is preceded by a vowel, add only an -s: valley toy chimney valleys toys chimneys

Note.-Verbs follow the rules governing the pluralization of nouns in forming the third person singular of the indicative mode:

I walk I come I try you walk you come you try he walks he comes he tries Rule X.-Adverbs ending in "-bly" from adjectives ending in "-able" and "-ible.”

The final e is dropped from adjectives ending in -able or -ble, and y is added, to form the corresponding adverbs: able capable responsible terrible ably capably responsibly terribly 

Rule XI.-Nouns ending in "-le,” “-el,” “-ol” vs. adjectives ending in “-al.”

Confusion sometimes arises between the endings -le, -el, -ol, and -al. It would be well to remember that the first

Spelling 

201 three are usually noun endings, the fourth an adjective ending. The following are nouns: 

principle pinnacle article handle personnel 

The following are adjectives: 

principal * personal capital * capitol nickel critical annual usual central * *Principal, capital, and central are used also as nouns; but they are in reality adjectives used substantively. 

Word Analysis 

Many students misspell words because they have only a hazy notion of what the words they use mean. 

To be an accurate speller one must know more than how to pronounce the words one uses and how to put in the right order all the letters. Mastering the spelling and the significance of the most common prefixes and suffixes of English words will help the average student materially in improving his spelling. After all, there are not so many root words in our language as we are wont to believe, and the spelling of these is not unusually difficult. More than seventy-five percent of the errors found in a recent examination of several thousand student themes and test papers occurred in the attaching of prefixes or suffixes. 

Below are lists of the most common prefixes and suffixes and their meanings. Master these and use them in forming derivatives; the practice will improve your spelling.

PREFIXES 

a-, ab- (at, in, on, to; from, full of; away): abed, abnormal, awake. ad-, and (through assimilation with the first letter of the stem of the word following) ac-, af-, ag-, an-, al-, ar-, ap-, as-, at- (to):

College Rhetoric accept, affect, agree, announce, allot, arrange, appear, assure, attend, admit, adduce. after- (behind, past; second): afternoon, aftermath. ambi-, amphi- (around, about, on both sides): ambiguous, ambidextrous, amphibious, amphitheater. an-, a- (not; without): anarchy, aphasia. ante- (before): anteroom, antecedent. ant-, anti- (against): antarctic, antidote. apo- (from): apocalypse, apostrophe. auto- (self): automatic, autograph, automobile. be- (to make; for, by; take off; give): beware, bedeck. bene- (well): benefactor, benediction. bi-, bis- (two, twice): biweekly, biscuit. circum- (around): circumference, circumflex. cis- (on this side): cisalpine, cisatlantic. con-, co-, com-, col-, cor- (together with, with): conjugate, cohere, communicate, collaborate, correspond. contra-, counter- (opposite, against): contradict, counteract. de- (down, down from; apart, from, away): depress, dethrone, deject, decline, decolor. demi- (half): demigod, demiwolf, demijohn. dia- (through): diameter, dialogue. dis-, dif-, di- (not; apart; opposite): disorder, dispel, disgrace, differ, digress. e-, ex-, ef- (from, out, out of, off; beyond, away): emit, elapse, export, effect. en-, em-, im-, in- (in, into, on; to make): ennoble, embitter, emblazon, immerse, insure, entangle, enroll. epi- (on, in; beside): epitaph, epilogue, epidemic. equi- (equal): equidistant, equilateral, equinox. eu- (well): euphonic, eupeptic, eugenic. extra- (beyond, outside): extravagance, extradite, extrajudicial. for (not; away, utterly): forbid, forbear, forlorn. fore- (in front, before): foretell, forefathers.

Spelling 203 hemi- (half): hemisphere, hemicycle. hyper- (excessively; beyond, over): hypercritical, hyperbole. hypo-, hyph- (under; into one, together): hypothesis, hypodermic, hyphenate. in-, il-, ir-, im- (not): infinite, illiterate, irregular, impersonal. inter- (between, among): interview, intermission, interrogate. intro- (within): introduce, introvert. juxta- (near): juxtaposition, juxtapose. mal- (bad): malpractice, maladroit. meta- (beyond, over; between; after; also change of form): metaphysics, metastable, metamer, metaplasm. mis- (not; evil, wrong): misdirect, miscarry, misbehave. mono- (one): monogram, monosyllable, monoplane. multi- (many): multiplicate, multimillionaire. nether- (down; low): nethermost, Netherlands. ob-, oc-, of-, op- (against, in the way of; over): object, obstacle, occur, offer, oppose. octo- (eight): octosyllable. omni- (all): omnipresent. out- (beyond; more): outdo, outlive. over- (beyond; above): overcome, overall, overburden. para- (beside): paraphrase, parallel. per- (by, through): perform, perchance. peri- (around): periphrastic, perimeter. poly- (many): polysyllable. post- (after): postpone, postscript. pre- (before): prefix, prepare. pro- (for, before, forth): pronoun, prologue, produce. re- (again, back): regain, return, reflect, retell. retro- (backward): retrograde, retroactive. se- (away, from; apart, aside): separate, secede, segregate. semi- (half): semicircle, semifluid, semimonthly. sub-, suf- (under, beneath; after): submerge, subside, sublet, suffix, suffuse.

SUFFIXES 

-able, -ible (can be, worthy to be): capable, defensible, readable, credible. -ace, -acy, -ance, -ancy, -ecy, -cy, -ency (state of being, state; office): menace, lunacy, disturbance, buoyancy, prophecy, bankruptcy, emergency, presidency. -aceous (resembling, having the nature of): herbaceous, carbonaceous. -acle, -cle, -icle, -cule (little): pinnacle, particle, icicle, molecule. -age (condition): bondage, marriage. -al, -eal, -ial (pertaining to): criminal, personal, cereal, filial, cordial. -an, -ian, -ean (pertaining to; person who): human, European, physician, musician, epicurean, subterranean. -ant, -ent (condition; person who): servant, superintendent. -ar, -ary, -er, -ery (condition, pertaining to; one who): vulgar, dictionary, trickery, adviser. -ate (adj., having quality of; noun, person who; verb., to make): ornate, potentate, vendicate, nominate. -dom (state, condition, quality): dukedom, kingdom, wisdom. -ee (one who is): employee, trustee. -eer, -er (one who; one who does); engineer, volunteer, helper, spinner. -en (1. little; 2. to make; 3. made of): maiden, blacken, wooden. -et, -let (little): locket, leaflet. -ful (abundance, full of): delightful, deceitful. -fy, -ify, -ize, -ise (to make): dignify, purify, christianize, advise.

THE CHAPTER IX  

HE QUALITIES that make for power and beauty in writing are simplicity, unity, coherence, mass, and variety. They have been touched upon here and there in the earlier discussions of this book, but the words which we here use to represent them have seldom been employed. 

The present section should afford a sort of bird's-eye view of the entire matter of style, rather than any elaborate examination of details. Simplicity

Call it sincerity if you will, honesty of mind and purpose; it is the first law of art. Avoid "showing-off," pomposity, bluster, smirking-every sort of posing, affectation, personal display. The one sure cure for the disposition to strike an attitude is a deep consuming desire to discover to oneself and a reader the real truth and beauty and ugliness and fun of things, free from all pretense, all artificial loftiness or leering indecency. The spell-weaver, the poseur, the literary Smart-Alec are all annoying to the serious reader, who craves authentic reality, spontaneous fantasy, and hearty romance. 

"FINE WRITING" 

Rhetoricians of some years ago had a fashion of labeling affected style "fine writing." Whoever invented the phrase, 219

College Rhetoric it is a good one. It penetrates to the heart of the mischief and blasts with irony. Fine writing abounds in sweet trite phrases and figures of speech at once pretentious and impossible.

EXAMPLES OF "FINE WRITING" 

(Reproduced verbatim from student compositions) 1. We are merely butterflies, sipping the honey with no thought of the day when we must crawl into our cocoon and become a worm. 

2. The understanding and contentment have flown from the dark corners of the room, seemingly, to have been appalled by the marvelous music ensuing forth from the family circle. 

3. A long path, that is full of pitfalls, hidden by flowers and pleasures, and a steep one that gradually widens as one travels along it. 

4. Like the stars that are ever twinkling in the immensity of void, chemistry is the guiding star which has lighted the footsteps of man to his present height of achievement and which he is still following to attain greater heights, the end of which no mortal is granted to see. 

As I gaze upon this hypothetical horizon I see that mighty symmetry being welded together to become an object of brightness and beauty. It is a true saying that chemistry fancies itself a demi-god, nay, a god, for only gods never tire of the prodigious. The highest, the largest, and the most costly is the breath of its vanity. 

POETICAL WORDS 

Many students who religiously avoid slang, provincialism, and vulgarisms go to the other extreme and develop mannerisms equally objectionable. Poetical words in the main are archaic; they should never be employed in prose writing (except perhaps for irony), and only as a last resort in verse.

Exercise 

The following poetical words have good substitutes which one should use in all expository writing. Give the prose forms for these: adown afar in sooth quoth morn repast affray apace arise methinks thee methought thou ne'er thrice anew o'er whilom dight ope yclept ere oft ye eve hither pen (write) perchance yon 

RHETORICAL QUESTION 

The rhetorical question should be used only in the expression of extreme emotion, as in Tennyson's "When shall their glory fade?" It neither requires nor permits an answer. Its employment in trivial or purely intellectual matters, especially with appended reply, is rather crudely affected.

Unity A brilliant American President is said to have spoken disparagingly of his own "single-track mind." But the same President has been praised for his singleness of purpose and his fine sense of direction. Most good serious writing and serious reading must be of the single-track variety. An agile circus rider may make forward on two ponies at the same time, but the beasts must be going in the same direction. Wherever there is the purpose to explain, to inform, to convince, to move to action, there must be congruity ("belongingness") of ideas, phrases, figures, and images. Incongruity ("unbelongingness") is the language of sheer fun, antic wit, buffoonery, and fooling. "Twas brillig, and the slithy toves Did gyre and gimble in the wabe" is deliciously ear-tickling, but the very point and glory of Lewis Carroll's drollery is, that it does not say anything. In fact, the famous poet of nonsense was most deliberately bent upon saying nothing in impressive fashion. 

The tragedy of the boy who wrote, "Football is a noble sport, they have to collect money at the gate to pay traveling expenses" is, that he was trying hard to say something, and failing.

Unity should characterize every department or segment of expression, from the course of lectures to the sentence. 

Students of a certain professor in a small college years ago were accustomed to note that there was one bit of information which he "lugged" into every series of talks. Being versatile, he taught a wide variety of subjects; but whether the course was nominally devoted to chemistry, zoölogy, or international law, he would always contrive before the end of the term to work in his favorite family recipe for the preparation of Boston baked beans. And the same teacher would break a sentence apart in order to inject a tribute to his own progeny. "These problems," he would exclaim,

College Rhetoric 

"are difficult-and no one knows this better than my son Gerald, who mastered them a year ago, but you must not surrender to them!"

However, those who have engaged in the practice of analyzing rhetorical style have put the greatest stress, and rightly, on the unity of the paragraph. Here we have sentences bound together in a sequence and set off in the page by indention. Just those sentences which belong togetherno others may be included. The paragraph should treat one phase of thought, and one only. 

As an example of the paragraph characterized by unity strictly adhered to, we quote the following, from LeBaron 

Russell Briggs' admirable essay Routine and Ideals: 

The older I grow, the more strongly I feel that the best thing in man or woman is being "there." Physical bravery, which is always inspiring, is surprisingly common; but the sure and steady quality of being "there" belongs to comparatively few. This is why we hear on every hand, "If you want a thing done well, do it yourself"; not because the man who wants it done is best able to do it, but because to many persons it seems a hopeless quest to look for any one who cares enough for them, who can put himself vigorously enough into their places, to give them his best, to give them intelligent, unremitting, loyal service until the job is done,-not half done, or nine tenths done, or ninety-nine hundredths done, but done, with intelligence and devotion in every nail he drives, or every comma he writes. Some are reluctant, some afraid of doing more than they are paid for, some indifferent, some obligingly helpless but not well trained and not so deeply devoted as to train themselves. I suppose that in one sphere of life or another a number of these persons earn what they get. 

Yet sometimes I think there are only two kinds of service, that which is not worth having at any price, and that for which no money can pay. All of us know a few who give this latter kind of service, and know what they are to us, and to every one with whom they deal. These are the people who are "there." 1 

1 Briggs, LeBaron Russell, College Life. Houghton Mifflin Co., Boston, 

1913. By permission of, and arrangement with, the publishers.

Try reading over the above quoted paragraph half a dozen times. Note that the first sentence is a "topic sentence": that it affirms a thesis, announces a theme. Then remark that what follows reaffirms, elaborates, explains, applies this one idea. Finally, observe that the last sentence closes as did the first, with the same surprising and revealing word. Having respect to a law which limits the construction of a symphony, the paragraph ends in the key in which it began.

Examine now a paragraph by a beginner in college writing, reproduced verbatim: 

Riding is important in that it develops sportsmanship. 

Being brought into contact with a horse, one is able to understand its habits and tricks. If one has an instructor, he will tell one of its action and peculiarities. 

He will teach one the rules of sportsmanship. Also he will enlighten you upon the proper posture and the position of hands and limbs in riding. 

If the first sentence suggests a "topic," the writer gets no further with it. Sentences two and three do not mention sportsmanship, nor do they seem to concern that subject even by implication. Sentence four, indeed, contains the word sportsmanship, but in a detached and obscure fashion. 

The final sentence speaks of physical posture. What is the paragraph about? Horses? How to ride? How to get acquainted with a horse? Surely not about the development of sportsmanship through riding, yet just that is what the initial sentence promised.

Finally, the doctrine of unity is "Stick to your text"—or rather, choose a text; announce it; and stick to it.

8. I think that it is just as important to become developed along the lines of physical education as it is to be developed scholastically or mentally. As I have a slow nature, I welcome the compulsion of athletics as a means by which I will get into the habit of being swift. Athletics require one to think quickly, act quickly, and therefore to develop swiftness. A student in college also learns to know many people, and gets much pleasure and enjoyment from all the social and religious events carried on on the campus. It is in these organizations that the students find a place to express themselves and their leadership ability.

Coherence

Think of incoherent speech: the ideas do not stick together; the sentences are disconnected; the mind of the speaker rambles without aim or hops goat-like from point to point; meanings are cloudy; the hearer must guess, or remain totally unenlightened. Coherent speech, on the other hand, is fused: parts are coördinated; relations are made evident; meanings are unmistakable; the hearer understands fully and follows easily.

It will be convenient to consider coherence as embodying two principles, closely related but not identical: (1) connection between sentences and between paragraphs, and (2) clearness of individual expressions.

CONNECTION 

Passage from one sentence to another is always a step; passage from one paragraph to another is always a leap. 

Help your reader to make the step with confidence; do not force him to leap in the dark. A criminal fleeing from bloodhounds tries to conceal his track: doubles his trail, jumps streams, walks in water. But as a writer you want to be followed; therefore be like the "hare" in the old game of hare-and-hounds, who as he runs scatters bits of torn paper continually, that there may never be a doubt as to the direction of his running. 

Make wide use of guideboard adverbs and phrases, such as however, moreover, indeed, none the less, on the other hand, furthermore, and, but, to return, to proceed, accordingly, hence, yet. But these expressions must be used accurately, else disaster is bound to occur. Suppose that 

General Garfield, instead of saying, as he did, "Fellow citizens, God reigns, and the government at Washington still lives," had inadvertently substituted "but" for "and." 

What a frightfully different meaning his words would have

College Rhetoric conveyed! Yet the young person who wrote, "I am applying for a scholarship; however, I expect to get it" quite as completely misrepresented his probable meaning. 

A final word on the subject of transition: no mere mechanical use by the writer of transitional words and phrases will avail completely to keep his reader in the track of the former's thought. What most matters is that the writer keep the reader in mind, directing him by all possible arts (even by those of which the writer may himself be unconscious) in the path he should follow.

CLEARNESS 

A freshman wrote, "No quarrels, no conflicts, and no hard feelings would make every sport a perfect one." At first glance this looks like, "No sighs, no tears, no agonized regret can ever quite atone for my sin." Sighs and tears may, indeed, almost atone; can quarrels and conflicts make a sport almost perfect? A reader is likely to conclude, however, that what the boy meant was, that a complete absence of quarrels, conflicts, and hard feelings would make every sport a perfect one. Any intelligent reader, given a little time, could figure this out. But why make him figure? Is it his business, or is it the writer's, to establish the meaning? 

Obscurity is a fault which freshmen share with seniors and college professors, lawyers, theologians, and worldfamous poets. Probably no writer continually and completely escapes it. But we should all try, and keep trying. 

Let every writer say, "This one thing I do: I make myself clear!"

It is here, and in the former, that their college education is an advantage to them over other fellows. The college expects its graduates to apply their education to further and better themselves.

50. I wish to say frankly, as men and women of this great 

United States of America; a nation who prides herself (and we have perfect logical and moral right to do so) and stands at the very summit of educational qualities and modern achievements in the world; then to realize that our vast productive fields of our future greatness, our colleges and universities are childlike in nature; holding to traditions and customs of centuries ago. 

It is of little wonder, that the public has become thoroughly disgusted after they come to realize the fact that humans, at the age of eighteen and over, are entering into that period of individual life known as manhood or maturity. A period of life which should begin to show difference in signs of manly conduct; behavior fitting for matured people. 2 2 3

Mass Mass concerns itself with arrangement and proportion. 

Arrangement may be chronological, logical, or psychological; or it may combine any two of these characteristics. 

A simple story, involving only one plot, is likely to proceed in chronological order; that is, straight forward from earlier to later events. However, in order to arouse and hold attention, a writer may begin his story in the middle, going back after a time to cover earlier phases of the narrative. This was a favorite psychological device of Tennyson and of Joseph Conrad (see Geraint and Enid and Chance). 

The logical order is seen typically in mathematical demonstrations. It is quite properly used in most expository essays since it is conducive to clearness, and therefore to easy understanding. Logical climax is achieved when the exposition or the demonstration is complete; it is found in the cap-statement (in a geometrical solution preceded by therefore and followed by quod erat demonstrandum-"which was to be proved"). But even in exposition, where the object of the writer is to instruct or convince the mind rather than to stir the emotions, he should take into account the "psychology" of his reader; in general, he should place less important things before more important, and qualifying ideas before principal ones.

Emotional story or description requires an adroit massing of images and phrases and the employment of effects of color and rhythm in the building up of an intended mood. 

This is the purely psychological arrangement. Poe's Raven, for example, begins somewhat nonchalantly with the picture of a tired man resting in a luxuriously appointed living room. Then follow suggestions of dreamy longing for the "lost Lenore," the two potent motifs of love and death being carefully blended. When the huge black bird flits into the room out of the dreary midnight, he is only a bird, un-

College Rhetoric gainly, rather stupid, though vaguely portentous. In the presence of this wild creature, however, with his monotonously chanted “nevermore” (a word rhythmic, beautifully flowing, and hauntingly meaningful), the man begins a process of self-torture which finally pictures the bird as something more terrible than a bird, and which rises to a climax of frenzy touching insanity in "Bird or fiend!", I shrieked upstarting.

From this point the mood sinks away to the ominous quiet of 

Shall be lifted nevermore! with which the poem closes. 

No fixed rules govern the arrangement of parts in a sentence, paragraph, or still larger unit of artistic expression. 

The purpose to compel attention, to rouse, to hold, may cause an artist to digress from or even reverse all logic and convention of established order. The newspaper account of the disastrous effects of a storm may begin with the deaths and conclude with the dinner postponements. It is said that when Handel was presenting The Messiah for the first time, in London, he found his audience tiring after two hours of the music. On a sudden impulse he injected into the program the great "Hallelujah Chorus," which had been originally put at the end. All auditors were thrilled. 

The King rose in his box and remained standing. The entire audience, taking the hint from the King, rose too. Attention was keen from that point to the conclusion of the oratorio, and the position of the incomparable chorus in the sequence of parts had been changed for all time. 

The subject of proportion may be covered by a few words of injunction. Principal things, those of large import, should be accorded the most extended treatment. 

Normally an introduction should cover considerably less space than the theme itself. There should be no long and

inconsequential digressions; parentheses should be few. 

No elaboration of a single point should run to unwarrantable length; it is therefore usually a mistake to let an illustrative story cover two pages of a three-page theme. A writer would do well to keep in mind the symmetry of a well-formed animal body or of an artistic building, and try to make his essay similarly well balanced. 

Exercise 

Make an outline for a theme of one thousand words on any one of the subjects listed below. Make the outline complete as to principal divisions and fairly minute as to subordinate ones. 

Indicate opposite each heading the approximate number of words which you think you would be likely to devote to its elaboration. 

1. Home-Coming Day 2. Vexation over Semester Grades 3. A Defense of Egotism 4. My Difficulties in Writing English 5. Campus Bores 6. A Program for Abolishing War 7. Jane Is Unpredictable 8. When School Days Stop Variety 

An almost absolute prerequisite to variety in writing, as in any other art, is individuality. One should be oneself and not another or a host of others; one should oneself be a variant, an individual. The student, appointed to do a literary task may object, "It is easy for you to say, 'Be original, be different,' yet how can I be? My life conforms to the lives of people about me, and so do my thoughts, my attitudes, my phrases conform. Such conformity is only natural!" But the law of difference in nature is quite as constant as that of likeness. Every blade of grass in the meadow, every minnow in the stream, differs from every other blade and minnow. No human being is exactly like

College Rhetoric another. Even twins differ in a host of traits. Truth, error, phenomena reach each one of us from a special angle and are seen by us in different lights. And this fact, if we would trust it, gives us something to say to any and all. 

Says Emerson: 

To believe your own thought, to believe that what is true for you in your private heart is true for all men-that is genius A man should learn to detect and watch that gleam of light which flashes across his mind from within, more than the lustre of the firmament of bards and sages. 

-RALPH WALDO EMERSON: 

Self-Reliance. 

Granted that a writer has learned to assert his individuality in what he writes, and that this latter differs in greater or less degree from the product of every other mind, he must yet strive to achieve variety of expression inside his own range of ideas. This he can do by redoubling his intensity. Pressure brought by the keen desire for clearness and exactitude of question and statement is bound to result in varied language. Delicate differences of perception, nice distinctions of idea, cannot be expressed by a few vague words and a few catch-all phrases. Active adventure in thinking is inevitably accompanied by expanding vocabulary.

Much can be accomplished, however, by simply taking thought not to fall into monotony of utterance. Certain detailed prohibitions may be set down here, some of which have been stressed in earlier sections of this book: 

1. Avoid hackneyed, stereotyped phrases and metaphors. 

2. Avoid verbal repetition, except for emphasis. 

3. Do not have pet stories and pet illustrations which you lead out on all public occasions. 

4. Do not imitate the jargon of the racetrack, newspaper column, pulpit, or underworld.

5. Avoid using a "sentence mold"; that is, casting successive sentences on the same grammatical shape. Vary your syntax. 

6. Avoid rhetorical mannerisms: the impersonal you and we, and the rhetorical question. 

7. Do not be consciously and continually figurative. 

Rest your reader with plain talk. 192 

8. Avoid "the immense pomposity of sesquipedalian verbiage. Use long words and short ones judiciously, with no preference for either. 

9. Vary your sentence length. Especially avoid the "choppy" or "hummocky" style: the monotonous succession of babyish short sentences. 

2 A humorous phrase employed by Herbert Spencer in The Philosophy of Style.
