CHAPTER X Letter-Writing 

ENERALLY speaking, letters may be classified as per- "sonal (or friendly) letters, business letters, and social notes, formal and informal; and while there are certain arbitrary rules governing the form and composition of each of these types, the observation of which is essential if one would avoid being regarded as crude or negligent, the first requirement of a good letter is good English. Accuracy as to grammar, correct spelling, intelligent punctuation, and interesting and forceful style are qualities of the best letters as much as they are qualities of any other type of composition. Loose and inaccurate statements, coarse and vulgar language, accusations and threats, sarcasm, and discourtesy too often reveal loose thinking or low character; furthermore they may cause unnecessary and annoying misunderstandings and, too frequently, a severing of the social or business relations between friends and acquaintances. 

There is no royal road to the writing of original and effective letters; and the student who masters the art of letter-writing must study this form of composition as he would poetry, painting, music, or sculpture seriously and arduously. He should be ever on the alert to improve his style. 

A systematic study of the published letters of famous men and women would be valuable in developing one's style and taste in this art. The following are recommended: Robert 

Browning, Elizabeth Barrett Browning, Mark Twain, Theodore Roosevelt, William James, Madame Sévigné, Lord 235

College Rhetoric 

Chesterfield, Emerson, Tennyson, Lincoln, Thackeray,

Franklin, and Dickens. From the correspondence of these writers it would be possible to learn what may with profit be included in letters, as well as how to write interestingly and effectively.

Materials. The essential effect of a letter may depend upon the reader's first impression. The writer, therefore, should take pains to make the physical parts of his letter neat and attractive. The conventional size of correspondence paper is 812 by 11 inches, though it is correct to use half sheets, 52 by 8½ inches, for short communications. 

Business letters should be written on white or delicately tinted (gray, green, or buff) paper, of good quality, unruled. The communication should be typed or written in ink by hand. If the letter is typed, a black ribbon (not red, bright blue, or purple) should be used. If the letter is written longhand, the ink should be black or blue-black (not red, green, or purple). For friendly letters, one's taste must select appropriate materials. Social notes of a formal or informal nature are written on a correspondence card or social note paper, which one may buy at any standard stationery store. Fashion changes in these matters, and frequently one must consult the managers of reputable stationery departments to discover the correct style of note paper, especially for formal correspondence. It is never in fashion to type a formal or even a semi-formal note. 

Neatness. A letter must represent the writer and take the place of a personal interview. It is important that one represent oneself well in personal correspondence. There are persons who will take pains to look correct when making a personal interview, and yet are willing to send as their representatives slovenly written letters. Any one who must write letters should provide himself with good materials, and take pains to produce a neat and attractive manuscript. 

One should not scratch out words, phrases, or sentences, nor

Letter-Writing 

237 have in one's letter elaborate insertions. A letter faulty in form or incomplete should be rewritten, especially if it is any sort of business or official letter. 

Length. Letters will be long or short according to the text of the communication. A short letter is preferred for business correspondence; it should be direct and clear. 

Even personal letters should not be extremely long. A bulky manuscript often does not receive as much attention as one of moderate length. It is better to write two short letters, either in business or personal communication, than to write one so long that the reading of it may have to be delayed until the recipient can have an hour of leisure. 

Paragraphing. Paragraphs ought not to be very long, especially if the body of the letter is typed single-spaced or is written in longhand. It is better to break up a long paragraph into two or three smaller ones, and usually one can do this without difficulty by a little reorganization of the material.

Spacing. It is best to single-space the lines in a letter, leaving double spaces between the parts of the letter and between paragraphs. If the letter is extremely short, one may improve the appearance by double-spacing the lines in the body. Some authors of texts on business letters recommend an occasional short paragraph of a line or two where there is a large amount of solid text. 

Abbreviation. One should not abbreviate in business letters, and seldom in personal letters except to one's most intimate acquaintances. The title in the address, excepting 

Mr., Mrs., and Dr., should not be abbreviated. It is better to spell out Reverend and Honorable. Never abbreviate the complimentary closing or greeting.

WRONG: Dear Doc, Dear Prof, Dear Prexy, 

BETTER: Dear Doctor, Dear Professor, Dear President,

IMPROVED: Dear Dr. Cox: Dear Professor Phelps: 

Dear President Elliot:

College Rhetoric 

WRONG: Y'rs tr'ly, Y'rs resptf'ly, CORRECT: Yours truly, Yours respectfully,

Folding. A letter should be folded simply. It should fit neatly into the envelope, and it should be easily opened up and refolded. Business correspondence paper of the size 82 by 11 inches should be folded once horizontally and twice vertically. The bottom edge of the paper should be brought to within half an inch of the top edge, then folded twice. If half sheets are used, they should be folded twice vertically or horizontally; letters folded thus will fit neatly into commercial envelopes of the conventional size. The two flaps of the folded page should be placed in the envelope with the outer edge of the flap pointing downward and touching the sealed side of the envelope.

Very long letters should be sent in larger envelopes, so that the folding may be simplified.

The envelope. Envelopes for all commercial correspondence should be similar to those sold at the post office (about 62 by 3 inches in size for usual business correspondence and 4 by 10 inches for official letters). When the paper used for the letter is not the conventional kind, the envelope should match in quality and color and size. The latter should bear the same address as that given in the introduction of the letter, similarly punctuated. The writer should include his own name and address (if these are not printed on the envelope) in the upper left-hand corner of the envelope for the return of his letter in case it cannot be delivered to the one for whom it is intended. In formal social correspondence, however, it is correct to omit the return address. In friendly correspondence, it is correct to put this information on the flap of the envelope, but it is never correct to put it here on a commercial or official envelope. 

Paging. Both personal and business or official letters of more than one page in length should be marked for the

Letter-Writing 

239 reader's convenience. It is correct and necessary to put the correspondent's name or his initials on each page of business or official correspondence. Both the page number and the name or initial of the addressee should be in the upper right-hand corner of each page after the first. Thus, at the top of page 2 could appear W.J.B. -2, and at the top of page 5, W.J.B. -5. It is not correct to use letterhead paper for any but the first page of a letter. 

It is also well to consider the reader's convenience when numbering the pages of a social note or a friendly letter written on the four-page conventional type of social correspondence paper. It is more correct (and certainly more convenient) to write the text of the letter on the pages in the natural order: 1, 2, 3, 4, than 1, 3, 2, 4, or 1, 4, 2, 3. It is also better form to write the lines of the letter at right angles to the crease in the paper, instead of making them parallel with the fold.

Arrangement. All closely related elements should be single-spaced (see Spacing, page 237), with double spacing between the main divisions of the letter and between paragraphs. The letterhead of the paper will be printed and centered at the top of the first page. The address given here is presumably the writer's correct one, and the writer need add only the date to complete the heading. This should be placed in the upper right-hand corner, down a space or two and at the right of the center heading. 

Margin. The amount of space left for the margins of a letter must be determined by the length of the letter.

General parts of the letter should be centered and so arranged as to give a balanced and pleasing effect. Short letters will have wider margins than longer ones, though it is never correct to have margins extremely narrow: at least an inch of margin should be left on each side of the page. 

One should never crowd the page; nor should one write on a second page a single line, or just the complimentary

College Rhetoric closing and the signature. One should carry over enough to make the two pages alike in general appearance. 

Postscripts. Postscripts are seldom permissible in business letters. If an important bit of information has been omitted, the letter should be rewritten. In friendly letters, one may add a note, but it is not necessary to put the abbreviation P.S. before the appended information. 

Courtesy. Any other fault possible in letter-writing is more easily forgiven than discourtesy. Kindness and consideration ought to be characteristic of every letter. 

Sincerity. The writer should guard against misrepresentation of facts in a letter. He should be direct, truthful, and sincere in what he allows himself to put on paper. 

The recipient of a letter must draw conclusions from what he reads, and the meaning should therefore be clear and correct. Apologies and excuses for misrepresentation seldom satisfy offended acquaintances or officials. It is cowardly and childish to hint at and insinuate what one dares not say directly.

Affectation. One ought not to allow oneself to develop absurd or extravagant mannerisms in his letters. Do not insult your reader by telling him when you are joking by writing Ha! after a remark that ought to be clear without the exclamation. Do not make a practice of underscoring words that you want emphasized. Allow your reader to feel the emphasis from the phrasing of your thoughts. Do not put question marks after declarative sentences. Trust your reader to get your meaning. Write direct and forceful

English. Choose discriminating words. 

Vocabulary. In letter-writing one has an excellent opportunity to develop a vocabulary; to do this one must take pains to avoid trite and vague phrasing. Above all things the writer must be clear and original, for the stereotyped letter seldom interests people in its content. In business or personal letters, also, it is important to phrase one's thought

Letter-Writing 

241 in such a way that the reader must read entire sentences to get the meaning. Surprise and delight your correspondent with originality. 

The following sentences copied from business letters reveal a sad lack of originality in the authors. Could you not suggest more effective wording for these thoughts?

The phrases that you have heard many times are likely to be the ones you should avoid. Find them in these passages: 

1. I have before me your letter of recent date, and the contents have been carefully noted. 

2. Enclosed please find my son's high-school record. 

3. We are sending you our report under separate cover. 

4. Permit me to thank you for bringing this matter to our attention. 

5. Our Mr. Klein will consider your proposition and report to you in due time. 

6. Your kind letter of 21st inst. has been duly received, and the contents carefully noted. In reply we beg to state that the delay has been unavoidable. 

7. Thanking you in advance for giving this matter your kind attention, I beg to remain 8. Please regard this matter as strictly confidential. 

9. Yours of recent date is at hand. In reply would say am expecting a new shipment in the near future. 

10. Enclosed find ten tickets to our benefit. Please return what you do not use, and oblige. 

11. Enclosed find my check for $10.00. Please credit the same to my account. 

12. We beg to disagree.

Personal Letters 

Personal, or friendly, letters are written to friends, relatives, or strangers. It is in the composition of this type of writing that one is permitted to call into play social and

College Rhetoric literary talents. There is no better medium for the establishment of lasting friendships than the letter, and no greater opportunity for the development of a clear and forceful style. The personal letter is a one-sided conversation. It may be a philosophical treatment of any subject the writer is interested in; it may be argumentative, dramatic, narrative, or poetic. Whatever phase of literary art the writer is interested in mastering, he may practise in correspondence with relatives and friends. The letterwriter speaks his mind without interruption from the one he is addressing. He can blame no one but himself if he fails to write effectively.

Friendly letters should be marked by the same careful attention to details of form and composition as that which characterizes the good business or correct social letter. No one ought to be guilty of sending through the mail a slovenly letter, even though it be to his most intimate friend.

Business Letters 

The letter has long been recognized as a convenient and efficient method of establishing and conducting business relations. The success of a business not infrequently depends upon the type of letter the manager can send out.

One's success in obtaining a position with a business organization or with a professional company may depend almost wholly upon one's ability to write an effective letter. 

The purpose of any letter is to move the recipient to a sympathetic consideration of the contents, whatever these may be; and if a writer fails in this purpose, his communication is valueless.

The observation of a few principles governing the form and composition of business letters may add much to their effectiveness. If the letter is to accomplish its mission, it must be read thoughtfully and sympathetically; and the

Letter-Writing 

243 least a writer can do to insure a careful reading of his correspondence is to make the task of the recipient easy and pleasant. No one who has anything else to do is likely to labor through an illegible and formless letter, unless he is convinced that the profit of doing so will be great—and such an incentive is indeed rare. 

The Form of the Letter 

All letters except very informal notes, and formal invitations and acceptances and declinations, should contain the following parts: 

1. The Heading: The address of the writer and the date. 

2. The Address: The name and address of the corre spondent. 

3. The Salutation: The greeting, or introductory phrase. 

4. The Body: The letter proper, the communication, or message. 

5. The Conclusion: The complimentary close and the writer's signature. 

6. The Superscription: The address on the envelope. 

The Heading 

All business letters and most social letters should contain a heading, which is the writer's address. One ought not to expect his friends to know or remember his exact location. 

The conventional place for the heading is at the top of the first page of the letter. It is permissible in certain types of personal letters to place this information at the close of the correspondence, below and at the left margin; but it is not proper to put it here in any form of commercial correspondence.

When letterhead paper is used, only the date need be added to complete the heading. If letterhead paper is not

College Rhetoric used, the full address of the writer should be placed at the upper right-hand corner on the first page only. Ample margins should be allowed at the top and at the right. The number of lines required for the heading will depend upon the elaborateness of the address. The date, as a rule, should be placed on a line below the name of the town and state. 

The lines of the address may be blocked, having an even margin at the left; or they may be indented, with successive indention at the left. The punctuation of either form may be open (no commas to close the lines and no periods. except after abbreviations), or closed (a comma closes each line, and a period closes the last word of the address). 

The first line of the heading, whether open or closed punctuation is used, should begin about the middle of the page-this, however, depending somewhat upon the length of the names in the address.

The illustrations below show the four possible forms for the heading: 

(Blocked with Open Punctuation) 

1142 South 31st Street 

Omaha, Nebraska 

January 30, 1934 (Indented with Open Punctuation) 

1142 South 31st Street · 

Omaha, Nebraska 

January 30, 1934 (Indented with Closed Punctuation) 

1142 South 31st Street, Omaha, Nebraska, January 30, 1934. (Blocked with Closed Punctuation) 

1142 South 31st Street, Omaha, Nebraska, January 30, 1934.

Letter-Writing 

245 There is good authority for using any of the above forms, but one should take pains to be consistent, using the same form for the heading and the correspondent's address in the letter and in the superscription on the envelope.

The Address of the Correspondent 

Business letters and friendly letters to all persons except one's intimate acquaintances should contain the address of the correspondent. The arrangement of the lines of the address and the punctuation of it must accord with the heading form. The address of the correspondent should be placed at the left-hand corner of the first page of the letter, a double space below and to the left of the last line of the heading. The following shows the correct arrangement of the heading and the address of the correspondent: 

University of Maryland, College Park, Maryland, December 20, 1933. 

Mr. James Richey, The Blackburn Hotel, Chicago, Illinois. 

My dear Mr. Richey: 

Very truly yours, John Lovell, Assistant, Department of English.

Title in the Address 

A title of respect and courtesy should appear in the address of the correspondent. Write Mr. before a man's

College Rhetoric name, Messrs. before those of several men; Master before a young lad's name; Mrs. before the name of a married woman (unless the married woman has retained her maiden name in wedlock); Miss before the name of an unmarried woman; Misses before the names of several young women; 

Mesdames before the names of several women, especially elderly women. Prefix Dr. to the name of a physician, a 

Doctor of Divinity, or a Doctor of Philosophy. Reverend (Rev.) should be prefixed to the name of a minister. The 

Honorable (Hon.) should be prefixed to the name of a member of Congress, a Senator, a judge, a member of the Presidential Cabinet. His Excellency should be prefixed to the name of the President of the United States, the Governor of a State, a king, or an emperor, especially in formal or official correspondence. 

To His Excellency, Franklin D. Roosevelt, 

Executive Mansion, Washington, D. C.

Literary and professional titles are sometimes correctly added to the name in the address: 

Dr. L. A. Sherman, Dean,

Graduate School, 

University of Nebraska, Lincoln, Nebraska. 

Susanne Sterling, M.D.,

Union Memorial Hospital, 

Baltimore, Maryland.

The Honorable George W. Norris, Congressman, 

McCook, Nebraska.

Note. It is not correct for a woman to use her husband's titles, or for one to address her with her husband's titles. 

WRONG: Mrs. Doctor Johnson (omit the "Mrs." if the woman is a doctor.)

RIGHT: Mrs. Jane Johnson, M.D. 

RIGHT: Mrs. Johnson (the husband's first name or his initials may be inserted after the "Mrs.")

WRONG: Mrs. President Andrews, Mr. and Mrs. President Hill CORRECT: Mrs. Andrews, President and Mrs. Hill

Letter-Writing 

247 

The Salutation 

The salutation, or introductory greeting, used in a letter may vary with the rank of the one addressed or with the writer's intimacy with his correspondent. Dear Sir is the salutation usually chosen for addressing a man with whom the writer has no intimate acquaintance. Gentlemen or 

Dear Sirs may be used when addressing several men. Dear 

Madam, Madam, Mesdames, and Ladies are greetings used in addressing women. Dear Madam suggests about the same degree of formality as Dear Sir, one being used when the writer knows his correspondent to be a woman, and the other when he knows him to be a man. Mesdames and 

Ladies correspond to Dear Sirs. 

Dear Miss Gray, Dear Dr. Klein, Dear Mr. Coffman are regarded as somewhat formal salutations, and are forms used when one is addressing persons one has been introduced to or has some acquaintance with. 

My dear Miss Gray, My dear Dr. Klein, My dear Mr.

Coffman are especially formal. Usually the My is prefixed in addressing one's superiors or correspondents with whom one has had no previous acquaintance. 

The greeting to the President of the United States or the Governor of a State may be Your Excellency. The salutation to the President may also be Mr. President. The following are correct: 

(Blocked Form and Closed Punctuation) To His Excellency, Franklin D. Roosevelt, The White House, 

Washington, D. C.

Your Excellency:

College Rhetoric (Indented Form and Closed Punctuation) To His Excellency, Governor Albert C. Ritchie, Maryland State Capitol, 

Annapolis, Maryland.

Your Excellency: 

(Blocked Form and Closed Punctuation) To The President, Executive Mansion, Washington, D. C. 

Mr. President: 

Very respectfully yours,

Note. The To may be omitted in the addresses given above. It is also correct to use open punctuation and indented form. 

The Governor of a State may be addressed less formally: 

The Honorable Albert C. Ritchie, Governor, Maryland State Capitol, 

Annapolis, Maryland.

My dear Governor Ritchie: 

Very respectfully yours,

Letter-Writing 

249 Just Sir is appropriately used in a formal official letter, either in the greeting or in the complimentary close: 

Sir: 

I am, Sir, Your humble servant,

In strictly official letters there is always greater formality than in any other type. The modern tendency, however, is toward more natural salutations and conclusions even in the most formal official correspondence. 

The salutation in a letter to intimate friends, relatives, and acquaintances may be quite informal, and the phrasing of the greeting may vary greatly. One's correspondence with an intimate friend or relative is one side of a conversation, and the one who writes will choose the greeting that will make the correspondent feel the right relationship toward him. Any of the following may be regarded as correct: 

Dear Friend 

Dear Boy 

Dear Sister 

Dear Mother 

Old Pal 

Dear Lad 

Dear Shorty 

Dear D. J. 

Dear Daddy

The list here is, of course, by no means complete. 

The Body 

The body of the letter contains the message. It is here that the writer has opportunity to give rein to his individual bent. In letters to a friend or relative the writer is his own master; he can choose his own subject, write as much as he pleases, and take as much time for the task as

College Rhetoric he cares to. So long as he refrains from coarseness and vulgarity, he may have great freedom in revealing his inner thoughts and feelings. One's letters should be a reflection of one's own personality and character.

The Complimentary Close 

The complimentary close should be just to the right of the center under the body of the letter, with a double space (if the letter is typed) below the last line of the letter.

The first word of the complimentary phrase should be capitalized, and the phrase should be closed with a comma. 

The complimentary close should correspond in tone to the salutation, and be in harmony with the relation between the writer and his correspondent. Letters beginning 

Dear Sir, Gentlemen, Dear Madam, or Ladies may be appropriately closed with any of the following: Yours truly, Truly yours, Very truly yours, Yours very truly, Respectfully yours, Very respectfully yours.

In closing a letter to a government official, to a person older than the writer, to one's employer, or to one in any way superior in position or experience to the writer, it is correct to use Yours respectfully, Respectfully yours, Very respectfully yours.

Recommendations, testimonials, and letters to friends may be appropriately closed with Sincerely yours, Yours sincerely, Very sincerely yours, As ever, Faithfully yours.

The Signature 

The signature should be placed below the complimentary close. It should be legible and should give the correspondent all the information he will need for making a correct reply. Business letters that are typewritten may have the name of the writer and his title typed below the handwritten signature.

Letter-Writing 

251 A writer should avoid signing his name in various ways. 

He should choose a definite form for his signature, and use this in signing all business correspondence and legal documents.

A woman may inclose in parentheses her title Miss or Mrs. When no title is given, the correspondent understands that the writer signing a woman's name should be addressed as Miss. A married woman who prefers to be addressed by her husband's name should inclose her title and her husband's name in parentheses below her own signature.

The Superscription 

The superscription (the address on the envelope) should be the same as that given in the upper left-hand corner of the letter. The writer's name and address should be written or printed in the upper left-hand corner of the official or business envelope for the return of unclaimed mail. It is not correct to put the return address on formal social notes. Letters to friends may bear the return address on the flap of the envelope, but this form is never permissible in business correspondence.

Model Letters 

The following letters by eminent men suggest the vitality and interest which are possible in letter-writing: 

A PERSONAL LETTER 

(The letters of Philip Stanhope, Earl of Chesterfield, to his 

Son are excellent examples of the informal personal letter.) 

Dear Boy: 

I told you, my dear, that I would send you some stories to amuse you; I therefore now give you this history of the siege of Troy, which is very entertaining. Homer, an ancient Greek poet, has written upon this subject the

College Rhetoric finest epic poem that ever was. By the way, you are to know that an epic poem is a long poem upon some great event, or upon the action of some great man. 

The siege of Troy is so very famous for having lasted ten years, and also upon account of the great number of heroes who were there, that one must by no means be ignorant of such an event. When you are older, you will read it all in the Greek of Homer. 

Adieu! you are the best child in the world.

I return your letter corrected; for though it had but few faults, it is, however, proper that you should know them.

A LETTER OF APOLOGY 

My dear and valued Wegeler:

In what an odious light you have exhibited me to myself! OH! I acknowledge it; I do not deserve your friendship. It was no intentional or deliberate malice that induced me to act toward you as I did-but inexcusable thoughtlessness alone. 

I say no more. I am coming to throw myself into your arms, and to entreat you to restore me my lost friend; and you will give him back to me, to your patient, loving, and ever grateful

Sometimes, indeed, one unknown person brings another equally unknown, to recommend him; and sometimes they recommend one another! As to this gentleman, I must refer you to himself for his character and merits, with which he is certainly better acquainted than I can possibly › Op. cit., p. 52.

Letter-Writing 

257 be. I recommend him, however, to those civilities, which every stranger, of whom one knows no harm, has a right to; and I request you will do him all the good offices, and show him all the favors, that, on further acquaintance, you find him to deserve 

B. Franklin.10 Social Notes 

Social notes include formal and informal invitations, replies, and announcements. The formal invitations and announcements, especially for large gatherings, are usually printed or engraved; and the style and form of these are fixed by custom or are determined by the printer. The average person sending out formal invitations or announcements has little opportunity to express his own individuality in this type of correspondence. He must depend upon his printer or a reputable stationery dealer to dictate what is appropriate and correct. 

Invitations and announcements for small gatherings are generally written out by the one sending out the notes. 

The style may be similar to that of the printed or engraved notes used for large and formal occasions. There is at present, however, a tendency to allow a natural arrangement of the line of the communication. 

Formal notes should be written in the third person. 

They should contain no heading, no address of the correspondent, no salutation, no complimentary close, and no signature. All important information must be in the text of the note itself.

No abbreviations except Mr., Mrs., and Dr. are permitted in formal notes. Figures should not be used except for house and street numbers. Days of the week, names of the month, and the year (if this is given) should be written out in full. 

10From Benjamin Franklin, by John T. Morse, Houghton Mifflin Co.,

New York, 1889. By permission of, and arrangement with, the publishers.

College Rhetoric 

Replies should be in the form and style of the invitation. 

They should be written in the third person, and the date, the hour, and the place mentioned in the invitation should be repeated in the reply. It is not necessary to keep the same line arrangement used in the printed or engraved invitation, though it is correct to do so if one prefers this form. Invitations should be answered promptly. Custom demands that one send a reply not later than the second day after receiving an invitation.

Formal invitations, announcements, and replies should not be typed.

Very few punctuation marks are necessary in formal notes, especially for engraved or printed invitations and announcements, where an irregular line arrangement is used. The period should be used after unavoidable abbreviations; e.g., Mr., Mrs., and Dr., and at the close of the note. The commas should separate elements which would be ambiguous without them.

College Rhetoric Informal Notes 

Informal invitations, acceptances, and declinations, like the informal personal letter, may be chatty, intimate, and daringly personal; or they may be reserved, dignified, and literary. The tone and the form and style of the note will depend upon the relationship of the correspondents and the nature of the communication. 

A correct invitation is one which makes the recipient desire to accept. It must be marked by real friendliness and cordiality. The writer's intelligence and culture must guide him in conveying the correct meaning. Social notes must never offend; and because they are usually brief, one should exercise care in phrasing the message accurately.

T. H. Huxley.11 BON VOYAGE LETTER 

To Mr. Perkins 

Dear Sir:

I am much pleased that you are going on a very long journey, which may, by proper conduct, restore your health and prolong your life. 

Observe these rules: 

1. Turn all care out of your head as soon as you mount the chaise. 

11 From Life and Letters of Thomas Henry Huxley, by his son, Leonard 

Huxley, D. Appleton and Company, New York, 1900, vol. II, p. 8.

Letter-Writing 

261 

2. Do not think about frugality: your health is worth more than it can cost. 

3. Do not continue a day's journey to fatigue. 

4. Take now and then a day's rest. 

5. Get a smart sea-sickness, if you can. 

6. Cast away all anxiety, and keep your mind easy.

This last direction is the principal; with an unquiet mind, neither exercise, nor diet, nor physic can be of much use. 

I wish you, dear Sir, a prosperous journey, and a happy recovery. I am, dear Sir, your most affectionate humble servant,

CHAPTER XI Principles of  Description 

I remember him as if it were yesterday, as he came plodding to the inn door, his sea-chest following behind him in a hand-barrow: a tall, strong, heavy, nut-brown man; his tarry pigtail falling over the shoulders of his soiled blue coat; his hands ragged and scarred, with black, broken nails; and the saber cut across one cheek, a dirty, livid white. 

ERE is Robert Louis Stevenson's introduction of his character the "Old Sea Dog" in Treasure Island.¹

This is, to be sure, not the only nor necessarily the best model to follow in writing personal description; but it is arresting and effective-it will linger long in the memory. 

An outline is not difficult to make: 

1. Story teller's psychology. 

2. Identification (with keen verb phrases, came plodding); and atmosphere, in sea-chest. 

3. General descriptive strokes: tall, strong, heavy, nutbrown. 

4. Striking visual details: tarry pigtail, soiled blue coat; black, broken nails, leading up to the 

5. Illuminating hint: saber cut, dirty, livid white.

A number of characteristics of style may be observed: 

1 Quoted by courtesy of Charles Scribner's Sons, New York, publishers. 263

College Rhetoric 

1. The description is largely objective. Except for the first eight words, which are subjective, every unit creates a sensory image: inn door, sea-chest, hand-barrow. 

2. The appeal of color is strong: nut-brown man, tarry pigtail, soiled blue coat, black nails, saber cut a dirty, livid white. 

3. Nouns and adjectives are conjoined in unusual association: ragged hands is particularly daring. 

4. Rhythm is marked. Try scanning, as if the lines were 

And the sabler cut across one cheek 

A dirty, livid white. 

One could set it to a tune. 

5. Growth and climax are achieved: the images progress from the most general to the most individual, with a flash as of lightning at the end.

To show that the Old Sea Dog model may with some adjustment be used effectively in picturing persons of widely differing appearance, the following paragraphs, written by students, are appended. 

THE SCHOOL GIRL 

I well remember my first glimpse of her, as she came tripping down the road, her tin dinner bucket swinging in her hand: a straight, slender little girl of about seven years, with large blue eyes and close cut hair; the tassel of her "stocking" cap bobbing up and down on the shoulders of the short eider-down coat. A sprinkling of tiny freckles appeared upon her round cheeks and tilted nose; and as she turned and looked at me I saw that two of her front teeth were missing.

THE TRASH GATHERER 

I can close my eyes and see him yet, as he came laboring along the middle of the street: a crude, once-tall, now-

stooping man of sooty hue; his fuzzy black curls grudgingly hidden by the weather-beaten derby of a style long since forgotten; his preacher coat green-worn and ragged; the left hand pendulum-like swinging from his coat sleeve, the other stomping a cottonwood cane and jerking the string attached to a crazy, dirty, wooden box, wabbling behind him on two wheels. Exercise 1. Following the Stevenson model somewhat closely, describe a person, preferably one that you have seen; as, a blacksmith, a traffic officer, a ball player, a charwoman. 

2. Make a second personal description, discarding the outline and the general structure and order of sentence parts employed before. Try, however, to get equal intensity, brilliancy of images, and climax.

Selection of details. The poet Gray could make an entire evening landscape visible to a reader by the use of a few details of sense: a tolling bell, lowing cattle, a plodding plowman, a droning beetle, a complaining owl. Pick out salient points. Cigarette ashes on a coat lapel may be more significant than a page of perfectly valid information. 

Eugene Sue's Wandering Jew had a single heavy black eyebrow extending across the entire forehead. Throughout the thousand pages of Sue's great romance that eyebrow was like the refrain of a theme-song-inescapable, producing and unifying mood.

The following excerpts illustrate the use of vivifying detail: "Yes," said Captain Seth, a middle-aged little man with ear-rings. 

-H. W. CHAPLIN: Five Hundred Dollars. 

I was now near enough to see him; a very handsome figure and countenance, swarthy, lean, long, with a quick, alert, black look, as of one who was a fighter and ac-

College Rhetoric customed to command; upon one cheek he had a mole, not unbecoming; a large diamond sparkled on his hand. 

-R. L. STEVENSON: The Master of Ballantrae.2 

Rather under middle height, he was broad shouldered and deep chested, with a clean-shaven, red face with-not a mole but a slight protuberance the size of half a large pea on the line from the nostril to the corner of the mouth; bald over the crown and to the line a couple of inches above the ear; below that, thick and somewhat bushy hair of yellowish red, showing a mingling of gray. 

-E. N. WESTCOTT: David Harum.3 Exercise 

Write a personal description which shall include mention of one unusual detail of face or figure. 

Color. Color expressions play a large part in effective description, because color, especially in contrast, is stimulating to the imagination. Some studies are of such nature that color presents itself instantly as the inevitable basis of the visualization; as, for instance, a negro boy eating watermelon, where the dark skin of face and hands, the white of the eyeballs, and the green rind, deep red meat, and black seeds of the melon appear before the mind's eye upon slight effort.

A few of many possible examples of personal description in which color is the chief element are given here: 

Only while she passed did he see her as a gleam of color, a gypsy elf poorly clad, her bare feet flashing beneath a short green skirt, a twig of rowan berries stuck carelessly into her black hair. 

-JAMES M. BARRIE: The Little Minister. 

The man who accompanied him was apparently his servant; he rode a shaggy little gray pony, had a blue bonnet on his head, and a large check napkin folded about his neck, wore a pair of long blue worsted hose instead of

3 By courtesy of D. Appleton-Century Co., New York, publishers.

-SIR WALTER SCOTT: The Black Dwarf. 

A sunburnt, quick, lithe little man, though rather thick set. Earrings in his brown ears, white teeth lighting up his grotesque brown face; intensely black hair clustering about his brown throat; a ragged red shirt open at his brown breast; loose, seamanlike trousers, decent shoes, a long, red cap, a red sash round his waist, and a knife in it.

Description by interpretation. Thus far in our discussion of the principles of description, we have dwelt upon only those points of face and figure which are so sharply defined that they may be stated in objective terms. The arch of eyebrows, the outline of face, the color in cheek and eyes and hair, all these admit of description by terms of the senses. But the author may find such forms of expression inadequate for the depicting of the finer, more elusive qualities of face or feature. He is likely then to resort to another and radically different mode of portraiture, the primary characteristic of which is that it describes by naming the effect which the face or feature makes upon the beholder. For instance, if one says, "He had the most terrifying face I ever saw," the description is made not in terms of lines and color, but in terms of experience. The same may be said of a beautiful face, an irresolute look, a strong chin: beauty is not a property of objects but an emotion of the one who sees; and the words irresolute and strong re-

College Rhetoric cord each a judgment of the beholder. To illustrate more particularly, "a square chin" is objective, while “a strong chin" is subjective (or interpretative). Square is a property of the chin itself; strong is the beholder's interpretation of what the squareness indicates it records a judgment.

George Eliot's description of Daniel Deronda says nothing of lines or special features, but dwells upon the effect which a sight of him produces: 

There was a calm intensity of life and richness of tint in his face that on a sudden gaze from him was rather startling, and often made him seem to have spoken, so that servants and officials asked him automatically, "What did you say, sir?" when he had been quite silent.

In rare cases a bit of description, usually short, is made up entirely of subjective terms, as in Mr. Barrie's "Her loveliness struck him full in the eyes." More often interpretative terms are mingled with sensuous in the same paragraph, sometimes even in the same line; as in Thack eray's brief sketch of Becky Sharp: 

She was small and slight in person; pale, sandy-haired, and with eyes habitually cast down: when they looked up they were very large, odd and attractive. 

The last two adjectives are interpretative. 

The following, from Hawthorne's Blithedale Romance, shows the two modes used interchangeably with a completely artistic effect.

ZENOBIA 

I was taking note of Zenobia's aspect; and it impressed itself on me so distinctly, that I can now summon her up, like a ghost, a little wanner than the life, but otherwise identical with it. She was dressed as simply as possible, in an American print (I think the drygoods people call it so), but with a silken kerchief, between which and her gown there was one glimpse of a white shoulder.

It struck me as a great piece of good fortune that there should be just that glimpse. Her hair, which was dark, glossy, and of singular abundance, was put up rather soberly and primly, without curls, or other ornament, except a single flower. It was an exotic, of rare beauty, and as fresh as if the hot-house gardener had just clipped it from the stem. That flower has struck deep root into my memory. I can both see it and smell it, at this moment. 

So brilliant, so rare, so costly, as it must have been, yet enduring only for a day, it was more indicative of the pride and pomp which had a luxuriant growth in Zenobia's character than if a great diamond had sparkled among her hair. Exercise Write a paragraph of personal description in which you employ both objective and subjective elements. Indicate the words and phrases which illustrate each. 

Description of mood. Personal description by no means confines itself in story-writing to the portraiture of a subject at the beginning of a tale. In the representation of life people must be seen under changing conditions, in different attitudes, and in a variety of moods. 

To quote again from George Eliot: following her initial portrait of Mr. Grandcourt, she says,

Attempts at description are stupid: who can all at once describe a human being? Even when he is presented to us we only begin that knowledge of his appearance which must be completed by innumerable impressions under differing circumstances. We recognize the alphabet; we are not sure of the language.

Each of the descriptive passages thus far quoted is like a photograph taken in the operator's studio, with all appliances ready and the subject posed for the imprint; and each is especially suited to the early pages of the story. 

Later views are likely to be rather like the kodak "snapshot," which catches the characteristic gesture, the sudden

College Rhetoric passion, the humorous situation.

For illustration, see the following rapid sketches: 

The spirit of the conflict seemed to be alive in her. Her eyes were on fire; her cheeks had each a crimson spot, so exceedingly vivid, and marked by so definite an outline, that I at first doubted whether it were not artificial. 

The Master sprang to his feet like one transfigured; 

I had never seen the man look so beautiful. "A blow!" he cried, "I would not take a blow from God Almighty!"

Lessing's black eyes dilated and his pupils seemed little points of fire. Mellish noted that his ears were set forward unpleasantly, and that his upper lip was very long and reminiscent of the animal. 

Domsie clutched the letter and would have torn off the envelope. But he could not; his hand was shaking like an aspen. He could only look, and I read.

I glanced round at the serried ranks of warriors behind us, and somehow, all in an instant, began to wonder if my face looked like theirs. There they stood, their heads craned forward, the hands twitching, the lips apart, the fierce features instinct with the hungry lust of battle, and in the eyes a look like the glare of a bloodhound when he sights his quarry.

Description of personal experience. There are mental images other than those of sight. The mind as truly carries memories of sounds and odors as it does visual pictures. 

And spoken or written words may call up images of taste, thirst, nausea, pain. Weariness, weakness, and cold write themselves so indelibly upon the mind that they may be reinstated with something like the vividness of the first experience.

The following paragraph from Stevenson's The Misad ventures of John Nicholson* graphically portrays an experience of mind and body in which little or nothing of the sight element appears:

Whether his father spoke, and whether he remained long in the room or at once departed, are points lost to history. 

A horrid turmoil of mind and body; bursting sobs; broken, vanishing thoughts, now indignation, now remorse; broken elementary whiffs of consciousness, of the smell of horsehair on the chair bottom, of the jangling of church bells that now began to make day horrible, of the hard floor that bruised his knees, of the taste of tears that found their way into his mouth; for a period of time the duration of which I cannot guess, while I refuse to dwell longer on its agony, these were the whole of God's world for John Nicholson.

Description of scene. Principles embodied in the effective description of a person are found in that of a land- By courtesy of Charles Scribner's Sons, New York, publishers.

College Rhetoric scape, a street, the interior of a shop or home. The art consists in the setting forth of significant details plus a true interpretation of general spirit, the parts of the description. being arranged in effective order; that is, an order which produces an ascending interest rising to climax. 

The following, from Nathaniel Hawthorne's The Scarlet 

Letter, is a good subject for study:

But, before Mr. Dimmisdale had done speaking, a light gleamed far and wide over the muffled sky. It was doubtless caused by one of those meteors which the night-watcher may so often observe burning out to waste in the vacant regions of the atmosphere. So powerful was its radiance that it thoroughly illuminated the dense medium of cloud betwixt the sky and earth. The great vault brightened, like the dome of an immense lamp. It showed the familiar scene of the street with the distinctness of mid-day, but also with the awfulness that is always imparted to familiar objects by an unaccustomed light. The wooden houses, with their jutting stories and quaint gable-peaks; the doorsteps and thresholds, with the early grass springing up about them; the garden-plots, black with freshly turned earth; the wheel-track, little worn, and, even in the market-place, marginal with green on either side;-all were visible, but with a singularity of aspect that seemed to give another moral interpretation to the things of this world than they had ever borne before. And there stood the minister, with his hand over his heart; and Hester Prynne, with the embroidered letter glimmering on her bosom; and little

Pearl, herself a symbol, and the connecting-link between these two. They stood in the noon of that strange and solemn splendor, as if it were the light that is to reveal all secrets, and the daybreak that shall unite all who belong to one another.

It will be seen that the above passage is largely objective, the interpretative element appearing only at intervals, as in, "The awfulness that is always imparted to familiar objects by an unaccustomed light," "with a singularity of aspect that seemed to give another moral interpretation to the things of this world than they had ever borne before,' and "as if it were the light that is to reveal all secrets, and

the daybreak that shall unite all who belong to one another."

As in the other selection studied, the more general outlines come first; then the visible details-—quaint gablepeaks, garden-plots, and black earth, the wheel-track, the human figures; and all rises to climax in, "They stood in the noon of that strange and solemn splendor." Try making your sketch seem a part of a story. Either of the student exercises given below, both written on the subject of A Harvest Field by Moonlight, might easily be regarded as a cutting from a longer narrative. 

How long I lay awake I do not know. I was dozing, I think, when a cry or some moaning sound awoke me. I started up. All was quiet. The regular breathing of my bed-fellow, the quiet moonlight shining in my eyes, almost forced me to believe that the sound had been of my

College Rhetoric dreams. But no-it was too realistic, too startling for that.

Hurrying to the window and raising higher the half blind, I looked out. Not a breath was stirring. 

Not a leaf on the old maple jostled its fellow. I looked beyond, at the harvest-field. The motionless wheat stacks, scattered here and there, looked like grim sentinels. Or did their weird shadows, lengthened by the descending moon, furnish lurking places for-I knew not what? The stillness overwhelmed me. Silent and motionless I stood there, gazing.

The little girl could not get to sleep on this her first night in the country. The pale light of the moon shone through her bedroom window, and she thought she would like to see how the strange new world looked at night. 

What were those little tents she saw lying before her? 

Why, the homes of Brownies, to be sure! Perhaps, if she waited a little while, they would come out and play with each other on that nice, clear place in front. But what was that great dark thing farther back? What did it mean by standing there so big and still? 

A trembling little girl crept hurriedly back again into her sheltering bed.

Nature in background and interpretation. Pure nature description is properly rare in narrative literature. Most readers require a fairly constant stir of action, which the lengthy picturing of scenes forbids. Nature, however, as an aid in the painting of the human subject is just about indispensable. The delicate art of Thomas Hardy so fuses the moods of skies and heaths and woods with those of his human characters as to make them seem part and parcel of one another. In such a passage as the following, from 

Hardy's Under the Greenwood Tree, both men and scene are quickened into vivid life, and glow together in the reader's consciousness:

Within the woody pass, at a level anything lower than the horizon, all was dark as the grave .. At the termina- 

5 By courtesy of Harper & Bros., New York, publishers.

tion of the wood, the white surface of the lane revealed itself between the dark hedge-rows, like a ribbon jagged at the edges 

Having escaped both trees and hedge, he could now be distinctly seen rising against the sky, his profile appearing on the light background like the portrait of a gentleman in black cardboard. It assumed the form of a lowcrowned hat, an ordinary-shaped nose, an ordinary chin, an ordinary neck, and ordinary shoulders. What he consisted of farther down was invisible from lack of sky low enough to picture him on Shuffling, halting, irregular footsteps of various kinds were now heard, coming up the hill from the dark interior of the grove, and presently there emerged, severally, five men of different ages and gaits, all of them working villagers of the parish of Mellstock. They too had lost their rotundity, and advanced against the sky in flat outlines.

The first was a bowed and bent man who carried a fiddle under his arm The next was Mr. Robert Penny, boot and shoe-maker, a little man, rather round shouldered . . . His features were invisible, yet when he occasionally looked round, two faint moons of light gleamed for an instant from the precincts of his eyes, denoting that he wore spectacles of a circular form.

The third was Elias Spinks, who walked perpendicularly and dramatically. The fourth outline was that of 

Joseph Bowman, who had now no distinctive appearance beyond that of a human being. Finally came a weak, lathlike form, trotting and stumbling along with one shoulder forward and his head inclined to the left, his arms dangling nervelessly in the wind as if they were empty sleeves. 

This was Thomas Leaf. 

They all advanced between the varying hedges and the trees dotting them here and there, kicking their toes occasionally among the crumpled leaves. Soon appeared glimmering indications of the few cottages forming the small hamlet of Lewgate, for which they were bound, while the faint sound of church bells ringing a Christmas peal could be heard floating over upon the breeze from the direction of Mintfield parish on the other side of the hills. 

A mode constantly in the employ of the descriptive artist is the use of natural scenes and events in the interpretation

College Rhetoric of the mood of a character. The storm-scene in King Lear interprets in terms of the objective the greater storm in the breast of the outraged old king.

The following, taken from Ian Maclaren's Beside the Bonny Brier Bush, illustrates this mirroring of a man's mood in the landscape. The student will see that the second paragraph marks a subtle change from despair to hope. The character, Drumsheugh, has been assisting the 

Doctor at the bedside of a man who is near death, and has been sent out into the night for a "breath o' air."

It was the hour before daybreak and Drumsheugh wandered through fields he had trodden since childhood. 

The cattle lay sleeping in the pastures; their shadowy forms, with a patch of whiteness here and there, having a weird suggestion of death. He heard the burn running over the stones; fifty years ago he had made a dam that lasted till winter. The hooting of an owl made him start; one had frightened him as a boy so that he ran home to his mother-she died thirty years ago. The smell of ripe corn filled the air; it would soon be cut and garnered. 

He could see the dim outline of his house, all dark and cold; no one he loved was beneath the roof, the lighted window in Saunders' cottage told where a man hung between life and death, but love was in that home. The futility of life arose before this lonely man, and overcame his heart with an indescribable sadness. What a vanity was all human labor, what a mystery all human life! 

But while he stood, a subtle change came over the night, and the air trembled round him as if one had whispered. 

Drumsheugh lifted his head and looked eastward. A faint gray stole over the distant horizon, and suddenly a cloud reddened before his eyes. The sun was not in sight, but was rising, and sending forerunners before his face. The cattle began to stir, a blackbird burst into song, and before Drumsheugh crossed the threshold of Saunders' house, the first rays of the sun had broken on a peak of the Grampians. human life. The novels of Thomas Hardy and James Lane

Atmosphere. A principal use of nature-description is that by which it provides an effective medium for the events of a narrative. The term "atmosphere," in the literary sense, is used to express the general feeling, or sentiment, experienced by the reader of a story; or, more objectively, the setting, the surroundings of a piece, so far as they affect the reader's sensibilities. For instance, a ghost story is best conceived in an atmosphere of the weird and fantastical, of deep woods and lonely moors and untenanted houses; Poe's The Black Cat is placed in the pitch-darkness of a subterranean dungeon; the description of a dull afternoon prepares the mind for a story of somber and hopeless events, and that of twilight for a reflective tale of tender sentiment. 

In his A Christmas Carol Dickens contrives to keep the reader aware, at every step in the progress of events, of the reality of the Christmas season. Mention of the intense cold of the weather occurs with persistent repetition, while one is not allowed to forget the holly and the mistletoe, and the great fires and the feasts and the glamor of 

Christmastide. 

It was cold, bleak, biting weather: foggy withal: and he could hear the people in the court outside go wheezing up and down, beating their hands upon their breasts, and stamping their feet upon the pavement stones to warm them. The city clocks had only just gone three, but it was quite dark already-and candles were flaring in the windows of the neighboring offices, like ruddy smears upon the palpable brown air . . . The brightness of the shops where holly sprigs and berries crackled in the damp heat of the windows made pale faces ruddy as they passed. . . A mighty blaze went roaring up the chimney . . . There were great, round, pot-bellied baskets of chestnuts, shaped like the waist-coats of jolly gentlemen, lolling at the doors.

College Rhetoric Exercise 1. Read Poe's The Fall of the House of Usher. Define the nature of the atmosphere, and observe how the introduction contributes to the emotional effect of the whole. 

2. In the same way analyze Kipling's In Flood Time (to be found in the volume entitled In Black and White). 

3. Write an "atmosphere" sketch of your own, as if you were introducing a story. Avoid hackneyed subjects.

Characterization 

It has been remarked, in effect, that the normal reader is interested, from first to last, in people rather than in any other possible subject. The "human interest" is the animating principle of the story. It is well-nigh impossible to make any description so beautiful or so sublime as to hold the eager attention of the average mind unless at least the suggestion of a human presence is given. Of absolute and final importance, then, are the persons of the story. They must be clear-cut, actual people-sharply defined, convincing, real. On finishing a well-written novel, the reader finds that the characters of it have become his own intimate acquaintances. He almost expects to meet Mr. Micawber and Becky Sharp on the streets of his own home village or John Ridd and Bud Means putting up hay in the meadow lot.

Adequate description of the outer man-form and feature is a first and important step in the process of forming a strong impression of personality. However, it is merely one step. It is the beginning of acquaintance. The reader's interest is aroused. He now desires to know more of this man he has met-his powers and weaknesses, his ambitions, his hates, his peculiarities, his inner ego. 

It should be remarked, just here, that intimate acquaintance with a character must be gained at first hand. A

friend may tell you that a person is eccentric or cruel or sweet-tempered or opinionated, but you are not satisfied with these general terms-because the adjectives embody the final judgments of the speaker. You would rather have the evidences furnished you and be allowed to form your own conclusions. To say that a certain man put a quarter into the contribution box and took out twenty cents in "change" is for this reason a stronger means of characterizing him than merely to declare that he is stingy. 

It is true that characterization by interpretation is a valuable aid in the telling of a story, but the indirect method, that of presenting to the reader the evidence from which he may form his own estimates, is of prime importance, and should have the first place in our studies. 

Some writers of cheap fiction make the same mistake in characterization that they make in personal description. 

As they describe by adjectives-"queenly," "beautiful," "statuesque"-they characterize by adjectives-"noble," "unselfish," "brave as a lion." But the real artist rarely thinks of introducing his hero with a procession of qualifying expressions, for this is not the usual way of nature. 

Character by evidence. How, then, does one become acquainted with a man in real life? One sees him at the railway station, and notes his face, his walk, his watchchain, perhaps. One brushes against him on the street, sees him buy fruit at the corner, observes his treatment of a beggar. One sits behind him at the concert and hears his comments on the music and the performers. Perhaps one is privileged to see him once more, in the presence of a crisis of some sort, perhaps a fire or a robbery or a lynching. One has analyzed his features; has heard him speak; has seen him act; and consciously or unconsciously one has formed an opinion.

The good story-writer will show the reader his people, will let him hear them speak and see them act-and from

College Rhetoric the many evidences he furnishes, with a little direct help in interpretation, perhaps, will let him draw his own conclusions.

The evidences from which one in real life forms a judgment respecting the nature of a person are of infinite variety in kind and of infinite range in value. For example, we may make up our estimate of a man from his selection of a wife, his party politics, or his method of affixing a postage stamp; and we may form a partial judgment from the fact that he shaves only once a week and talks loud through the telephone. A chance remark may so distinctly characterize a man that people will quote it years after it was made, as affording the clearest insight into his nature.

In the literary narrative, deeds, utterances, characteristic habits are made to body forth the person until we finally come to feel acquainted with his inner spirit and know him, perhaps, as intimately as we know our friends. For example, here are some of the touches with which William Allen White characterizes a person called Etherwylde Swaney, a girl employed as typesetter in the office of a town paper:

Right at the busy hour of the afternoon we used to catch her with a type in her fingers and her hand poised in the air, looking off into space for a minute at a time, and when we spoke to her she would put her head on her case and cry softly, and the foreman would have to apologize to her before she would go back to work. And then she would have to take the broken piece of looking-glass that she kept in her capital "K" box and make an elaborate toilet before settling down .. She was eternally drying her hands and talking to one of the men 

We put up with her perfumery and her powder rags and we did all we could to teach her the difference between a comma and a period-though she never really learned; and we were still patient with her when she deliberately pied a lot of type after being corrected for some piece of carelessness or worse. . 

Her mother came to our office one winter

day in her black straw hat with a veil around it, and with the coat she had worn for ten years, and told us that she was afraid working in the shop would hurt her daughter's social standing. So when the Princess walked out that night in a gust of musk-in her picture hat and automobile cloak, with her bangles tinkling and her petticoat swishing the office knew her no more forever . . It was known in the neighborhood that the Princess dragged her shoestrings till noon, and that her bed was never made up in the daytime in the memory of man . . Though women sniffed at her, the travelling men on the veranda of the 

Hotel Metropole craned their necks to watch her out of sight. She jingled with chains, and watches, and lockets and chatelaines, and she carried more rings than a cane rack, and walked with the air of the heroine of the society drama at the opera house." 

...

The foregoing portraits of character are taken from published literature. But it is important for the student to reflect that he himself may and probably does know per- By courtesy of Charles Scribner's Sons, New York, publishers. 

8 By courtesy of Charles Scribner's Sons, New York, publishers.

sons who, if properly presented, would prove quite as interesting. The more common traits of character-prodigality, ostentation, ignorance, cruelty, naïveté, faithfulness, generosity, courage-are present in the community life of every city and the smallest village and rural district. 

It ought not to seem difficult to build a story about the 

Arkansas janitor who said, "The last opery we uns had in this town was Johnson's Trained Mice"; or about the housewife who, having read a recipe for cake, rushed to the grocery store and asked for a package of "ingredients"; or the girl who declared that she had read "Romeo" but not "Juliette."

The woman who selects the books for her new library by the color of the bindings, the man who maintains that the sacred scriptures were originally written in English, the widow who goes out to do washing in order that her daughter may have a fine dress for Commencement, the boy who rides his bicycle on the top rail of the iron bridge, are everyday people of village life. All the characters necessary to the making of a dozen novels are numbered among the acquaintances of any adult person.

Character by interpretation. Thus far in our study of the art of depicting character and mood, the stress has been laid on what may be termed the objective method, by which the author presents the evidence merely, respecting the nature or the emotional state of the person treated, leaving the reader to draw his own conclusions. But, just

College Rhetoric as there is a subjective method of description, so there is a subjective method in the portrayal of character and of mood. When Dickens tells us that "Scrooge was a squeezing, wrenching, grasping, scraping, clutching, covetous old sinner!" the statement furnishes not material, but a product; not evidence, but a judgment. 

To make the distinction plainer, if need be, contrast the above characterization of Scrooge with the following: "Scrooge had a very small fire, but the clerk's fire was so very much smaller that it looked like one coal. But he couldn't replenish it, for Scrooge kept the coal-box in his own room; and so surely as the clerk came in with the shovel, the master predicted that it would be necessary for them to part. Wherefore the clerk put on his white comforter and tried to warm himself at the candle." It will be seen that the method of characterization here is the indirect, or objective, one, evidence being furnished from which the reader is expected to make up his own judgment.

If one asks which of the two methods is preferable, it may be said that the two are complementary rather than antagonistic. The characteristic act or speech is indispensable in rendering the sharply defined impression of a person, while the studied analysis, recorded in a finished estimate, is important in the rounding out of a complete personal study. Those modern writers who have succeeded best in the intense method of characterization by the indirect hint have in no way despised the method by interpretation. The two modes are combined in a single sentence in "Oor herts were as water afore Tammas' words, an' a' saw the doctor shake in his saddle."

Exercise 1. In the following passages discriminate between the two literary modes contrasted above; tell which expressions are objective, which subjective:

(a) She had liked his eyes. They became unbearable; they dwelt in the memory as if they had left a phosphorescent line. (b) His good qualities were drenched in the first person singular. (c) The tears sprang to the Celt's eyes. (d) The sudden consciousness struck her a buffet. (e) The gentleman turned to his retinue and said calmly: "Lash me these animals to their kennels!" The mob vanished instantly. (f) A ghastly paleness overspread his features. There was a convulsive movement of his throat. (g) Holingsworth looked at me fiercely, and with glowing eyes. He could not have shown any other kind of expression than that, had he meant to strike me with a sword. (h) In the hurry of the moment she did not examine a lightning terror that shot through her. 

2. Select, from standard literature, half-a-dozen examples of the direct, or subjective, interpretation of mood and character. 

Plot 

Desire. The basic principle of plot is desire. The reader must want something; where plot interest is strong, he wants it poignantly, desperately. Whether the motif, the general theme, be adventure (as in Treasure Island), or love (as in Lorna Doone), or mystery (as in The Murders of the Rue Morgue), the animating principle is desire: in adventure for success and safe return; in love for mating; in mystery for solution.

Here is an observable analogy between literature and life. Life is a succession of desires and satisfactions. A man goes into the field and works, and the desire grows within him for rest and food. He eats and sleeps, and the cycle of experience is complete. The enjoyment of music is based on the same ground. The dissonance creates the

College Rhetoric desire, the chord satisfies it. Strike the so-called "chord of the dominant seventh" (which is a dissonance) and the ear must have the only possible satisfaction; namely, the chord based on the tonic. Where this satisfaction is delayed through a series of suspensions, the experience is one of "hope deferred," as in a continued story. We must not make the mistake of thinking that pleasure is realized from satisfaction alone. The laborer enjoys the work as truly as the rest. The expectant craving of the dissonance is as keen a joy as the chord into which it is resolved. The reader's agony of suspense is as truly enjoyed by him as the satisfactory adjustment of the hero's difficulties. The wise reader will control the impulse to turn to the back of the book with a view to finding out immediately how the story "turned out." He will prefer continued anticipation to the premature satisfaction of desire. 

How may an author make the desire of the reader clear and strong? For answer note the method employed in a great work of fiction. The desire in Les Misérables is that 

Jean Valjean shall be redeemed from evil ways and recompensed for the bitter experiences of an unjust fate. It is the author's purpose that there shall be no sort of indifference on the part of the reader, that he shall not mildly wish the well-being of the unfortunate man; he must crave it with his whole soul, cry out for it, agonize over it. To inspire this desire Victor Hugo has resorted to every phase of literary art, particularly to characterization and description, shot through with an intense poetic fervor, with a continued suggestion of broad, humane philosophy.

First one sees Jean Valjean himself surrounded by the hard environment of a French peasant in the days of monstrous iniquities of social caste and unjust laws. From the character hints and interpretation one finds him to be a man of rude violence, but of tender heart. Then ensues

the situation of the starving children; our imagination is wrought upon by their cry for bread. The principle of "interchange of identity" has already begun to operate: the reader has become Jean Valjean, and through his hungry eyes sees the baker's loaf temptingly displayed in the window. From this point forward, vivid description plays a large part in the development of our desire. We endure with the man the injustice of the crushing penalty; experience with him the horrors of the galleys, and suffer with him the brutalizing of all the tender instincts. Then comes the crowning bitterness of a release from captivity and the inhumanity of inn-keepers and citizens. When at last we view the man from the standpoint of the good bishop, we are ready to forgive, as the bishop does, every act of wicked ingratitude, and with undivided hearts to crave his redemption and his happiness.

Whatever stirs our sympathies for a character tends to make us feel his necessities and to desire what we believe is best for him. The suffering from poverty and the rumor of hidden treasure make us crave success in an adventurous project; the cruelty of an unjust suspicion and the known or suspected innocence of the accused man combine to make us desire his vindication. The point of the present discussion for the young writer or the young reader is a realization of the power which a narrative gains in the creation of a strong and clear-cut desire. Let the writer produce the desire by any art known to him, but produce it he must. Exercise 1. Discuss in a paragraph the means by which the reader's desire is brought about in any one of the following stories: 

Ben Hur, Uncle Tom's Cabin, Ramona, A Tale of Two Cities, or in any other novel you have read. 

2. Read a number of the short narratives of animal life by 

Ernest Thompson Seton and Charles G. D. Roberts. Observe

College Rhetoric that a strong and clear-cut desire is conceived in the course of the first few sentences of each sketch. Be able to show how this is done.

Obstacles. A story in which desire were satisfied without hindrance would, merely as a story, be unreadable; just as a career (if such could be imagined) in which there were no foes to fight, knots to untie, issues to await, would be unlivable. Plot interest is one of suspense; the wish to surmount obstructions is what makes a reader loath to drop the book till he has come to "a good stopping place."

As to the number and nature of these obstacles, they may be few or many, objective or subjective. Storm, drouth, the sea, failing eyesight, misunderstanding, caste, business failure, accident, moral imperfection, the law, social custom, absence-any of these or any combination of these may be erected as a barrier to hope. They may appear together or singly. It is necessary to continued interest, however, that after the appearance of the first difficulty there shall be no point in the story previous to the dénouement when there is not present before the mind of the reader at least one such barrier. The fewer "good stopping places" in a story, the better.

Plot management. The highest aim of the story writer's art cannot be said to have been fairly achieved unless throughout every portion of the tale the interest of the intelligent reader be perfectly sustained. And the first principle of story composition, the principle which, in its observance, makes this sustained interest possible, is unity; this, as we shall see, embraces selection and proportion. 

Let an author encourage too far his bent for fanciful description, or for pedantic instruction, and the reader will find his interest flagging and may properly ask, What has this to do with the story? Unity is the name we give to the quality of a composition by which every part has a bearing on the central idea; and if in rhetoric this is im-

portant for logical reasons, it is equally important in narration in that it conserves the reader's attention and directs it to a predetermined end. 

The safe maxim for the management of a story plot is this: make the action the unit of the piece. Some novelists contrive to let the reader feel that every descriptive touch and every apparently discursive remark in some way promotes the progress of the story. Even so seemingly valuable a literary effect as a strong character hint is worse than valueless unless it have bearing on incidents actually in progress or of later development. Some of the actual workings of this principle of unity based on action may be pointed out as follows: 

1. Unity indicates the line of selection. It is evident that in a story the complete imitation of life in all its details, almost or quite unrelated as many of them may be. to any trend of thought or purpose, would be impossible; and if such reciting of details were possible, the result would be unreadable. The art of the author must operate in picking out the significant days of the life, the significant moments of the day; and in stringing them on that thread of relationship known as the story-action. 

2. Unity makes a virtue of necessity by binding up the essential story elements into one common bundle. For instance, take the case of what may be called "antecedent information" (respecting conditions and events preceding the actual opening of the story). Good art suppresses everything of this nature except what is absolutely essential, and contrives, by a variety of means, to build the little that remains into the substance of the theme. Likewise, description and characterization and the interpretation of thought and feeling, things so essential to the variety and dignity of the whole composition, are carefully inwrought with the fabric of the tale.

College Rhetoric 

3. By the close correlation of character and action the quality of plausibility, necessary as it is to the reader's satisfaction and interest, is realized. The art of the best masters of fiction imitates life in this, that no person of theirs ever acts "out of character"; that is, does anything not warranted and rendered inevitable by the laws of his own nature. 

4. A proper regard for unity maintains action in its rightful place as the foremost consideration of the writer. 

Said Robert Louis Stevenson:

It is not character but incident that woos us out of our reserve. 

Something happens as we desire to have it happen to ourselves; some situation that we have long dallied with in fancy is realized in the story with enticing or appropriate details. Then we forget the characters; then we push the hero aside; then we plunge into the tale in our own person and bathe in fresh experience; and then, and then only, do we say we have been reading a romance.⁹ 

Stevenson's biographer, Robert Bridges, adds, with pertinent meaning:

How he labored to make every incident fit into his general scheme is shown in many of his letters. To a suggestion that he change a certain ending, he replied that every incident in the story had led up to that. An invalid for half his years, he looked on life and art with the eye of a man of action. The psychology of a character interested him, as it naturally would the descendant of the metaphysical Balfours. But no amount of analysis was sufficient in Stevenson's view to reveal a character to his readers. Action was the mirror in which it was reflected.10 Exercise 

1. State how much, in some novel that you have read, you have "skipped" because it was irrelevant to the story.
