Remarks on the Composition of a Short Story 

Of the students who read these pages, it is probable that all, or nearly all, have at some time practiced the writing of stories. Some have doubtless had special training in this line of work and have succeeded well with their original productions; others may have attempted narration only in a desultory sort of way and may have found considerable difficulty in conceiving and in executing a story theme. It is hoped that a few suggestions given here, following the detailed study of preceding lessons, may tend to increase the student's confidence in his own ability to write a good story, and may augment his appreciation of whatever is of worth in this growing department of literature. The following remarks will be addressed to one who, as he is setting about the task of writing a first story, wishes to have clearly in mind the principles which govern such composition.

The theme. The best possible material for your story exists in your own experience. Think over a number of incidents in your own life that you remember with the most lively interest. Did you ever, as a child, run away from home? Or did some little nephew or niece of yours, or a neighbor's child, run away? Did you ever live in the country, and see a little girl with a braid of yellow hair down her back spell down the neighborhood at a "spelling school"? Or did you ever know a woman who took her little boy to the city doctor to see whether his eyesight could be saved? Did you ever know two old people who loved each other; or a boy who worked in a grocery store; or an old lady who wanted to go back home on a visit?

College Rhetoric 

Did you ever know a man who needed a job, or an overcoat?

One whose son did not respect him? One who feared his wife had cancer? A boy who wanted a pony, or a mouthorgan? A girl who had a twisted foot, or who sang off pitch? Do not begin with a love-story, or a tragedy of fatal accident or murder. Write something simple. Idealize your incidents somewhat, if you care to do so, but get your theme out of life. 

Inventing a plot. Do not be alarmed by the dread necessity of having a plot for your story. Do not let the term carry so great weight with you as to suggest insurmountable difficulties. You need only to set yourself consciously to the task of creating a simple desire in your reader and the surmounting, by natural means, of the obstacles to this desire. The desire may be directed toward no more important end than that a little girl shall play her piece well at the public recital; or that a certain boy shall pass the examination; or that the old man shall not lose his place as janitor in the court house but some such desire there must be, with its obstacles and its fulfillment or non-fulfillment, if your composition is not to lack the proper story element. 

Make your plot simple. Let the time covered by the story incidents be short-not longer than a day or a week. 

Do not waste your own and the reader's energy on unnecessary "antecedent information." Let there be one "situation" (and only one) where the lines of interest are drawn and the suspense as great as you can make it. 

The descriptive element. Make considerable use of description. Begin your story with a descriptive passage and continue with a descriptive setting for your incidents. 

Let the atmosphere of your piece be not overdone, but distinctive. Keep your imagination alive. See clearly every person and every scene. Hear the voice of every one you cause to speak.

The style. Put as much as you can of thought and suggestion into every sentence. Make it glow. Resolve to make your story attractive for the charm and purity of your English. Avoid the too frequent use of any important word. Strive for variety and grace in the structure of your sentences.

Avoid slang, unless you are writing a story in which slang is "in character." Avoid absurd and grotesque expressions. Do not attempt to be funny unless you can be sure that your fun is chaste and natural. Swagger and coarseness are not art. Try to keep your attempts at humor and pathos from being too evident. Tell your story simply and sincerely, being careful at all times to be interested in it yourself. Exercise Write a story of not fewer than 500 words nor more than 1500 on a theme of your own selection. The following list of subjects is given in the hope that some of them may prove suggestive. The Cultivation of Taste 

Many a young reader laments his inability to appreciate the greatest or the choicest things in literature. He would like to see what others profess to see in those literary products that are called "standard" and "classical," but is obliged to admit that his personal choice is for the lighter novels and the catch magazine stories of the time. One may say, for instance, that George Eliot's Daniel Deronda is "beyond me," is too deep, or is tiresome. To such a one, if he be a student who has faithfully followed through the course of our studies in narration, a few words of counsel may, in a spirit of sympathy, be offered here. 

In the first place, it must be evident to the inquirer who has made any considerable study of the subject, that the difference between good and bad (or good and less good), between great and insignificant, is not so much a difference of the modes used by different writers as of their skill and taste in handling the modes, and combining elements. 

There is a further difference, too, in theme as well as in treatment, a difference in writers' ideals and purposes, their knowledge of the world, their grasp of the principles of thought and life.

Very many, if not most, of the current stories, would be admitted by their authors to be transitory in theme, not serious or profound, but flippant and "smart" in treatment. 

This sort of literature is "current" in every sense-it keeps pace with passing events and popular interests and fads. 

Stories of bicycle-riders give place to those of automobilists and airmen, as one sort of conveyance gives place to another in popular use. And so very modern in spirit has story-writing become, that the tendency is for every man

College Rhetoric to have his specialty; and one writes stories of horseracing, another of business complications, another of political jobbery, another of love-at-first-sight. Upon one who, feeling the fascination of the more superficial stories, is yet desirous of improving his taste for forms more artistic and themes more enduring, two injunctions may be urged. 

The first of these is-Give the classic author a fair chance with you. Sit down deliberately with your copy, let us say, of Middlemarch or Giants in the Earth; resolve to expend every bit of the time and effort necessary to the mastery of the thought and the appreciation of the beauty. 

It is likely that the great themes of heroism and sacrifice and growth of character through suffering will be able, finally, to stir you to deep thoughts and purposes; and the subtle charm of a style that with all its vigor and richness is severe and chaste will come to win your heartiest admiration. Do not think you must read "to improve your mind" but learn to read with the deliberate intention of getting the rich experience of satisfaction that others get. 

The second injunction is, Give yourself a chance with the classic author. One's appreciation of a literary product is certain to be limited by the bounds of his own experience. 

Perhaps, to be really candid, you have not yet lived long enough and widely enough to be able to understand George Eliot's analysis and philosophy. Perhaps even the vocabulary of the author is in some places beyond you. Do not give up. Begin to study and work hard and look about you and see life-and then try it again, a year-five years hence. Teach yourself to inquire into the deep mysteries of language and learn to love the noble English in its purity. Some day you may be proud of intellectual kinship with Tennyson and Shakespeare, and the austere beauties of George Eliot will make all but the very best of the later fiction seem cheap and vulgar in comparison.

CHAPTER XII  

Meter

HYTHM, or measure, is everywhere in the processes of animal existence: in periodic work and rest; in the alternate supply and waste of body tissue; in the intake and outgo of breath; in the systole and diastole of the heart. 

The same principle governs the programs of our lives: there is a daily rhythm of sleeping and waking, of tasks and meals; and there are rhythms of days in the week and seasons in the year. Life sets itself to a rhythm and saves itself by so doing. Things done rhythmically are done economically—with the least loss of force. A breaking of the rhythm—the postponement of a dinner or foregoing of a night's rest-causes nervous disturbance, headache, a slowing up of progress. 

We make rhythms for ourselves, or they are made for us by our constitutions. This must be so if we are to live and get on in life. Rhythmic organization is adjustment to necessity. We have a rhythm of walking, of closing and opening our eyes, of hearing and learning. Children set the multiplication table to a tune, and learn the respective lengths of months by a jingle: 

Thirty days hath September, April, June, and November.

We march to drum beats, sing one another to sleep, print our wall papers in recurring patterns, and vote for phrases: 297

College Rhetoric Tippecanoe 

And Tyler too! 

Keep cool 

With Coolidge!

In conventional poetry, rhythm is the base of the following phenomena: meter, rhyme, refrain, stanza. Meter is the division of a line into rhythmical units called feet by the regular recurrence of stress, or accent. Stressed (accented) syllables are called "long"; unstressed syllables, "short." The terms long and short are more specifically used of the character of the syllable itself. The Latin lēges consists of two long syllables, both vowels being long. 

In the present discussion, however, the terms long and stressed are used interchangeably, as are short and unstressed. The grouping of long and short syllables into differing types of succession gives rise to the various socalled "meters," chief of which are the iambic, the trochaic, the dactylic, and the anapæstic. IAMBIC (~~) The curfew tolls | the knell | of part|ing day. 

The curve () marks a short (unstressed) syllable, the bar (-) a long (stressed) syllable. An iambus is a foot which consists of a short syllable followed by a long one. 

Iambic verse is verse in which the iambic foot predominates.

The terms monometer (one-measure), dimeter (twomeasure), trimeter (three-measure), tetrameter (fourmeasure), pentameter (five-measure), hexameter (sixmeasure), septameter (seven-measure), and octometer (eight-measure) may be used in place of one stress, two stresses, etc. The former apply more technically, however, to meters in classical verse, where length of syllable, rather than accent, determines the classification of the foot.

Rhyme

Rhyme is an aid in dividing metrical units. The recurrence of rhyme sounds at regular intervals both excites the ear and rests the mind. These sounds serve as a sort of sensuous punctuation. At each of them the reader or hearer pauses, is refreshed, and gets a new start. 

Rhyme syllables show (1) identity of the root vowel sound and consonants following, and (2) difference of consonants preceding: cold, mold (o, ld identical; c and m different). But cold and cold do not rhyme, nor do mold and mold. Mere identity of vowel sound does not constitute rhyme. The following are not rhymed syllables: am, can; bold, slow; bridle, stifle. Resemblance of vowel sound which is short of identity is not sufficient for rhyme. The following do not rhyme: west, haste; bend, stand; narrow, tomorrow; wonder, yonder. The verbs strive and live do not rhyme: while the vowel characters are identical the sounds are different. The two used together constitute an eye-rhyme merely. 

Feminine rhyme. Flitting and sitting constitute a "feminine" rhyme, the rhyme syllables being followed by an added identical syllable. The feminine rhyme, in addition to playing an important part in serious poetry, offers, possibilities for ear-tickling extravagance. Witness the following pairs employed with humorous effect by James 

Russell Lowell: junk to all punctooal

Cæsar bread an' cheese air Florida horrider levyin' taxes jest wut I axes

Rhyme within the line, where it occurs effect of dividing a longer unit into 

Oh, the circus-day parade! How the bugles played and played! 

And how the glossy horses tossed their flossy manes and neighed,

As the rattle and the rhyme of the tenor-drummer's time 

Filled all the hungry hearts of us with melody sublime! 

-JAMES WHITCOMB RILEY: "The Circus-Day Parade."* 

Parade and played and rhyme and time are evenly placed; whereas glossy and flossy-conforming to no space scheme constitute, perhaps, an instance of "unintended," or "accidental," internal rhyming.

Refrain. Repetition of a phrase, a line, or a whole stanza at regular intervals constitutes refrain (a “breaking back" to an earlier set of sounds). Refrain is particularly effective in poems which are to be sung. Often the refrain is the only part of a song which all the singers know, in which case it constitutes a "chorus" in which all join: 

In the good old summer time!

Tramp, tramp, tramp, the boys are marching! 

Why not? Why not? Why not come to Him now?

With each repetition emotion rises higher, voices become more surcharged with feeling. 

The modified refrain may have a special appeal, as in- 

Kingsley's "Three Fishers": 

And the harbor bar be moaning— 

Though the harbor bar be moaning- 

Then goodbye to the bar and its moaning!

The stanza pattern. The stanza is a succession of lines representing a unit within the poem. Normally, each 

*From Rhymes of Childhood, copyright 1890 and 1918. Used by special permission of the publishers, The Bobbs-Merrill Company.

stanza should be typical in structure, and hence identical in form with every other stanza. There should be a lineby-line agreement; e.g., if line 2 in stanza 1 is short, line 

2 in stanza 2 should be short; if in stanza 1 line 2 contains internal rhyme and line 3 does not, the same phenomena should be repeated in later stanzas. 

Let's contend no more, Love, Strive nor weep: 

All be as before, Love, Only sleep! 

What so wild as words are? 

I and Thou 

In debate, as birds are, Hawk on bough!

Observe, in the above, that lines 2 and 4 are short in both stanzas; and that in both the last word of lines 1 and 

3 is not rhymed but identical. The same scheme of structure was continued by the poet (Robert Browning) throughout the ten stanzas of the poem, the title of which is "A Woman's Last Word." 

But yesterday I looked away 

Where happy lands in sunshine lay

In golden blots inlaid with spots 

Of shade and wild forget-me-nots. 

And cool and sweet my naked feet

Found dewy pathways through the wheat, And out again where down the lane The dust was dimpled with the rain. 

Oh, lad and lass and orchard pass, And briered lane and daisied grass! 

Oh, gleam and gloom and woodland bloom, And breezy breaths of all perfume.

Note 9-Again as commonly pronounced does not rhyme perfectly with lane, though rhyme is clearly intended here. 

The above is from James Whitcomb Riley's "A Song of Yesterday." Each stanza consists of two rhymed couplets, 

*From Rhymes of Childhood, copyright 1890 and 1918. Used by special permission of the publishers, The Bobbs-Merrill Company.

College Rhetoric showing internal rhyme in lines 1 and 3, but not in lines 

2 and 4.

Rhyme schemes. The stanza most commonly seen consists of four lines rhyming a b c b: 

I heard a thousand blended notes,

While in a grove I late reclined, In that sweet mood when pleasant thoughts 

Bring sad thoughts to the mind. 

-WORDSWORTH. 

The rhyme effect of the quatrain may be heightened by the a ba b: 

Fare thee well! and if for ever, Still for ever, fare thee well:

Even though unforgiving, never 'Gainst thee shall my heart rebel. 

-BYRON. 

A stanza scheme sometimes called "The Tennysonian" is employed in Tennyson's In Memoriam: 

But who shall so forecast the years 

And find in loss a gain to match? 

Or reach a hand through time to catch 

The far-off interest of tears? Rhyme schemes are almost infinite in variety. Read 

Tennyson's "Claribel" and Browning's "Meeting at Night" for systems more intricate than those illustrated above. 

The sonnet. For the discussion and illustration of special stanza and poem schemes such as the roundel, the triolet, the ballade, the student is referred to textbooks on versification. Mention must be made here, however, of the sonnet, a form which, originating and developed in Italy, has been adapted to use in English verse by eminent poets, and is today a stimulating mode, a challenge to the most painstaking and ingenious craftsmanship.

The sonnet must treat one idea only, and must be complete in fourteen lines. The Italian model divides the

whole into an octave, setting forth the idea itself, or the symbol; and the sestet, setting forth the application of the idea, or the interpretation of the symbol; its conventional rhyme scheme is abba ab bacdecde. The Shakespearean, or English, scheme shows no division into eightand-six. It ryhmes a ba b c dc defefgg, the couplet at the end serving to impart the lift of terminal climax. 

ITALIAN MODEL 

When I consider how my light is spent Ere half my days in this dark world and wide, And that one talent which is death to hide 

Lodged with me useless, though my soul more bent 

To serve therewith my Maker, and present

My true account, lest he returning chide; "Doth God exact day-labor, light denied?" 

I fondly ask. But Patience to prevent 

That murmur, soon replies, "God doth not need 

Either man's work or his own gifts. Who best

Bear his mild yoke, they serve him best. His state 

Is kingly thousands at his bidding speed, And post o'er land and ocean without rest; 

They also serve who only stand and wait."

JOHN MILTON: "On His Blindness." SHAKESPEAREAN MODEL 

Since brass, nor stone, nor earth, nor boundless sea,

But sad mortality o'ersways their power, How with this rage shall beauty hold a plea, Whose action is no stronger than a flower? 

Oh! how shall summer's honey-breath hold out 

Against the wreckful siege of battering days,

When rocks impregnable are not too stout 

Nor gates of steel so strong, but Time decays? 

Oh, fearful meditation! where, alack, Shall Time's best jewel from Time's chest lie hid? 

Or what strong hand can hold his swift foot back? 

Or who his spoil of beauty can forbid? 

Oh, none! unless this miracle have might, That in black ink my love may still shine bright. 

-SHAKESPEARE.

College Rhetoric Certain Poetical Embellishments "POETICISMS": WORDS; PHRASES; INVERSIONS 

Words of archaic form, and those of exclusively poetical association (burthen, ere, amid), are not much used by the best contemporary poets, the present fashion being to make the language of verse imitate the actual speech of living men and women. Poetical contractions (o'er, ne'er, 'neath) are frowned upon. O'er becomes a handy equivalent for over (1) when the writer needs a one-syllable word to fit his meter, or (2) when he needs a rhyme for more rather than for rover; but an acute conscience will not permit him to bend the language to serve such trivial convenience. On sleep for asleep and summertide for summer time are outworn, and now seem affected. On pleasure bent for bent upon or seeking pleasure is hardly permissible. Such literary distortion as was formerly seen in metrical versions of the Psalms is not now considered even passable poetry: 

The Lord's my shepherd, I'll not want;

He makes me down to lie; 

In pastures green he leadeth me,

The quiet waters by. Alliteration

The close juxtaposition of words beginning with the same sound is striking and, when used in moderation, effective. "Weak and weary," "quaint and curious," "sounding sea" are phrases with which Poe worked his charms. Perhaps the repetition may appear extreme in the following: 

Poor shattered shapes of ships that once stood out, Full-freighted to the far horizon's sweep. 

Alliteration is not now (as it was in the period of Old English poetry) made a basis of metrical sequence and organization. Its employment should seem casual rather than intended.

Onomatopoeia 

Some words imitate actual sounds of nature: zip, whiz, roar, clang. The addition of "frequentive" particles containing the sounds of the liquids 1 and r, suggests natural sounds repeated: tinkle, ripple, rumble, flutter, spatter, mutter. The use of such words imparts something of the vividness of actual sensation to language: 

The flying hinges roar—

I heard the ripple washing in the reeds, And the wild water lapping on the crag.

Imitative effects are produced partly by alliteration in the following:

Thus over all that shore, Save for some whisper of the seething seas, A dead hush fell A wind arose and rushed upon the South, And shook the songs, the whispers, and the shrieks

Of the wild woods together. 

And overhead

The broad ambrosial aisles of lofty lime Made noise with bees and breeze from end to end. 

Where's danger, With that thin flitting instantaneous steel 'Gainst the blind bull-front of a brute-force world?

Suggestions for Young Poets 

Theme. Something just the least bit out of the ordinary-an odd fancy; something distinctive in a character or mood; a look of pain that you surprise in the face of a friend; some trick or habit of thought; some odd appearance of a landscape, translated into terms of human experience; a child's pathetic mistake, a mother's dream, a girl's hope, a dog's devotion, a lizard's blinking indifference, a cow's

College Rhetoric friendly tolerance. Get an old idea from a new angle: a new metaphor, a compelling parallel. 

Tune. Get a rhythm running "in your head." Set your idea to a distinct meter, with flowing sequence and distinct rhyme. Get your climax first: write the final stanza, or know just what it is going to be—and then build up to it. 

Hold yourself responsible for climax-no climax, no poem!

Your piece must arrive somewhere. 

Pattern. Scheme completely and write one stanza. 

Then make every other stanza conform to the first. Compare all lines of the same number: all second lines, for instance, must be alike—as to length, stresses, and internal rhyme. 

Clarity. Never leave your reader guessing what you mean. It takes effort to be clear. "There are fifty ways of saying anything"; try all fifty, if you must, but do not give up. 

Language. Avoid hackneyed formulas and cliché rhymes. Shun "poeticisms," but achieve beauty. Anybody can write empty, jingling doggerel: make every line worth reading, make every stanza a jewel, if you can. 

Attitude. Never apologize for writing verse. apologize who do not write verse. ful. There is too little loveliness, world-not too much.

Let those 

Beauty is never shamesentiment, glory in the Exercise 

Write an original poem of twelve, fourteen, sixteen, or twenty lines, on a theme and under a title of your own selection. Mark the scansion.
